abstract_pt: 'A imagem médica desempenha um papel crucial na deteção, diagnóstico
  e tratamento de doenças oncológicas. Os avanços nas tecnologias de imagiologia têm
  evoluído significamente, optimizando o planeamento do tratamento, e reduzindo os
  custos associados aos cuidados de saúde. No entanto, a imagem médica continua a
  ser suscetível a diferentes tipos de ruído, incluindo artefactos de movimento, interferência
  de implantes metálicos, entre outros. Estes artefactos podem esconder detalhes anatómicos,
  dificultando as avaliações clínicas e exigindo o desenvolvimento de novas estratégias
  para mitigar imperfeições na imagem, evitando assim a necessidade de aquisição de
  novas imagens.Uma solução promissora para estes desafios é a aplicação de técnicas
  de imputação, frequentemente denominadas \textit{inpainting} no contexto de imagens,
  que têm como objetivo restaurar imagens degradadas ao remover ou corrigir artefactos
  existentes. No entanto, em modalidades de imagem médica bidimensionais (2D) e tridimensionais
  (3D) existem outros desafios que se materializam na escassez de métodos de imputação
  baseados em aprendizagem automática adaptados à complexa natureza da imagem médica
  e a falta de avaliações sistemáticas que priorizam a relevância clínica.Esta tese
  aborda estes desafios ao explorar metodologias de imputação para melhorar a qualidade,
  interoperabilidade, adaptabilidade e confiabilidade da imagem médica, focando-se
  em dois domínios críticos: imagens 2D e 3D.No domínio 2D, atendendo à modalidade
  principal de diagnóstico do cancro da mama em mulheres -- a mamografia, esta tese
  explora novas técnicas de imputação capazes de reconstruir as imagens atendendo
  a diferentes tipos de informação -- anatómica e de textura. Além disso, investiga
  \textit{métodos de image-to-image translation} como uma abordagem inovadora reduzindo
  a necessidade de aquisição de novas imagens diminuindo assim a exposição do doente,
  assim, a exposição do doente à radiação e demais consequências para o seu estado
  físico e psicológico.No domínio 3D, esta tese enfatiza aplicações de tomografia
  computadorizada no diagnóstico de cancro da mama e pulmão. É explorado o papel das
  modalidades de imagem na deteção de metástases ósseas no cancro da mama e investigadas
  técnicas de imputação para melhorar a qualidade da imagem no cancro do pulmão. Utilizando
  metodologias do estado de arte e partindo da experiência adquirida ao longo da modalidade
  2D, foram estudadas técnicas de imputação de imagens 3D para reconstrução de radiografias
  e de tomografia computadorizada de corpo inteiro .O trabalho desenvolvido durante
  esta tese demonstra que metodologias avançadas de imputação, especialmente aquelas
  que utilizam estruturas de aprendizagem profunda, podem reduzir significativamente
  as imperfeições na imagem médica. Os resultados obtidos destacam o potencial transformador
  da imputação baseada em pixéis, da \textit{image-to-image translation} e da reconstrução
  3D na minimização de imperfeições nas diferentes modalidades de imagem, aumentando
  a sua relevância clínica e confiabilidade no suporte do diagnóstico.Entre as arquiteturas
  de aprendizagem profunda exploradas, as redes autoencoders convolucionais e as redes
  adversarias generativas mostraram ter o maior potencial devido à sua capacidade
  de capturar simultaneamente características locais e globais da imagem. Ao melhorar
  a qualidade da imagem e reduzir a necessidade de novas aquisições, esta tese contribui
  para avaliações clínicas mais precisas, maior confiança nas recomendações médicas
  e melhores resultados para os doentes.'
abstract_en: 'Medical imaging plays a crucial role in the detection, diagnosis, and
  management of cancer across various oncological pathologies. Advances in imaging
  technologies have greatly enhanced diagnostic precision, optimized treatment planning,
  and reduced associated healthcare costs, ultimately improving patient outcomes.
  However, despite these advancements, medical imaging remains susceptible to some
  artifacts, which often lead to missing data, including motion artifacts, interference
  from metal implants, and noise. These issues can obscure anatomical details, complicating
  clinical evaluations, and require the development of robust strategies to mitigate
  imaging imperfections in order to avoid the need for new image acquisition.A promising
  solution to these challenges is the application of data imputation techniques, often
  referred to as inpainting in the context of images, which aim to restore degraded
  images by removing or correcting existing noisy artifacts. However, in medical imaging
  modalities such as two-dimensional (2D) and three-dimensional (3D) imaging, significant
  challenges persist. These include the limited adaptation of advanced machine learning-based
  imputation methods to the complex nature of medical imaging and the lack of systematic
  evaluations that prioritize clinical relevance.This thesis tackles these challenges
  by advancing imputation methodologies to enhance the quality, interoperability,
  adaptability, reliability, and diagnostic value of medical imaging, focusing on
  two critical domains: 2D and 3D imaging.In the 2D domain, with a particular focus
  on the main diagnosing image for women''s breast cancer, mammography, this thesis
  explores the inherent limitations of current imaging practices and introduces innovative
  pixel-level imputation techniques. Furthermore, it investigates image-to-image translation
  methods as a novel approach to reduce the frequency of repeated imaging sessions,
  thereby lowering patient burden and exposure to ionizing radiation.For 3D imaging,
  this thesis emphasizes computed tomography applications in breast and lung cancer
  diagnosis. It explores the role of imaging modalities in detecting bone metastases
  in breast cancer, investigates slice imputation techniques to overcome challenges
  in lung cancer imaging, and extends these methods to full-body X-ray reconstruction.
  We leverage cutting-edge methodologies, building upon the experience developed to
  address the limited progress in 3D image inpainting.The work conducted throughout
  this thesis demonstrates that advanced imputation methodologies, particularly those
  employing deep learning frameworks, can significantly reduce imperfections in medical
  imaging. The obtained results highlight the transformative potential of pixel-based
  imputation, image-to-image translation, and 3D reconstruction to reduce imperfections
  across the studied imaging modalities, enhancing their clinical relevance and diagnostic
  reliability.Among the deep learning architectures explored, convolutional autoencoder
  networks and generative adversarial networks showed the most promise due to their
  ability to capture local and global image features. By enhancing image quality and
  reducing the need for repeated imaging acquisitions, this thesis contributes to
  more accurate clinical assessments, increased trust in medical recommendations,
  and better patient outcomes.'

# Missing Data Methodologies Applied to Healthcare Images

Joana Cristo dos Santos 
 
 
MISSING DATA METHODOLOGIES APPLIED 
TO HEALTHCARE IMAGES

supervised by Professor Pedro Henriques Abreu and Professor

Informatics Engineering of the Faculty of Sciences and 
Technology of the University of Coimbra

i

Abstract
Medical imaging plays a crucial role in the detection, diagnosis, and management of
cancer across various oncological pathologies. Advances in imaging technologies have
greatly enhanced diagnostic precision, optimized treatment planning, and reduced asso-
ciated healthcare costs, ultimately improving patient outcomes. However, despite these
advancements, medical imaging remains susceptible to some artifacts, which often lead
to missing data, including motion artifacts, interference from metal implants, and noise.
These issues can obscure anatomical details, complicating clinical evaluations, and require
the development of robust strategies to mitigate imaging imperfections in order to avoid
the need for new image acquisition.
A promising solution to these challenges is the application of data imputation tech-
niques, often referred to as inpainting in the context of images, which aim to restore
degraded images by removing or correcting existing noisy artifacts. However, in medical
imaging modalities such as two-dimensional (2D) and three-dimensional (3D) imaging,
significant challenges persist. These include the limited adaptation of advanced machine
learning-based imputation methods to the complex nature of medical imaging and the
lack of systematic evaluations that prioritize clinical relevance.
This thesis tackles these challenges by advancing imputation methodologies to enhance
the quality, interoperability, adaptability, reliability, and diagnostic value of medical
imaging, focusing on two critical domains: 2D and 3D imaging.
In the 2D domain, with a particular focus on the main diagnosing image for women’s
breast cancer, mammography, this thesis explores the inherent limitations of current imag-
ing practices and introduces innovative pixel-level imputation techniques. Furthermore,
it investigates image-to-image translation methods as a novel approach to reduce the
frequency of repeated imaging sessions, thereby lowering patient burden and exposure to
ionizing radiation.
For 3D imaging, this thesis emphasizes computed tomography applications in breast
and lung cancer diagnosis. It explores the role of imaging modalities in detecting bone
metastases in breast cancer, investigates slice imputation techniques to overcome challenges
in lung cancer imaging, and extends these methods to full-body X-ray reconstruction. We
leverage cutting-edge methodologies, building upon the experience developed to address
the limited progress in 3D image inpainting.
iii

The work conducted throughout this thesis demonstrates that advanced imputation
methodologies, particularly those employing deep learning frameworks, can significantly
reduce imperfections in medical imaging. The obtained results highlight the transformative
potential of pixel-based imputation, image-to-image translation, and 3D reconstruction
to reduce imperfections across the studied imaging modalities, enhancing their clinical
relevance and diagnostic reliability. Among the deep learning architectures explored,
convolutional autoencoder networks and generative adversarial networks showed the most
promise due to their ability to capture local and global image features. By enhancing image
quality and reducing the need for repeated imaging acquisitions, this thesis contributes
to more accurate clinical assessments, increased trust in medical recommendations, and
better patient outcomes.
iv

Part I
Introduction
1

Chapter 1
Contextualization
Medical imaging has become an essential tool for diagnosis, monitoring, and treatment
planning. However, it is not exempt from challenges, as issues can arise due to factors such
as patient motion, equipment limitations, safety protocols, and time constraints during
scanning [1]. These factors often lead to missing data, a common challenge in real-world
datasets where ideal conditions for data collection are rarely achievable [ 2]. Missing data
in medical imaging refers to the absence of information within an image or the complete
loss of an image, which can compromise its reliability and clinical utility. This issue is
particularly concerning as it may obscure critical anatomical details, potentially leading
to misdiagnoses or treatment delays.
Medical imaging data presents high complexity due to its high dimensionality, intricate
spatial relationships, and detailed anatomical structures. Addressing missing data in such
images requires specialized techniques tailored to the spatial, textural, and color properties
of visual data. Among these, imputation methods — commonly referred to as inpainting in
imaging contexts — aim to reconstruct missing regions by leveraging contextual information
from surrounding pixel patterns and textures [ 3]. Effective imputation techniques are
crucial for applications where image quality directly impacts performance, including object
detection, segmentation, and classification [4].
Given the clinical significance of accurate image imputation, there is a pressing need for
advanced imputation methods capable of restoring missing information with anatomical
precision and clinical accuracy.
2

Chapter 1. Contextualization
1.1 Motivation
The increasing integration of machine learning into healthcare environments has
revolutionized clinical diagnostics, particularly in medical imaging, by enhancing detection,
classification, and predictive capabilities [ 5]. Despite these advancements, missing data
remains a persistent and critical challenge. Missing data in medical imaging not only
impacts the performance of machine learning applications, such as segmentation and
classification, but also risks compromising clinical decision-making and patient care.
Furthermore, repeated imaging is often necessary to compensate for missing or unusable
data, leading to increased healthcare costs and unnecessary patient radiation exposure.
Medical imaging datasets present unique challenges for imputation due to their high
dimensionality and the clinical importance of their features. Missing data can occur at
the pixel level, where specific regions of an image are corrupted or absent, or at the
image level, where entire slices or volumes are unavailable [ 6]. Imputation in medical
imaging serves both technical and clinical purposes. On the technical side, it enhances the
completeness and quality of the data, enabling machine learning models to perform more
effectively. On the clinical side, imputation ensures that reconstructed data is reliable
and has diagnostic value, fostering confidence among clinicians. Inaccurate imputation,
however, risks introducing artifacts or errors that could mislead clinicians.
Addressing these challenges requires advanced imputation techniques capable of re-
constructing missing information while preserving fine-grained textural and anatomical
details essential for accurate diagnosis. Furthermore, medical images are often affected
by artifacts such as motion blur, noise, and metal implants, which not only degrade
image quality but can also directly lead to missing data by obscuring or distorting critical
anatomical regions, rendering them uninterpretable. Additionally, each modality (X-ray,
ultrasound (US), computer tomography (CT), magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), among
others) has unique properties, clinical applications, and susceptibility to specific artifacts,
requiring tailored imputation strategies [4].
Two frequent pathologies, breast cancer (BC) and lung cancer (LC), impose a significant
global burden [7], and their early detection and diagnosis heavily depend on high-quality
medical imaging. Specifically, mammography is the gold standard for BC detection,
while CT plays a crucial role in LC screening. In both cases, missing or corrupted data
can impair diagnostic accuracy, underscoring the importance of precise reconstruction
techniques.
3

Chapter 1. Contextualization
Mammography relies on detecting subtle morphological changes that indicate early
malignancy. However, image degradation due to motion and positioning artifacts is a
leading cause of technical repeats [ 8]. Technical repeats occur when images are deemed
uninterpretable by radiologists, requiring repeat imaging [ 8]. While clinical guidelines
recommend an acceptable technical repeat rate of 3–5% [ 9, 10], studies indicate that this
rate can reach as high as 12% [ 11]. Additionally, artifacts alone contribute to a 6% retake
rate [12]. Considering the estimated 319,750 cases of BC in the United States in 2025 [ 7]
and assuming each patient undergoes a single mammogram, at least 9,000 mammograms
will require retakes solely for the diagnosed population. This issue is even more pronounced
at a global scale, as mammography is widely used for BC screening, compounding the
burden of technical recalls. Beyond inflating healthcare costs, these repeat scans expose
patients to unnecessary radiation, further increasing the risks associated with diagnostic
imaging.
In contrast, CT imaging requires volumetric imputation to restore anatomical structures
across slices, ensuring consistency and accuracy in 3D reconstructions. With an estimated
226,650 cases of LC in the United States in 2025 [ 7], CT plays a crucial role in its
early detection and staging. Although CT rejection rates (1–3%) [ 13, 14] are lower
than those observed in mammography, the modality is still susceptible to artifacts,
particularly those caused by patient motion and metal implants. Despite advancements
in standardized protocols and positioning techniques, artifacts continue to compromise
image quality, potentially affecting segmentation, classification, and diagnosis. To address
these limitations, robust imputation techniques are essential to mitigate their impact and
ensure accurate and reliable diagnoses.
Addressing the issue of missing data in medical imaging is therefore not merely a
technical requirement but a crucial step toward improving patient outcomes. However,
key challenges such as imaging modality differences, textural and structural complexities,
and disease-specific diagnostic requirements necessitate tailored imputation approaches.
Advancing imputation techniques to account for these factors will ensure that reconstructed
data is both diagnostically valuable and clinically reliable, ultimately enhancing the quality
of patient care.
This thesis explores recent advancements in machine learning, especially deep learning,
to uncover intricate patterns in data, handling distinct modalities and offering improved
reconstruction quality. While state-of-the-art techniques in image imputation have demon-
strated remarkable progress [15], these methods remain underexplored in the domain of
medical imaging imputation. The inherent complexities of medical imaging—such as high
4

Chapter 1. Contextualization
dimensionality, clinical relevance, and modality-specific challenges—necessitate specialized
approaches tailored to this domain. Therefore, there is a critical need to systematically
evaluate existing methodologies, identify their limitations, and propose novel solutions
that address the unique demands of medical imaging.
1.2 Research Goal
The lack of robust methodologies capable of performing accurate and artifact-free
imputation in medical imaging remains a critical gap in the field. Current techniques
struggle to ensure that reconstructed images maintain both anatomical integrity and diag-
nostic reliability. Artifacts introduced during imputation can mislead clinical assessments,
and existing algorithms often fail to generalize across different imaging modalities. Most
state-of-the-art approaches are either optimized for other images or fail to address the
specific challenges of medical imaging data.
Having this in mind, the goal of this thesis is to develop novel methodologies tailored
to different oncological pathologies, leveraging their respective gold-standard imaging
techniques. To accomplish this, the following tasks will be undertaken:
• Reviewing the state-of-the-art imputation methodologies applicable to mammography
and other medical imaging modalities, identifying their strengths, limitations, and
applicability;
• Investigating and evaluating the potential applications of imputation techniques in
mammography to enhance image quality and improve BC diagnosis;
• Expanding the scope of imputation techniques to positron emission tomogra-
phy/computer tomography (PET/CT) imaging, exploring their role in improving
data completeness and diagnostic accuracy in 3D medical imaging.
1.3 Research Contributions
Throughout the course of this thesis, several research contributions have arised, re-
sulting in 4 publications in international conferences and 3 publications in international
journals.

1.4 Outline
This thesis is structured into four main parts, each addressing a critical aspect of the
research and its contributions. Part I introduces the motivation and objectives of the
thesis, setting the stage for the studies that follow. Parts II and III form the core of the
thesis, encompassing its primary contributions to the field of medical imaging.
In Part II, the focus is on 2D medical imaging, with a particular emphasis on mam-
mography:
• Chapter 2 presents a comprehensive literature review of traditional and machine
learning-based techniques for improving mammography imaging quality, with an
emphasis on contrast enhancement and noise reduction methods;
• Chapter 3 presents two approaches aimed at enhancing mammography imaging
quality through machine learning-based imputation techniques;
• Chapter 4 extends beyond pixel-based imputation to explore additional techniques,
including image-to-image translation and interoperability, to further enhance the
field of mammography imaging.
In Part III, the work transitions to 3D medical imaging, focusing on CT and its related
methodologies:
• Chapter 5 presents a comprehensive review on deep image inpainting in medical
imaging, analyzing its evolution, impact, and existing limitations;
7

Chapter 1. Contextualization
• Chapter 6 investigates the application of positron emission tomography/computer to-
mography (PET/CT) analysis in cancer diagnostics and treatment planning, focusing
on BC and LC. We first examine the role of PET/CT in detecting bone metastases
in BC patients, comparing its diagnostic efficacy to that of bone scintigraphy. Then
focus on investigating the application of imputation techniques in LC CT imaging;
• Chapter 7 delves into advanced imputation techniques for 3D medical imaging. This
chapter focuses on machine learning for reconstructing full 3D CT volumes from
X-rays. Two innovative methodologies are explored: neural radiance fields (NeRF)
for generating detailed 3D representations, and convolutional neural network (CNN)
for reconstructing high-resolution CT volumes from X-ray acquisitions.
Lastly, Part IV finalizes the thesis, summarising its main conclusions and providing
a cohesive summary of its impact on the field of medical imaging. It discusses the
implications for clinical practice, outlines some open challenges and limitations, and
proposes directions for future research.
8

Part II
Handling Missing Data In 2D Images -
Mammography Applications
9

Chapter 2
Enhancing Mammography: A
Comprehensive Review of Computer
Methods for Improving Image Quality
Mammography remains the gold standard for breast cancer (BC) detection and
diagnosis. However, challenges related to image quality can lead to misdiagnosis, increased
radiation exposure, and higher healthcare costs. Enhancing image quality is therefore
essential for improving diagnostic accuracy and ensuring patient safety. This chapter
provides a comprehensive review of both traditional and machine learning (ML)-based
techniques designed to enhance mammography image quality and improve diagnostic
precision.
2.1 Introduction
BC stands as the most commonly diagnosed cancer and is the leading cause of
cancer-related fatalities among women on a global scale [ 16]. According to the American
Cancer Society, BC accounts for 32% of all newly reported cancer cases, with a projected
319,750 new cases and 42,680 new fatalities anticipated in the female population of the
United States in 2025 [ 7]. In recent years, the mortality rate of BC has exhibited a
consistent decline, attributable to the effectiveness of high-quality prevention strategies
and treatments. Nonetheless, early BC detection remains the most effective means of
further reducing the mortality rate.
Despite the employment of various imaging modalities such as ultrasound (US), mag-
netic resonance imaging (MRI), and positron emission tomography/computer tomography
10

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
(PET/CT) for BC detection and diagnosis, mammography maintains its status as the
gold standard [17]. This imaging technique is predominantly adopted for BC diagnosis
due to its exceptional efficiency in screening for the disease [18].
A mammography image (MI) represents a low-dose X-ray of breast tissue, offered
in two forms: the traditional 2D mammogram or the reconstructed 3D mammogram,
commonly known as breast tomosynthesis. MIs play a crucial role in assessing breast
tissue composition and detecting significant abnormalities associated with BC.
The quality of a MI relies on the visibility of breast tissue composition. However,
several factors challenge this visibility, such as the characteristics of the image acquisition
hardware, the technician’s skill, patient cooperation, and intrinsic variability in breast
tissue composition (different responses to X-ray attenuation and tissue characteristics such
as overlaid tissues and areas of denser fibroglandular tissue) [ 19, 20]. Consequently, these
characteristics contribute to the potential for misdiagnosis, with experienced radiologists
misinterpreting 10 to 30% of tumors during mammography screenings [21].
To improve mammogram interpretation and consequently, the accuracy of medical
diagnosis, Computer-aided Diagnosis (CAD) systems have been developed to elevate the
quality of MIs. These techniques help interpret MIs, reducing misdiagnosis, and avoiding
the re-acquisition of MIs in case of poor image quality. This not only minimizes patient
radiation but also cuts down on hospital costs [ 22]. Given the diversity of techniques,
it is crucial to identify the best approaches for specific applications. Prior reviews have
focused on comparing techniques [ 23, 24, 25, 26], with some overlooking the contribution
of ML-based methodologies.
In this work, MIs quality improvement algorithms are addressed by answering the
following questions:
1. What are the primary challenges associated with processing 2D and 3D MIs?
2. How can improvements in image quality be assessed?
3. What are the best techniques to improve the image quality of MIs?
To address these questions, a comprehensive research work was undertaken, encom-
passing 115 studies focusing on the enhancement of image quality, contrast, and D of
MIs. The identification of relevant research for this extensive literature work involved
a thorough search across databases, including Web of Science, Scopus, PubMed, IEEE
Xplore, and Springer. Articles spanning the period between years 2015 to 2024 were
selected using a combination of keywords such as ”mammography,” ”breast tomosynthesis,”
11

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
”enhancement,” ”denoising,” and ”contrast enhancement.” The initial search yielded a com-
pilation of 203 articles, from which 89 were excluded due to a lack of novelty. Exclusion
criteria included studies that replicated previously established methodologies without
significant modifications or improvements, follow-up studies by the same authors that
did not introduce substantial advancements, and applications of the same technique to
different datasets without demonstrating improvements in performance. The subsequent
analysis encompassed a comprehensive evaluation of various techniques, acknowledging
their limitations, offering recommendations, and addressing open challenges. The analysis
concluded that ML techniques stand out due to their versatility in enabling effective
contrast enhancement (C) and denoising (D) without compromising overall image quality.
The remainder of the chapter is organized as follows: Section 2.2 offers an overview
of essential background knowledge encompassing breast tissue description, enhancement
techniques, image quality assessment methodologies, and MI datasets. Section 2.3 delves
into a comprehensive literature review of quality improvement techniques employed in
mammography. Section 2.4 explores recommendations and presents an overview of the
key open challenges. Finally, Section 2.5 summarizes the conclusions drawn from this
literature review.
2.2 Background Knowledge
In this section, an overview of the background knowledge on BC diagnosis and imaging
is conducted, encompassing further description on breast tissue, detailed descriptions of
enhancement techniques, image quality assessment, and mammography image datasets.
2.2.1 Breast Tissue Composition
The breast, a symmetrical mammary gland, is predominantly composed of fibroglandu-
lar and adipose tissue. Fibroglandular tissue comprises fibrous tissue providing structural
support and glandular tissue and adipose tissue fills the spaces between fibroglandular
tissue, determining breast size and shape. The proportions of fibroglandular and adipose
tissue vary according to individual biological characteristics.
Mammography imaging evaluates breast density by reflecting its composition. Fibrog-
landular tissue attenuates X-rays more than adipose tissue. Therefore, increased breast
density occurs when fibroglandular tissue predominates. In an MI, fibroglandular tissue
appears bright, while adipose tissue, considered the background, appears darker.
12

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
Apart from normal breast composition, MIs identify four tissue anomalies (masses,
calcifications, architectural distortions, and asymmetries), indicating changes in normal
tissue and potential features of BC. Due to the their density, anomalies are represented
as bright areas and consequently, a radiologist primarily concentrates on these region
of interest (ROI) to interpret an MI [ 27]. However, anomaly visibility is significantly
impacted by breast tissue density, with denser tissue leading to lower anomaly visibility
due to tissue overlap. The four main anomalies have distinct characteristics:
• Masses exhibit various shapes and margins. Differentiating benign from malignant
masses relies on boundary characteristics: benign masses generally possess smooth,
round, and well-circumscribed boundaries, as opposed to malignant tumors, which
usually have spiculated, rough, and blurry boundaries [28];
• Calcifications, small calcified spots, appear as white dots in MIs. Analyzing the mor-
phology, size, number and distribution of breast calcifications allows the classification
into benign or malignant [29];
• Architectural distortion describes a distortion in the normal breast tissue with no
definite mass visible. In most cases, architectural distortion are a suspicious finding
and require further study to confirm its malignancy [30];
• Asymmetries represent a unilateral fibroglandular-density finding not resembling
a mass. They are considered non specific and can represent benign or malignant
tissue [31].
Considering the diversity of breast anomalies and breast densities, enhancement
techniques must be suitable for all types of breast tissue without causing loss of information.
2.2.2 Overview of Enhancement Techniques
Mammography enhancement algorithms primarily focus on C and D to improve image
quality. Contrast or edge enhancement techniques aim to improve image spatial resolution
and the detectability of anomalies in the image. Denoising techniques, on the other
hand, reconstruct images corrupted by noise [ 32] while preserving essential features. C
techniques help the radiologist differentiate types of masses and identify calcifications
hidden in denser tissue. D techniques eliminate the noise that reduces the accuracy of
the detection of masses and can lead to the incorrect identification of calcifications. An
optimal balance between C and D is crucial, as excessive contrast may lead to increased
13

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
noise, and overusing D techniques may result in image blurring and the loss of essential
details [33, 34].
Image enhancement techniques are categorized into spatial domain and transform
domain algorithms [ 35]. Spatial domain techniques manipulate pixel values directly,
while transform domain algorithms decompose images into sub-band coefficients, enabling
multi-scale analysis.
The methodologies considered in this review work fall into the categories: HE, filtering,
UM, fuzzy-based techniques, transform-based techniques, and ML [ 26]. These techniques
are applied in either the spatial or transform domain, depending on their underlying
approach. In the following subsections, the different categories of enhancement techniques
are described.
2.2.2.1 Histogram Equalization
HE techniques aim to improve contrast by redistributing image grey levels to obtain a
uniform histogram [36]. Global HE is the basic form of HE and is a popular technique
due to its simplicity, speed, and ease of use. However, due to the lack of control over the
level of enhancement, it usually results in over-enhanced images with poor contrast, noisy
appearance, and in some cases, loss of information [ 37]. To improve the control over the
level of enhancement, some variations of technique have been explored, such as adaptive
HE, which applies this concept of local window processing, and contrast limited adaptive
HE that applies a clip level to limit the magnitude of C for each pixel.
2.2.2.2 Filtering
Filters are the main technique for D medical imaging, especially for X-ray imaging
[38] and can be implemented in both the spatial and transform domains. Linear filters,
commonly used for image smoothing, result from linear combinations of neighboring pixels.
While these filters are favored for their simplicity, they tend to introduce a blurring effect
and inadequately filter noise. To address these limitations, non-linear filters have been
developed. Non-linear filters provide better filtering by suppressing the effect of noises
and preserving the edges. However, a drawback of non-linear filters is their requirement
for a large number of coefficients, which leads to an increase in computational complexity.
14

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
2.2.2.3 Unsharp Masking
UM techniques aim to enhance local details in images. Traditional UM substracts
a high-pass filtered image from the original image and then adding the result back [ 39].
However, excessive sharpening can lead to the introduction of halo artifacts due to noise
amplification and over-enhancement of sharp areas. To address these limitations, various
adaptations of UM have been developed, including Linear UM, Quadratic UM, and
Adaptive UM. These adaptations aim to mitigate halo artifacts and improve performance.
Additionally, Nonlinear UM methods have been proposed to overcome the shortcomings
of linear UM. Nevertheless, tuning the parameters of nonlinear combinations can be time-
consuming and may result in undesired over-shoots, particularly for different categories of
breast tissues [26].
2.2.2.4 Fuzzy-based Enhancement Techniques
Fuzzy-based techniques are knowledge-based approaches that apply human-like reason-
ing to improve overall image quality. Since fuzzy characteristics are produced during the
acquisition or transmission of mammograms [ 40], MIs are considered fuzzy and benefit
from using fuzzy reasoning. The process of fuzzy techniques is mainly composed of three
stages: fuzzification, an operation on membership values, and defuzzification [ 41]. The
main difference to other image processing methodologies is that the input image data
will be processed in the membership plane where fuzzy logic, fuzzy set theory and fuzzy
measure theory can be used to modify the membership values [41].
Fuzzy logic can be categorized into the type-I fuzzy set (FS), type-II FS and intu-
itionistic fuzzy set (IFS) according to how the membership functions are represented.
Type-I FSs are the simplest fuzzy set techniques since they represent the fuzzy set in a
two-dimension model. Although type-I FSs are simpler to study, these techniques produce
some artifacts with fewer gray levels and low contrast values. Type-II FSs improve the
enhancement results in comparison to type-I FSs, due to their use of a third dimension in
the representation of the fuzzy set [42]. IFS theory considers two uncertainties membership
and non-membership degree, whereas type-I and type-II FS theory consider only fuzziness
in the FS with one membership function [ 43]. Overall, IFSs achieve good quality in terms
of image enhancement, but they fail to produce better-enhanced images with fine details.
15

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
2.2.2.5 Transform-based Enhancement Techniques
Transform-based enhancement techniques improve image quality by modifying pixel
values through mathematical transformations applied in two primary domains: the spatial
domain and the transform domain.
In the spatial domain, enhancement is achieved by directly altering pixel intensities or
gray-level distributions using functions such as logarithmic transformations, power-law
adjustments, and piecewise linear mappings. These transformations modify the brightness
and contrast of an image by redistributing pixel values, thereby enhancing important
visual details [44].
In contrast, the transform domain operates on the frequency components of an image
rather than individual pixel values. By decomposing images into sub-band coefficients,
multi-scale analysis enables targeted enhancement through techniques like thresholding or
filtering [26]. The most widely used multi-resolution transforms include Wavelet, Discrete
Cosine, Curvelet, Contourlet, Shearlet, and Laplacian Pyramid transforms. These methods
are particularly effective for emphasizing fine details and improving the visibility of ROIs
while suppressing background noise.
2.2.2.6 Machine Learning Techniques for Denoising and Contrast Enhancement
ML techniques have shown in recent years a high impact in mammography imaging
in tasks such as segmentation and classification [ 45]. Although these techniques are not
yet popular in mammography C and D tasks, they provide outstanding performance
compared to other techniques [ 45]. The most popular techniques for image enhancement
are convolutional neural network (CNN) and denoising autoencoder (DAE).
CNN is a technique that extracts feature maps directly from the data through a series
of convolution filters. The architecture of a CNN is composed of an input and output layer
with multiple hidden layers. The hidden layers are composed of a sequence of convolutional
layers followed by the rectified linear unit activation function and a pooling layer that may
be repeated and attached to the end of the network of fully connected layers. The fully
connected layer takes the output data from the sequence of convolutions and transforms
it into the desired output.
An autoencoder is a generative model composed of two connected networks: an encoder
and a decoder. The encoder network converts the input of the network into a smaller
denser representation of the data and the decoder network uses this representation to
convert it back to the original input. The representation of the data aims to preserve the
16

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
relevant information in the limited encoding, discarding what is not considered essential.
The DAE is a variation of the standard autoencoder that introduces random corruption
to the input data, compelling the model to learn how to reconstruct missing or distorted
values [46].
2.2.3 Image Quality Assessment
Image quality assessment of an image after enhancement is difficult to evaluate. The
comparison between images (original versus enhanced) is performed based on objective
and subjective analysis that evaluates factors such as contrast, noise, brightness, and
enhancement [47]. However, there is no consensus on which is the best methodology or
measure to be used in this context [47].
The objective analysis is the quantitative evaluation of the comparison between the
original and the enhanced image. This evaluation is performed based on performance
metric values such as mean squared error (MSE), peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR),
structural similarity index measure (SSIM), contrast improvement index (CII), absolute
mean brightness error (AMBE), and enhancement measure (EME).
The MSE metric quantifies the average squared difference between two images and is
calculated by:
M SE = 1

Variations of this metric include root mean square error (RMSE) and mean absolute error
(MAE).
The PSNR metric assesses the ratio between the maximum possible power of a signal
and the power of corrupting noise and is calculated by:
P SN R = 10 × log10

common variant of this metric.

Although the objective analysis is an essential step, subjective analysis methodologies
have become more popular. The new trends of mammographic image enhancement are
focused on the clinical value provided by the improvement of the image quality, rather
than a performance metric value. In this context, the mean opinion score (MOS) [ 50]
is a subjective analysis evaluation that converts the visual assessment performed by
experienced radiologists into a metric value.
2.2.4 Mammography Public Datasets
Several public mammography image datasets are available for research purposes, each
with variations in terms of the number of patients, image types and types of anomalies.
These datasets include:
1. Mini-MIAS Mammographic Database (MIAS) [ 51] - This database is composed
of 161 patients and includes radiologists’ annotation, the characterization of the
background tissue, and the type and severity of the anomaly 1;
2. Digital Database for Screening Mammography (DDSM) [ 52, 53] - The DDSM is
composed of 2,620 patients, including patient (age, breast density rating, subtlety
rating for anomalies, American College of Radiology keyword description of anomalies
and BI-RADS score) and image information 2;
3. Curated Breast Imaging Subset of DDSM (CBIS-DDSM) [ 54] - An updated and
curated version of DDSM, CBIS-DDSM contains 1,644 cases and complementary
information (breast density category, number of anomalies, mass shape, mass margin,
calcification type, calcification distribution, BI-RADS score, the severity of the
anomaly), specifically selected for CAD systems research 3;
4. INbreast [55] - This database includes 115 patient cases, along with information
about the type of anomaly (masses, calcifications, asymmetries, and distortions),
breast density rating, BI-RADS score, and ROI cropped images 4;
5. MedPix [56] - This database is a free open-access online database of medical images
that includes MIs from 560 patients 5;

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
6. Breast Cancer Digital Repository (BCDR) [ 57] - The BCDR is subdivided into
two different repositories: a film mammography repository (BCDR-FM) and a full-
field digital mammography (FFDM) repository (BCDR-DM). This dataset contains
1,734 patients cases with mammography and US images, clinical history, lesion
segmentation, and selected pre-computed image-based descriptors 6;
7. Benign Breast Tumour IEEE database [ 58] - This dataset contains 80 patients
with benign tumors and includes information such as patients’ clinical history and
histopathological features7;
8. VICTRE [59] - This dataset is a replicate of a clinical trial that used human patients.
The dataset contains 2,986 synthetic patients with breast sizes and radiographic
densities representative of a screening population 8;
9. Breast Cancer Screening - Digital Breast Tomosynthesis (BCS-DBT) [ 60] - This
curated dataset contains 22,032 reconstructed digital breast tomosynthesis (DBT)
volumes and includes annotations by 2 experienced radiologists 9.
These datasets differ in terms of the number and the type of MIs (film, FFDM or
DBT), types of projections (craniocaudal (CC) or mediolateral oblique (MLO)), and the
type and severity of anomalies. Table 2.1 presents a comparison of the previous datasets.
Among the datasets examined, CBIS-DDSM and BCS-DBT stand out as the most
comprehensive options for ML research due to their larger sample size and inclusion of
diverse imaging types. However, despite their advantages, these datasets are still limited
in scale when considering the demands of deep learning methodologies, which typically
require massive amounts of labeled data to achieve high-performance models. The lack of
sufficiently large datasets remains a significant barrier to the development and validation
of deep learning-based approaches for BC detection, emphasizing the need for expanded
and more diverse datasets in future research.

2.3 Comparative Review
In this section, we focus on analysing the 115 articles specifically centered on objectives
involving improving quality (IQ), contrast enhancement (C), denoising (D), and brightness
preservation (BP) within the domain of mammography imaging. For a visual understanding
of the distribution of these articles, Figure 2.1 facilitates the comprehension of how these
articles are distributed among the categories (HE, filters, UM, fuzzy-based techniques,
transform-based, ML, and other). HE emerges as the most prominently featured technique,
whereas UM stands out as the least frequently employed method. Furthermore, it is worth
noting that the reviewed works predominantly incorporate a diverse range of techniques
based on filtering, transform-based, and ML methodologies.

Basic HE techniques have undergone adaptations aimed at addressing issues related to
poor contrast, noisy image appearance, and information loss [ 61, 72]. These adaptations,
while enhancing contrast, also introduce increased computational complexity and may
result in a lower quality enhancement of finer details such as calcifications.
23

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
Notably, the fusion of HE techniques with pixel information-based methods, such as
gray-level clustering and Otsu’s thresholding, has proven effective in augmenting the global
contrast of HE algorithms while mitigating computational complexity [ 65, 73, 74]. These
resultant techniques are characterized by their simplicity and computational efficiency,
but they may not adequately address local enhancement requirements.
To overcome the limitations associated with local enhancement, HE techniques have
been combined with complementary techniques, such as filters [ 75, 76], transforms [67, 77,
78], and gray relational analysis [64, 66], which individually contribute to local enhancement
of MIs. These integrated approaches offer the potential for achieving superior global and
local C while preserving image brightness.
Crucially, controlling the degree of enhancement is a vital consideration in the context
of HE-based C. Evolutionary algorithms and fuzzy techniques, given their adaptability to
image characteristics, have demonstrated an ability to achieve improved local and global
enhancements.
Evolutionary algorithms provide a methodology of determining the optimal plateau
limit for the HE methodology based on MI characteristics [ 62, 69, 71, 79, 80, 81]. These
techniques contribute to global C, with a specific focus on mitigating issues like over-
enhancement and brightness degradation. However, it should be noted that the computa-
tional complexity may increase with the application of evolutionary algorithms.
Fuzzy approaches also offer controlled enhancement within HE by virtue of their
adaptability to image characteristics [63, 68, 70, 82, 83, 84]. These techniques apply fuzzy-
clipped inference systems that automatically select the clip limit during the enhancement
process. These methods not only enhance contrast of MIs but also preserve and sharpen
the edges of breast lesions while reducing image noise.
The inclusion of fuzzy approaches is a particularly promising avenue for augmenting
HE-based C; however, it is important to consider the limitations, such as the potential
introduction of artifacts and the inability to consistently produce finely detailed, well-
enhanced images.

The filtering techniques were found to be broadly popular due to their versatility in
addressing both C and D. The most impactful articles related to filtering algorithms are
described in Table 2.3.

Linear filters stand out as straightforward solutions for image smoothing and local
enhancement [86, 88, 96]. These techniques contribute to the improvement of image
quality, enhancing edge and boundary details crucial for distinguishing suspicious masses
in highly dense mammograms without compromising finer details.
On the other hand, non-linear filters are commonly employed for D MIs [ 90, 91, 92, 94,
97, 98, 99, 100] and brightness preservation [ 89, 101]. Despite their higher computational
complexity, these non-linear filters effectively eliminate noise while preserving global C
and brightness.
25

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
Non-linear polynomial filters have also garnered attention for their potential to control
noise and enhance contrast and edges while reducing the characteristic computational
complexity associated with these filters [ 102, 103, 104]. These methods result in noise-
controlled contrast and edge enhancement that alleviates blurring in tumor regions and
background noise suppression.
In addition to the aforementioned filtering techniques, others, such as total variation
[85], convolution [105], fractional differential [87, 106], and combined filters [ 93, 95], have
been developed to enhance the visual quality of MIs.
It is worth noting that while the filtering techniques under review exhibit capabilities in
both C and D, the primary focus leans towards D. Furthermore, they often present exper-
imental setups based on a limited number of images, restricting the scope of experiments
to specific breast tissue and noise types.
2.3.3 Unsharp Masking
UM techniques predominantly find their application in the realm of local enhancement
for MIs. Their limited scope of application has led them to receive less attention in the
context of mammography enhancement compared to other methods. The most impactful
articles related to UM algorithms are described in Table 2.4.

The combination of UM with other techniques such as filters [ 108, 109] and transforms

details while concurrently eliminating noise.
In recent years, non-linear UM techniques have gained popularity for localized en-
hancement in MIs [ 112, 113]. These techniques are adept at addressing the issue of
unwanted overshoots across different categories of breast tissue. Notably, they manage

to enhance less-dense, medium-dense, and high-dense tissue MIs without introducing

Despite significant advancements in UM techniques, the reviewed articles have not
extensively explored these methods across various types and levels of noise. Further
research in this regard could potentially yield valuable insights into the adaptability of
UM techniques to diverse noise conditions and types.

Type-1 fuzzy type algorithms have been developed with the objective of enhancing
image contrast, while meticulously preserving image details and effectively reducing the
amplification of noise [ 115, 116, 119]. These implementations primarily emphasize the
refinement of rule application within the membership plane and the introduction of
innovative techniques for defuzzification.
The majority of the fuzzy-based techniques implemented in these studies revolves
around the application of IFS. This choice is attributed to their ability to surmount the
limitations associated with both type-I and type-II fuzzy sets [ 40, 114, 117, 118, 120, 121].
27

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
These advanced techniques play a pivotal role in elevating both contrast and visual
quality when it comes to anomalies in MIs, including masses and microcalcifications, while
ensuring finer image details are preserved. However, it’s important to note that while
these techniques have demonstrated potential in reducing noise amplification, research in
the domain of D for mammography imaging remains rather limited, with only a single
application explored in the reviewed literature. Further exploration of this aspect could
present promising opportunities for enhancing the versatility of fuzzy-based techniques in
the context of MIs.

[123, 127, 129, 130]. These techniques improve the contrast effectively and preserve the

28

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
The wavelet transform, the most widely implemented technique within transform-
based enhancements, is meticulously explored for C [ 122, 131, 132, 133, 134, 135] and D
[126, 136, 137]. Despite its limitations, including over-smoothing, the wavelet transform
has witnessed a surge in popularity in recent years due to its simplicity and ability to
significantly enhance image quality. These techniques play a pivotal role in improving
overall image quality by improving both local and global contrast, and, in certain instances,
mitigating noise through effective filtration. Moreover, alternative transforms such as
curvelet, contourlet and shearlet transform are also applied for D [ 125, 138, 139] and C
[128, 140, 141, 142].
The adaptability of transform-based techniques is evident, and they offer substantial
potential for enhancing the quality of MIs. However, it’s worth noting that a limitation
in the reviewed literature is the relatively restricted testing of these transform-based
algorithms, typically performed on small sample sizes of images. This limitation, in turn,
poses challenges in assessing the generalizability of the results to broader contexts.

CNN have been the focal point of numerous studies with applications ranging from
D MIs to mitigating blurring artifacts within DBT images, thereby enhancing overall
image quality. Alterations on CNN architectures based on smoothing priors [ 146], deep
CNN [144, 145, 149], vectorization CNN [ 148] and data augmentation [ 150] are proposed
for MIs D. Notable applications on DBT images center around the reduction of blurring
artifacts [155, 162], improvement of image quality [159, 161] and D [156, 157]. Importantly,
these techniques extend beyond D and encompass C to facilitate more discerning visual
interpretations of lesions [154, 158]. CNNs are revered for their adaptability, permitting
their deployment across a broad spectrum of applications encompassing both D and C.
The DAE focus on noise reduction, without compromising the image contrast and
preservation of finer details. These techniques have been applied for D of different levels
and types of noises [ 46, 160] and for the restoration [ 151, 152] of MIs. DAEs exhibit a
remarkable capacity to recover image details even in scenarios characterized by high levels
of noise, surpassing the capabilities of alternative techniques.
Complementary methodologies grounded in ML include singular value decomposition
[143] and convolutional-based networks [ 163], which have also found application in D.
Additionally, generative adversarial network (GAN) have been proposed to synthesize 2D
MIs from DBT images [147, 153].
The distinguishing feature of ML algorithms lies in their competence to tackle D and C.
Their unparalleled ability to recover image details in the presence of high noise levels far
surpasses the capabilities of traditional methodologies. While these techniques undoubtedly
excel in noise filtration, their full potential in contexts characterized by extreme noise
levels remains an avenue of further research. Furthermore, their applicability in the sphere
of image enhancement warrants more comprehensive exploration.
31

for Improving Image Quality

2.4 Discussion
The previously reviewed techniques have demonstrated their effectiveness in enhancing
the visual quality of mammographic images by improving local and global contrast and/or
D. After analysing these techniques, some trends have been identified in terms of main
goals and strategies to addressed them. The summary of the tendencies are illustrated in
Figure 2.2.
Contrast Enhancement emerges as the predominant technique, featured in 55 articles,
followed by D, featured in 33 studies. The prevalence of C studies is closely tied to
the abundance of works employing HE, filtering, and transformation-based approaches.
32

Figure 2.2: Comparative Trends of the Reviewed Works.
Notably, brightness preservation, synthesis of MIs, and the integration of C with D stand
out as less explored areas. Although brightness preservation and combined enhancement-
denoising are relatively underrepresented, the synthesis of MIs from DBT has recently
gained attention as an innovative method for enhancing mammography image quality.
In terms of mammographic image types, 2D MIs dominate in 87% of the works,
primarily due to the extensive availability of open-source datasets for 2D MIs within the
scientific community. Conversely, datasets for 3D MIs or DBT are notably scarce. Despite
DBT providing image quality improvement for denser breasts with the 3D reconstruction,
this improvement comes at the cost of an increased radiation dose for the patient [ 174]
and added blurring with the loss of skin and superficial tissue resolution [ 175]. Therefore,
more work should be devoted to improving DBT image quality. Notably, only 2 works in
the dataset focus on enhancing quality for both 2D and 3D MIs. This dataset selection
should be a primary focus for the near future, as image quality improvement is crucial for
2D and 3D MIs and could lead to more consistent and robust development in the future.
33

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
The assessment of image quality primarily relies on PSNR and SSIM, with metrics
such as MSE, AMBE, CII, and H being employed to a lesser extent. However, for a more
comprehensive evaluation, it is advisable to incorporate all these metrics in experimental
setups to thoroughly gauge the impact of methodologies on diverse aspects of image
quality enhancement. Subjective analysis has emerged as a valuable means of evaluating
visual improvement, involving reader studies with trained clinical observers. Surprisingly,
only a limited number of works, 7 works, incorporate this assessment method.
The selection of image datasets significantly influences algorithm success, with 63%
of works opting for the MIAS dataset. This prevalence is justified by the dataset’s
accessibility and its consistent presence in the literature, although considerations for
dataset diversity are essential for robust algorithm evaluation. Despite the MIAS dataset
being considered the baseline, 31% of works prefer to use private datasets. With the
inclusion of private datasets, specific breast tissue compositions can be studied, and
the effects of the algorithms for different density and anomalies can be evaluated in
detail. However, without the use of common datasets, it is not possible to generalize the
conclusions of these datasets for other algorithms.
The reviewed techniques encompass distinct categories, each with its unique methodol-
ogy and inherent limitations. Table 2.9 offers a comparative overview of these categories
to help pinpoint their technical disparities.
Following our comprehensive review and the aforementioned comparisons, it is challeng-
ing to single out a universally superior approach for enhancing the quality of mammographic
images. Nonetheless, we can provide recommendations for choosing the most suitable
technique depending on the specific objectives.
The chosen methodology should consider the type of mammography under considera-
tion, either traditional 2D MI or DBT. While techniques applied to 2D MI are generally
suitable for DBT, the latter requires algorithms adept at addressing higher noise levels
resulting from reconstruction artifacts and can be applied either before or after recon-
struction.
The chosen methodology should align with the specific objective it aims to accomplish.
For D, filters and ML-based techniques are the most suitable. These techniques can remove
the noise within the image, but C is often not considered. The remaining techniques for D
present limitations on noise filtration, meanwhile the C techniques create blurring effects
or incomplete noise filtration.
For C, HE and transform-based are the most suitable. These techniques gave different
advantages for image C, however, the main limitation is the local enhancement of smaller
