details that causes noise amplification, loss of information, and poor local contrast. The
alterations applied to these techniques to improve local contrast often lead to an increase
in computational complexity and require further study to improve their performance. The
diversity of D techniques is smaller in comparison to C techniques, however, to improve
image quality there is an advantage in combining the two tasks.
For less conventional objectives, such as mammography synthesis and brightness preser-
vation, tailored techniques are essential. In the context of mammography synthesis, ML
based techniques, specially generative adversarial networks, have surfaced as a promising
technique for synthesizing 2D MIs. For brightness preservation, HE and filtering emerge
as the primary application methodologies.
Regardless of the particular strengths of different methodologies according to the type
of image and key objective, ML techniques stand out as capable of delivering the most
comprehensive improvement in mammography image quality. Their versatility enables
35

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
both C and D without compromising image quality. Although ML algorithms often require
extensive datasets, alternative approaches, such as those in that employ small sample
sizes [46] or rely on transfer learning [ 158], can still achieve superior performance, even
with limited image datasets [ 176]. The most promising architecture incorporates a deep D
autoencoder into a stacked CNN developed to improve the visual interpretation of lesions
[152]. This proposed architecture is based on a sparsity D algorithm and achieves a PSNR
value of 32.23 and a SSIM value of 0.91 for the MIAS database.
Despite the advancements that have been made in enhancing MI for the past 10 years,
several challenges warrant further consideration:
• Limitations in Addressing High Noise Ratios: Despite recent progress, improv-
ing visual quality in images with higher noise ratios remains constrained. The
development of new ML techniques offers an opportunity to address this limitation,
particularly in enhancing the image quality of noisy images associated with a high
rate of misdiagnosis;
• Applications of GANs and Synthesis Techniques: The emergence of generative
adversarial networks and related techniques holds promise for tasks like synthesizing
2D MIs from 3D MIs. However, given the relatively recent application of these
techniques, additional studies are essential to optimize their performance;
• Inconsistency in Evaluation Metrics: The evaluation of image quality in mam-
mography enhancement suffers from a lack of standardized metrics, making direct
comparisons between studies challenging. Establishing a more consistent experimen-
tal framework for selecting evaluation metrics would improve research reliability and
enable more meaningful comparisons;
• Challenges in Analyzing Breast Tissue: Analyzing breast tissue in mammographic
images presents a considerable computational challenge owing to the variability in
breast tissue characteristics. Differences in tissue density and tissue overlap can
obscure lesions such as masses and calcifications, demanding techniques capable of
distinguishing denser background tissues from anomalies. While this challenge is
addressed in some of the techniques discussed, these studies are often conducted on
small datasets, limiting their generalization to other breast tissue characteristics.
Furthermore there are no studies that evaluate how these algorithms affect the
diagnosis and how that translates into patient outcome/management;
36

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
• Limitation of Image Datasets: Image datasets selected for experimental setups
frequently feature either a limited number of images or a restricted range of breast
tissue characteristics. This results from the limitations of publicly available datasets,
which typically offer a small number of images that may not be suitable for ML
techniques. Moreover, these datasets lack the representation of all the anomaly
types associated with BC. There is also a notable shortage of public datasets for
DBT, requiring the acquisition of private datasets.
2.5 Conclusion
In this chapter, we conducted a comprehensive literature search with the aim of
establishing the state-of-the-art techniques and recent developments for MI, spanning
the period from 2015 to 2024. The search was meticulously performed across prominent
databases, including Web of Science, Scopus, PubMed, IEEE Xplore, and Springer. This
rigorous search yielded a curated list of 115 articles, which were subsequently subjected
to a thorough analysis.
Following the discussion of related research, it is important to note that definitively
determining the best approach for enhancing mammography remains a challenging task.
Nevertheless, our review offers valuable recommendations to guide the selection of the
most appropriate technique based on specific enhancement objectives. It is paramount to
consider the type of mammography under examination, with DBT requiring algorithms
capable of addressing higher noise levels, often associated with artifacts during the
reconstruction process.
For C, HE emerges as a prime candidate due to its simplicity of implementation
and low computational complexity. In contrast, for D, filtering techniques prove to be
most suitable, offering ease of implementation without necessitating an extensive training
dataset.
Among the spectrum of enhancement techniques explored, ML techniques shine brightly
due to their versatility in simultaneously handling C and D, all while preserving image
quality. Although several ML methodologies showcase their capabilities in enhancing
overall mammography image quality, a notable architecture stands out. This architecture,
founded on the integration of a D autoencoder within a stacked CNN, achieves exemplary
performance, culminating in a PSNR value of 32.23 and an SSIM value of 0.91 for the
MIAS database.
37

Chapter 2. Enhancing Mammography: A Comprehensive Review of Computer Methods
for Improving Image Quality
In conclusion, this literature review presents the landscape of mammography enhance-
ment techniques and highlights the promise of ML in this domain. Our recommendations
offer valuable guidance for tailoring enhancement strategies to meet specific objectives,
making strides toward enhanced mammography image quality and ultimately contributing
to improved clinical diagnostics and patient care. Aditionally, we have highlighted several
open challenges requiring further investigation, such as limitations in addressing high noise
ratios, inconsistencies in evaluation metrics, complexities in analyzing breast tissue, and
constraints related to available open-source image datasets. These areas provide ground
for future research endeavors, fostering the advance in efficacy and comprehensiveness of
image enhancement techniques in mammography.
While substantial progress has been made in mammography image enhancement, a
critical research gap remains in the area of imputation techniques. Unlike other medical
imaging modalities where imputation has been successfully employed to restore missing or
corrupted data in compressive sensing [177], image segmentation [178], image augmentation
[179], and classification [180] applications, mammography has received little to no attention
in this regard. Addressing this issue is particularly important, as imaging artifacts and
acquisition errors can compromise the diagnostic accuracy of mammograms. Developing
imputation methodologies tailored to mammography could significantly improve image
reliability and aid in clinical decision-making.
38

Chapter 3
Imputation Methodologies Applied to
Mammography
As discussed in the previous chapter, pixel imputation strategies have had limited
application in mammography image (MI). However, they have demonstrated promising
results in other imaging modalities. Despite these advancements, existing applications
predominantly focus on healthy tissues [ 181, 182] and involve only a small number
of artifacts [ 183], limiting their applicability to MI. To address this gap, this chapter
introduces two complementary approaches for handling missing pixel data in MI. In
Section 3.1, a novel variational autoencoder (VAE), named VAE-WL, which incorporates
a custom loss function designed to enhance the imputation of missing values is presented.
This extension aims to provide a more robust framework for reconstructing and improving
image quality in MI. In Section 3.2, a comparative analysis of machine learning techniques
is provided, assessing their performance not only in terms of evaluation metrics but
also in morphology preservation and classification performance. By addressing both
methodological advancements and clinical applicability, these approaches offer valuable
insights into the strengths and limitations of different imputation strategies. Ultimately,
this chapter aims to improve diagnostic accuracy and image reliability in MI, contributing
to both the fields of medical imaging and data science.
39

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
3.1 Variation Autoencoders with Weighted Loss for Mam-
mography Image Imputation
As shown in the previous chapter, generative models have shown great potential for
enhancing and synthesizing MI. Researchers have recently explored various approaches to
imputing missing data in images, with a strong focus on generative models. State-of-the-
art methods rely primarily on deep learning algorithms, such as generative adversarial
network (GAN) [184] and VAE [185].
Autoencoders and their denoising variants are able to extract a clean output from a
noisy input. Mattei and Frellsen [ 186] use an approach based on an importance weighted
auto-encoder to perform single or multiple imputations of incomplete images. The resulting
dataset was later used in a classification task and achieved 98.68% of accuracy, an increase
of 0.02% from the complete dataset. Gondara [ 46] applied a denoising autoencoder built
using convolutional layers. This approach achieves good denoising performance for small
sample sizes, which are typical on medical image databases. The increase from 300 to 720
samples only improved the mean SSIM score from 0.89 to 0.90.
GANs have been applied in the field of imaging due to its capacity to reconstruct
an image and to perform efficiently when complete data is unavailable. Shang et al.
[187] developed a novel approach for View Imputation with a GAN. This approach is
able to integrate knowledge from the domain mappings and the view correspondences
to effectively recover a missing view/modality. Yoon et al. [ 184] proposed a method for
imputing missing data named generative adversarial imputation network (GAIN). This
method outperformed several state-of-the-art imputation techniques such as Multiple
Imputation by Chained Equations, MissForest, Matrix Completion, Autoencoders, and
the Expectation-Maximization algorithm, presenting a difference from 0.0062 to 0.0182 in
RMSE values between the GAIN and the best performing imputation technique for each
dataset.
Autoencoders and GANs represent the most advanced algorithms for missing image
data imputation, consistently achieving promising results compared to more traditional
methods. However, despite its success in other applications, the VAE has not yet been
utilized for image imputation and its loss function assigns equal importance to all data
points in their default loss function.
40

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
To this end, this section focuses on proposing a novel variant of the VAE, called
variational autoencoder with weighted loss (VAE-WL), which incorporates a custom
loss function designed to prioritize the reconstruction of missing data. The proposed
VAE is trained and evaluated on three image datasets (MNIST, CBIS-DDSM Mass, and
Calcification) and is compared against two state-of-the-art generative models — a standard
VAE and the GAIN method — using the mean absolute error (MAE) metric.
3.1.1 Weight Loss Motivation and Definition
A VAE is a variant of the Autoencoder family that has generative capabilities. While
the basic Autoencoder simply learns a compressed representation of the input data in a
unsupervised way, the VAE learns the parameters of a probability distribution representing
that data, namely the mean and variance of a Gaussian curve. By sampling from these
learned parameters, the model is able to generate new data with the same characteristics
[188]. Although a VAE can be used in its original form to perform missing data imputation
tasks, its loss function is not the most suitable for this purpose [ 185]. As Equation 3.1
shows, the default VAE loss function contains two terms: the first is the reconstruction
error and the second is a regularizer. Moreover, q(z |X) is the encoder output, p(X |z) is
the decoder output, X is the input data and z is the new sampled data from the learned
distribution.
L(X ) = −Ez Q (z|X )[l og p(X |z)] + K L(q(z|X ||p(z)) (3.1)
The reconstruction error is the basis (and often the only term) of every loss function
used with neural networks. Some of the most frequent used functions here are the Mean
Squared Error or the Binary Cross-Entropy for scenarios with only two possible outcomes.
This term is essential for the decoder to learn how to reconstruct the data. On the other
hand, the regularizer from the second term is the Kullback-Leibler divergence between
the encoder and decoder distributions. This term is needed to ensure that the latent
space is well structured, meaning that similar input data should be represented by similar
representations of the latent space [ 188]. When considering the use of a VAE for missing
data imputation, this loss function poses two problems. First, the reconstruction error
gives the same importance to the available values and to the missing ones. Although
this error should consider all the data to ensure a complete learning of the network,
for imputation purposes the reconstruction of the missing values should have a heavier
weight on this process. Secondly, considering the importance of both terms from the loss
41

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
function in imputation tasks, it is admissible to lose some of the structure from the latent
space to ensure a better reconstruction of the missing values [ 189]. In other words, the
Kullback-Leibler divergence may have a smaller impact on the learning process, which
will lead to better reconstructions and, as consequence, better imputation results.
To address these issues, we propose the VAE-WL, consisting in a VAE with an extension
of the default loss function that is presented in Equation 3.2. In this new function, the
reconstruction error is split between the data containing missing values ( Xmv ) and the
data that is complete (X av ), assigning a heavier weight to the first one through a coefficient
γ > 1. Moreover, the Kullback-Leibler divergence is penalized by using another coefficient
β within the range [0, 1[.
PE (X ) = −Ez Q (z|X )[l og p(X |z)]L(X ) = −(PE (X av ) ∗ γPE (Xmv ) + βK L(q z|X )||p(z)) (3.2)
The choice of this mechanism over other approaches stems from its ability to directly
incorporate missing data patterns into the loss function, ensuring that the model learns to
reconstruct absent pixel values more effectively. Unlike traditional VAEs, which treat all
pixels equally during optimization, the VAE-WL loss function strategically assigns greater
importance to missing data, thereby improving reconstruction fidelity without altering
the model architecture or hyperparameters.
An example of the impact of the proposed changes in the VAE-WL loss function is
presented in Figure 3.1. The first image is an original character from the MNIST dataset
and the second one is the same image with 50% of its pixels missing completely at random.
The last two images represent the imputation with a regular VAE and with the VAE-WL.
Figure 3.1: Example of Image Imputation. From left to right: the 1st image is the original one,
the 2nd image has 50% of its pixels missing completely at random, the 3rd image was imputed
with a regular VAE, and the 4th image was imputed with the VAE-WL.
The results demonstrate a clear improvement in image reconstruction when using the
VAE-WL loss function, which prioritizes missing values during training. By focusing on
these regions, the model is better equipped to infer and restore missing details, leading to
higher-quality imputation compared to a conventional VAE.
42

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
3.1.2 Evaluation of the Proposed Approach
In order to properly evaluate the impact of the proposed approach in an imputation
task, an experiment was conducted to compare the VAE-WL with a regular VAE. The
GAIN [184] method was also considered, being another generative state-of-the-art model
for missing data imputation.
Regarding VAEs, the architecture was determined through extensive experimentation,
ensuring optimal performance. The key components of the model are outlined in Figure
3.2 and can be summarized as follows:
• Encoder:
– Two convolutional layers with 32 and 64 filters, kernel size of 3, ReLU activation
function and stride length of 2 (eliminating the need for max pooling layers);
– Two fully connected layers with 392 and 196 units, ReLU activation function
and mean and variance layers with 32 units each;
• Decoder:
– Two fully connected layers with 392 and 196 units and ReLU activation function;
– Two transposed convolutional layers with 64 and 32 filters, kernel size of
3, ReLU activation function and stride length of 2, and a final transposed
convolutional layer with stride length of 1 and sigmoid activation;
• Training Details:
– Optimization algorithm: Adam;
– Learning rate: 0.001;
– Batch size: 64 images;
– Maximum of 200 epochs;
– L2 regularization applied to each layer to prevent overfitting;
– Dropout layers with a 20% rate.
This structured approach ensures a balanced architecture that enhances the model’s
ability to learn meaningful representations while mitigating overfitting.
Regarding the parameters for the VAE-WL loss function, after some experimentation
with stable results they were define as γ = 5 and β = 0.1. Both VAEs use Binary Cross-
Entropy for the reconstruction error of the function.
43

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
Figure 3.2: Illustration of the VAE Architecture used in the experiments. The top rectangle
represents the encoder and the bottom one the decoder. The architecture consists of 2D
convolutional layers (Conv 2D), with certain layers repeated twice (2x) in the encoder, while
others layers are repeated three times (3x) in the decoder. Fully connected layers (FC) are
included to process feature representations.
The experiment considered three datasets: MNIST, CBIS-DDSM Mass and Calcifica-
tion. The first is a well-known dataset for benchmarking in image related works, and it
contains 70,000 greyscale handwritten digits with a size of 28 by 28 pixels. The second
and third datasets contain greyscale scanned mammography studies with 1,696 and 1,872
images, respectively, which where resized to 64 by 64 pixels. These two datasets were used
because they represent a domain of medical imaging where missing values are frequent.
All datasets where normalized within [0, 1] and split in train, validation and test sets with
60%, 20% and 20% proportions. The experiment considered four missing rates (20%, 30%,
40% and 50%), assigning the missing values randomly to each image and pre-imputed with
zero. To account for stochastic behaviors, each method was executed 30 times (the average
was used as the final result) with the datasets being shuffled in each run. The imputation
results were assessed through the mean absolute error (MAE) metric calculated with the
original images and the imputed ones. The results obtained from the experiment are
presented in Table 3.1.
The VAE-WL outperforms the standard VAE and the GAIN method in all experimented
scenarios. This allows for the conclusion that the imputation of missing values with the
VAE-WL is in fact better than the state-of-the-art generative models. Moreover, the VAE-
WL presents stable results across the different missing rates, with minor error increases in
higher rates (the same behavior is observed in the regular VAE). On the other hand, the
GAIN method presents in general worse results for smaller missing rates.
44

3.2 Assessing the Impact of Data Imputation in Mammog-
raphy Image Morphology
Traditional work on data imputation essentially evaluates two characteristics of impu-
tation algorithms: imputation quality (how well the original values are reconstructed) and
classification accuracy (performance of classifiers built with imputed data). However, in
healthcare contexts, more than aiming to reduce imputation or classification errors, the
imputation process should be interpretable to some extent.
For instance, in the scope of BC diagnosis, the effects of imputation on the morphology
of cancer lesions in MI are of utmost importance, since it lays the foundation for physicians
to be able to interpret and analyze the imputation results and determine whether they
add to the patient’s evaluation. Ultimately, the performance of imputation algorithms on
the preservation of morphologic structures may be the decisive factor in the adoption of
the method by clinicians. Data science research is often focused on metrics rather than on
clinical relevance, whereas the medical community often refrains from using methods from
data science in daily practice due to the lack of interpretability, confidence, and clinical
significance of what is proposed.
In what follows, we will assess the performance of six imputation methods (k-nearest
neighbour (KNN), multivariate imputation by chained equation (MICE), a matrix comple-
tion (MC) algorithm, VAE, deep image prior (DIP) and CycleGAN) for the reconstruction
45

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
of missing pixels in different morphologies in mammography images. The images in-
cluded are collected from four public datasets (CBIS-DDSM, Mini-MIAS Mammographic
Database (MIAS), INbreast, and Cohort of Screen-Aged Woman (CSAW)) and the impu-
tation results are evaluated through the MAE and structural similarity index measure
(SSIM). This approach goes beyond the traditional evaluation of imputation algorithms,
analyzing imputation quality, morphology preservation and classification performance.
We study the imputation of mammography images on a variety of BC morphologies and
determine the applicability of the reconstructed images by evaluating the preservation
of their anatomical characteristics. To that end, we determine the percentage of pixels
that are correctly reconstructed by each technique and assess the classification of imputed
images into masses/calcifications, and malignant/benign images.
3.2.1 Methodology
The research is divided into three phases, illustrated in Figure 3.3. In an initial
phase, missing pixels are artificially generated in images from four public datasets and
six state-of-the-art methodologies (KNN, MICE, a MC algorithm, VAE, DIP [ 190] and
CycleGAN [191]) are then evaluated for their effectiveness in imputing the missing pixels
based on MAE and SSIM metrics. In the second phase, images from the CBIS-DDSM
[54] with specific morphologies are examined to evaluate how the morphology is affected
by the imputation. In the last phase, the imputation of missing pixels is assessed by the
ability of a classifier to identify the specific morphological structures in the mammography
images, namely masses and calcifications, and distinguish between malignant and benign
images.
3.2.1.1 Data Collection and Preparation
The image data included is extracted from four mammography image datasets: the
CBIS-DDSM [54], the MIAS database [ 51], the INbreast database [ 55], and the CSAW)
[192]. Both region of interest (ROI) patch images and whole mammography images
are included. The whole mammography images are composed of two image projections:
craniocaudal (CC) and mediolateral oblique (MLO).
The ROI patch images are collected from the CBIS-DDSM dataset that contains a
total of 3,568 images, separated into 1,872 images of calcifications and 1,696 images of
masses. The 8 most common categories of the mass images from this dataset are selected
to study how the morphology is affected by the imputation. These categories are irregular

shape with spiculated and ill-defined margins, oval shape with circumscribed and obscured
margins, lobulated shape with circumscribed and obscured margins and, round shape with
circumscribed and ill-defined margins.
The whole mammography images are collected from the remaining datasets: 322 MLO
projections from the MIAS database, 250 CC and 250 MLO projections from the INbreast
database, and 1000 CC and 1000 MLO images selected from the CSAW database. The
pre-processing of the ROI patch images applies resizing of the image into 100x100 pixels
and normalization between 0 and 1. The pre-processing of the whole mammography
images removes the background to eliminate annotations and image markers and applies
resizing of the image into 256x256 pixels and normalization between 0 and 1.
3.2.1.2 Data Imputation
The six different imputation methodologies studied are KNN, MICE, MC, VAE [ 33],
DIP [190] and CycleGAN [ 191]. The missing data is randomly synthetically generated to
create a matrix of missing pixels for rates of 5, 10, 20, 30, 60, and 80%. The synthetically
generated missing pixels are then applied to the images.
47

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
The image datasets are randomly split into training (60%), validation (10%) and
testing (30%) sets at each run of the experimental setup. The VAE and CycleGAN models
are trained considering the training and validation sets of the original images, the missing
pixels, and the images with the missing pixels. These models originate a complete imputed
image that will substitute the values of the missing pixels. The KNN, MICE, MC, and
DIP methodologies directly impute the missing pixels with alternative values. After the
imputation, no post-processing step is applied to the imputed images. The experimental
setup is executed 30 times for each methodology and each missing pixel rate to obtain
average results and mitigate the impact of stochastic imputations.
The algorithms are implemented in Python code using a Pytorch framework for DIP
and CycleGAN algorithms and a Keras framework with Tensorflow backend for the
remaining algorithms. The experiment was performed on two NVIDIA Quadro RTX 6000
GPUs.
The evaluation of the imputation of the missing pixels is performed considering visual
comparison and quantitative analysis based on the evaluation metrics MAE and SSIM,
as defined in Chapter 2. A paired t-test at an alpha value of 0.05 is used to test for a
statistically significant difference in means between the imputation performance of the
algorithms.
3.2.2 Impact on Morphology and Identification of Cancer Lesions
The visual results of the imputation phase in the CBIS-DDSM dataset are illustrated in
Figures 3.4 and 3.5. The first column presents the images with the randomly synthetically
generated missing pixels. The last column presents the original image or ground truth. The
remaining columns exemplify the imputation results from the six imputation algorithms.
The lines represent the different missing rates.
The images obtained from the KNN and MICE algorithms present the best image
quality for both images until the missing rate of 30%. For higher missing rates, the KNN
and MICE imputed images became corrupted and consequently, the calcifications and the
margin of the mass are not visible. The images obtained from the MC algorithm present
similar results to KNN and MICE, however, MC presents higher corruption. The VAE and
CycleGAN algorithms are not capable of reconstructing the image without significantly
increasing the noise. The images obtained from the DIP algorithm are the most similar to
the ground truth. This algorithm visually outperforms KNN and MICE, since the location
of the calcifications and the margin of the mass remains visible for all missing rates.
48

focused on the three best-performing algorithms: KNN, MICE, and DIP. The average
values of the metrics for the three algorithms are presented in Tables 3.2 and 3.3.

3.2.2.1 Evaluation of the Imputation on the Morphology
To evaluate how the mammography image morphology is affected by the imputation,
the CBIS-DDSM dataset is divided into subsets of the morphology categories previously
identified and an evaluation is conducted to understand how the imputation affects the
image quality of the various morphologies.
The visual comparison of the imputation results between four of the morphology
categories (irregular shape with spiculates margins, oval shape with circumscribed margins,
lobulated shape with obscured margins, and round shape with ill-defined margins) for
60% of missing pixel rate is illustrated in Figure 3.6. The first column presents the images
with the missing pixels and the last column presents the ground truth. The remaining
columns illustrate the imputation results from KNN, MICE and DIP. The lines represent
the 4 different morphology categories.
The visual assessment of the imputed images does not allow distinguishing differences
between the imputation algorithms, nor identifying whether certain algorithms perform
better for specific morphologies. An analysis considering the values of MAE and SSIM
achieves a similar conclusion, in which the values of the metrics for each morphology
category are similar to the values obtained from the complete CBIS-DDSM dataset.
Although metrics such as MAE and SSIM and visual assessment can evaluate the
overall quality of the image, to improve the analysis by morphology, an analysis to study
local corruptions of pixel values that may lead to deformations in the image morphology
is also conducted. This evaluation focuses on understanding the level of corruption caused
by the imputation based on the percentage of pixels that are correctly reconstructed. This
evaluation is performed based on the error between the pixels of the imputed and the
original image according to pre-defined thresholds: pixels with errors between 0 and 1%

The results demonstrate that DIP outperforms the other algorithms in terms of
preserving morphology for missing rates of 30, 60 and 80%. However, for missing rates
of 5, 10, 20 and 30%, all imputation algorithms successfully recover at least 80% of the
missing pixels within the correctly imputed range, ensuring that the overall morphology
of the images is preserved.
The evaluations performed have not been able to distinguish one of the algorithms
as the best. However, for medical imaging, the preservation of the morphology is more
relevant than the values of metrics. Therefore, considering that DIP can achieve the best
overall morphology preservation and the highest morphology preservation for missing rates
between 30 and 80%, this algorithm is considered the most suitable methodology.
53

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
3.2.2.2 Evaluation of the Imputation in Classification Tasks
To assess the ability of a classifier to classify the imputed mammography images,
the classification evaluation is divided into two main classification problems. The first
classification problem focuses on morphology by distinguishing between masses and
calcifications, while the second classification problem focuses on distinguishing between
malignant and benign characteristics.
These two problems differ in terms of complexity and importance for clinical application.
The classification between masses and calcifications only allows identifying the type of
anomaly of BC which is an initial step in the diagnosis of BC. Further analysis of the type
of shape, type of margin, and even a biopsy to diagnose malignancy is required to obtain a
definite diagnosis of BC. The classification between malignant and benign tissue overcomes
the previous classification and presents relevant information for the diagnosis of BC. This
classification is more complex than the first classification and may provide information
that if incorrect can harm the treatment plan of a patient. Due to the importance of
distinguishing malignant from benign images, it is considered preferable to misclassify a
benign image than to misclassify a malignant image.
To evaluate the imputation by the two classification problems, two CNN-based binary
classifiers are developed to distinguish between masses and calcifications and between
malignant and benign images. The CBIS-DDSM ROI patch dataset is split into training
(60%), validation (10%), and testing (30%) sets. The experimental setup is executed
30 times for each classifier, each algorithm, and each missing pixel rate to account for
stochastic behaviour. The accuracy, sensitivity, and specificity results from the evaluation
of the imputation based on classification tasks are presented in Table 3.7.
In the initial phase, the testing set without missing pixels is evaluated to understand
the full potential of the classifiers. The results show that the mass and calcification
classifier effectively differentiates between the two morphologies, while the malignant and
benign classifier struggles to distinguish between the two image types.
The presence of missing pixels in the testing set significantly reduces the accuracy of
both classifiers, with a relevant reduction of sensitivity. The value of sensitivity is important
for the classification analysis due to the preferability of misclassifying a benign image.
To analyze how the imputed images improve or worsen the results of the classification in
comparison to the images with missing pixels, the evaluation should focus on how much
the sensitivity has improved.
54

Missing 53.33% 9.08% 93.49% 50.78% 39.60% 58.51%
KNN 77.34% 76.85% 77.78% 58.47% 46.27% 66.90%
MICE 74.98% 74.11% 75.77% 58.08% 42.96% 68.53%
DIP 84.73% 86.64% 83.00% 63.64% 43.51% 77.54%
The results for the classifier that distinguishes masses and calcifications concluded
that MICE achieved better classification than KNN imputed images for missing rates
between 5 and 30% and similarly to the previous stages, DIP imputed images achieved
better classification for missing rates of 60 and 80%. The values of sensitivity increased
for all the imputed images; however, no imputation methodology is able to achieve similar
or better results than the results on the testing set without missing pixels.
On the other hand, for the classifier that distinguishes malignant and benign images,
DIP imputed images outperform the other algorithms’ images with the highest accuracy and
sensitivity. This result is significant since the sensitivity values improve after imputation
55

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
and in the case of the missing rate of 5% the values of accuracy, sensitivity and specificity
are even higher than the values from the testing set.
Overall, the DIP algorithm outperforms the KNN and MICE algorithms for the
classification of malignant and benign images, and for the classification of mass and
calcification for the missing rates of 60 and 80%. This algorithm also presents the highest
values of sensitivity for the classification of malignant and benign images.
3.3 Conclusion
In this chapter, we addressed two key limitations on missing pixel imputation in MI.
The first limitation is the lack of exploration of VAE for image imputation and the
absence of a suitable loss function tailored for this problem. To address this, we introduced
an extended version of the VAE, named VAE-WL, which incorporates a custom loss
function specifically designed to enhance the imputation of missing values. Our results
demonstrate that VAE-WL consistently outperforms both benchmark models — a standard
VAE and the GAIN method — across all tested scenarios, achieving improvements of over
40% in several settings.
The second limitation is the lack of comprehensive performance assessments of impu-
tation algorithms for reconstructing missing pixels in different morphological structures
in MI. Our approach goes beyond traditional evaluations of imputation algorithms by
analyzing imputation quality, classification performance, and morphology preservation.
By conducting a thorough imputation research while emphasizing the importance of
preserving breast lesion morphologies in BC diagnosis, we contribute to both the data
science and medical communities, bridging the gap between computational advancements
and clinical relevance. The results show that while KNN and MICE provide strong overall
imputation performance, DIP is the most effective for higher missing pixel rates. All three
methods successfully preserve breast lesion morphology, but DIP excels in both overall
morphology preservation and performance at missing rates above 30%. Furthermore, DIP
achieves the highest sensitivity in classifying malignant and benign cases, making it the
most suitable approach for mammography pixel imputation.
The approaches presented in this chapter advance the state-of-the-art not only due to
their novelty but also their potential for further research and clinical applications. By
introducing a novel image pixel imputation algorithm and conducting a comprehensive
analysis of morphological structure preservation, we have expanded the current knowledge
in the field and laid the groundwork for future studies.
56

Chapter 3. Imputation Methodologies Applied to Mammography
Future research should focus on evaluating the proposed methods on additional
datasets, particularly those containing color images, and assessing their performance
across different missing pixel patterns. A multi-objective approach that simultaneously
optimizes imputation, classification, and morphology preservation should also be explored.
Expanding the range of tested algorithms could help identify the most effective imputation
techniques for varying missing pixel rates, ultimately contributing to more accurate and
reliable imaging methods for medical applications.
57

Chapter 4
Complementary Applications in
Mammography
This chapter moves beyond pixel-based imputation to explore higher-level techniques
that address the broader challenges of medical image reconstruction and clinical inter-
pretability. Specifically, it examines two complementary approaches: image-to-image
translation for mammography reconstruction and concept-based interpretability for as-
sessing model reliability. In Section 4.1, the potential of image-to-image translation for
reconstructing mammography data is examined and its clinical applicability is assessed.
Recognizing the need for robust validation, Section 4.2 introduces a concept-based inter-
pretability method that translates machine learning outputs into clinically relevant scores.
This approach enhances the understanding of imputation performance and its implica-
tions for clinical use. Together, these methodologies aim to advance both the technical
capabilities and practical relevance of mammography imaging imputation research.
4.1 Mammography Reconstruction via Image-to-Image Trans-
lation
To accurately diagnose breast cancer (BC), both mediolateral oblique (MLO) and
craniocaudal (CC) projections are indispensable for clinical examination due to their
complementary information. However, this procedure requires breast repositioning, a time-
consuming process often associated with patient discomfort due to tissue recompression
[193].
58

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
During the acquisition process, the integrity of information within one or both projec-
tions may be compromised [20, 33]. Image-to-image translation, proficient in transforming
one image type into another, has presented the ability to generate realistic synthetic
images in different medical imaging modalities which makes it promising in its application
for mammography projection translation [194].
Image-to-image translation approaches predominantly utilizing generative adversarial
network (GAN), have been extensively applied in medical imaging, including computer
tomography (CT), positron emission tomography (PET), and magnetic resonance imaging
(MRI) [194, 195].
Armanious et al. [ 196] propose a combination of a conditional GAN with non-
adversarial losses and a novel generator architecture to undertake various image-to-image
tasks such as PET to CT translation, MRI motion correction, and PET denoising. The
approach outperforms other conditional GANs, achieving SS I M = 0.92 and P SN R = 24.62.
Kong et al. [ 197] introduce a GAN capable of translating between T1 and T2 MRI for
brain imaging. Compared to established GANs like pix2pix [ 198] and CycleGAN, [191]
their proposed approach demonstrates slight performance improvements with SS I M = 0.83
and P SN R = 24.00.
Furthermore, Liu et al. [ 199] propose an enhancement to CycleGAN [ 191] by using a
pseudo-cycle consistent module and a domain control module for additional identical con-
straints. This approach, evaluated for MRI to CT translation, surpasses the performance
of CycleGAN and other conditional networks with M AE = 0.04 and P SN R = 39.10.
Moreover, Dalmaz et al. [200] present ResViT, a GAN architecture combining vision
transformers with convolution operators for multimodal medical image synthesis. This
approach, studied for translating between MRI to Multi-Contrast MRI and MRI to CT,
outperforms existing methodologies.
In mammography, however, the focus has mainly been on generating synthetic images
for dataset augmentation and contralateral mammographies. While approaches like the
ones proposed by Lee et al. [ 201] and Joseph et al. [ 202] have effectively generated realistic
mammography image (MI), none have been explicitly developed for reconstructing a new
mammography projection from another. Implementing such an approach could alleviate
patient burden, minimize radiation exposure, and reduce costs by requiring the acquisition
of only one mammography projection. Thus, a notable research gap remains in approaches
addressing this specific need within mammography imaging.
59

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
This approach aims to investigate the feasibility of reconstructing a mammography
projection from its complementary projection, to potentially reduce the need for repeating
the examination and consequently, mitigate patient burden, minimize radiation exposure,
and reduce costs [ 22]. To evaluate mammography reconstruction, five image-to-image
translation approaches are compared: Pix2Pix [ 198], CycleGAN [191], DiscoGAN [203],
UNIT [204], and ResViT [ 200]. The methodology is tested on two open-source datasets,
Cohort of Screen-Aged Woman (CSAW) and Curated Breast Imaging Subset of DDSM
(CBIS-DDSM), to assess its adaptability across different contrast levels and abnormalities.
Performance is measured using mean absolute error (MAE), peak signal-to-noise ratio
(PSNR), and structural similarity index measure (SSIM).
4.1.1 Image-to-Image Translation Techniques
This approach aims to evaluate various image-to-image translation techniques for
reconstructing one mammography projection from its complementary projection. The
translation process is assessed in both directions: from MLO to CC and from CC to MLO.
The translation directions and the basic architectures of the approaches are depicted in

60

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
In selecting approaches for this experiment, the aim is to encompass the main commonly
employed approaches in other image modalities, namely pix2pix [ 198] and CycleGAN
[191], along with less commonly used such as DiscoGAN [ 203], UNIT [204], and ResViT
[200].
Pix2Pix [198] employs a conditional GAN architecture with a U-Net generator and
PatchGAN discriminator, focusing on paired image translation. In contrast, CycleGAN
[191] introduces cycle consistency loss for unpaired image translation, using ResNet
generators and PatchGAN discriminators. Also designed for unpaired image-to-image
translation, DiscoGAN [203] uses cycle consistency loss but with simpler generator and
discriminator structures, incorporating an identity mapping loss and UNIT [204] employing
cycle consistency and identity mapping losses alongside a multi-scale discriminator. ResViT
[200], designed for medical image synthesis, combines residual connections and visual
transformers, featuring aggregated residual transformer blocks for diverse information
blending and a unified implementation for various source-target modalities.
The used mammography images originate from two distinct image datasets: the CSAW
[192] and CBIS-DDSM [ 54]. The pairs of CC and MLO projections for each breast are
selected from each dataset and in total, the CSAW dataset has 1,000 image pairs and
the CBIS-DDSM dataset has 1,216 image pairs. Table 4.1 presents the description of the
datasets according to the available information on BC diagnosis and type of abnormality.
Prior to analysis, pre-processing involved background removal, resizing to a standardized
size of 256x256 pixels, and normalization to scale pixel values between 0 and 1.
Table 4.1: Description of the BC Diagnosis and Type of Abnormality in both Image Datasets,
according to the pairs of MLO and CC projections.
CSAW CBIS-DDSM
Diagnosis Diagnosis Abnormality
Normal 622 Benign 653 Calcification 602
Cancer 378 Malignant 564 Mass 614
The translation capability is evaluated in both MLO to CC and CC to MLO directions.
The datasets are randomly split into training (80%) and testing (20%) set for each run,
with the experimental setup executed 30 times to account for stochastic behavior. The five
image-to-image translation approaches are adapted to train and test the mammography
grayscale images and fine-tuned to the two image datasets. The evaluation is performed
considering visual comparison and the evaluation metrics MAE, PSNR, and SSIM. A
paired t-test at an alpha value of 0.05 is used to test for a statistically significant difference.
61

The approaches demonstrate the capability to produce realistic mammography pro-
jections. DiscoGAN performed poorly, producing distorted images with noticeable noise.
Pix2pix and UNIT struggle with consistency, leading to misshapen breast contours. In
contrast, both CycleGAN and ResViT excelled, accurately capturing the intricate shape
and texture of breast tissue.
Additionally, significant disparities are observed between the CSAW and CBIS-DDSM
datasets, with CSAW images exhibiting higher contrast and more intricate details compared
to CBIS-DDSM. Despite these differences, all approaches demonstrated versatility in
handling mammography images with diverse characteristics.

Results show similarity in performance for both translation directions:
• For translation from MLO to CC projection, pix2pix performs better on the CSAW
dataset, while ResViT excels on the CBIS-DDSM dataset. However, statistical tests
reveal no significant performance difference between pix2pix and ResViT in the
CSAW dataset for MAE and SSIM metrics. Similarly, in the CBIS-DDSM dataset,
no statistically significant difference is observed between the two models across all
evaluated metrics;
• For the translation from CC to MLO projection, ResViT achieves the best results
for the CSAW dataset, while pix2pix performs better for the CBIS-DDSM dataset.
Again, statistical tests show no significant performance disparity between pix2pix
and ResViT in the CSAW dataset for PSNR metrics and in the CBIS-DDSM dataset
for the PSNR and SSIM metrics.
63

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
Consequently, due to the lack of statistical relevance in both translation directions,
relying solely on evaluation metrics fails to definitively establish a superior approach
between pix2pix and ResViT. Upon visual examination, pix2pix exhibits deficiencies in
image structure, leading to the determination of ResViT as the most effective methodology
for the translation of both directions.
Overall, evaluation metrics indicate lower performance in reconstructing MLO from
CC projection, possibly due to missing vital information in the CC projection, such as
pectoral muscle location, crucial for accurate MLO reconstruction. These findings suggest
a greater potential for further exploration of the opposite translation, offering promising
avenues for future research.
4.2 Improving Image Classification via Human-in-the-Loop
Approaches
Medical imaging is an essential tool for diagnosing, managing, and treating patients.
However, the high demand of medical imaging acquisition and posterior time-consuming
clinical assessment requires the development of efficient techniques to aid in this process.
Medical image classification is a computational science problem that improves patient
prognosis by providing information on disease assessment, staging and treatment response.
As previously discussed, most of the classification methodologies proposed in the
literature are based on image enhancement [ 205, 206] and the development of refined
neural network architectures [ 207, 208] that outperform state-of-the-art and clinician
assessments, with deep learning technologies occupying the pool position for this task.
However, the major issue of such techniques relies on their black-box nature which has
delayed their use in real-world scenarios.
Interpretability methodologies have become widespread for medical applications due
to their capacity to translate black-box models into understandable information. These
methodologies are mainly divided into intrinsic interpretability, which refers to machine
learning models considered interpretable due to their simple structure and, post hoc
interpretability which refers to the application of interpretation methods after model
training [209]. Interpretability strategies can be differentiated into categories such as
feature importance, which gives the importance of an input feature on the prediction,
saliency maps that visually illustrate the importance of different features by coloring a
pixel in a given prediction, and model visualization techniques that help visualize patterns
64

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
detected in an image or the feature distribution in the dataset [ 210]. These methods are
also described according to global interpretability which aims to understand the common
patterns on a population level, by studying the model’s parameters and the learned
relationships [211].
In the healthcare scenario, one of the most promising techniques based on global inter-
pretability is concept-based techniques. These methods can understand the predictions of a
deep neural network through user-specified concepts, which can be any data characteristic
from a visual pattern to clinical information. However, concepts should be meaningful
and coherent to increase human understanding of such models [ 212]. Concept-based ex-
planations were first formalized with the concept activation vector (CAV) formalism [ 213]
to generate post hoc global explanations for neural networks. CAV [ 213] is a numerical
representation that generalizes a concept in the activation space of a neural network layer
and measures the extent of that concept’s influence on the prediction for a particular class.
CAVs are learned by training a linear classifier to distinguish between the activations
produced by a concept’s examples and random opposing examples in any layer [213].
Extensions of this work, such as concept activation region (CAR), intend to improve
the generation of global explanations. CAR [ 214] introduces an extension of the CAV
formalism that does not assume linear separability by allowing concept examples to be
scattered across different clusters in the neural network latent space. This generalization is
implemented by replacing the linear classifiers with kernel-based support vector classifiers.
The global explanations provided by the CAV and CAR implementations allow the
identification of the relationships that the network learned and can be used to identify
possible errors during training.
In case these errors are identified, it may be possible to use this knowledge to improve
the classification model. However, the identified flaws should present significantly different
behavior in comparison to other concepts so that data balancing or augmentation may lead
to improved classifier performance. Several works have studied the application of these
approaches in medical imaging [ 215, 216, 217], however, none has presented an automatic
approach capable of improving classification performance after concept detection.
Here in, we study how the CAV and CAR implementations can be used to identify
flaws in the training of a neural network and how these weaknesses can be mitigated in a
human-in-the-loop process. Our main goal is to determine whether the identification of
user-defined concepts within a human-in-the-loop approach can be used to automatically
improve the performance and trustworthiness of a classifier.
65

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
To reach such goal, three phases have been defined: in the initial phase, the baseline
classifier is trained using publicly available datasets; in the second phase, the concept-based
interpretability methodologies CAR and CAV are applied to the baseline classifier, and the
results are analyzed to identify the concepts that affect the performance of the baseline
classifier; lastly, a human-in-the-loop approach validated by a team of 2 clinicians that
manipulates the dataset according to the concepts identified previously is implemented to
improve the classifier performance. Four medical imaging datasets of different imaging
modalities (histology, endoscopy, mammography, and smartphone images) are included in
this study to prove the generality of the proposed method. The achieved results identified
two concept categories that represent flaws in the training of the classifiers and the
human-in-the-loop approach achieved an improvement of 0.73% in accuracy and 1.62% in
recall for the abnormality classifier that distinguishes masses and calcifications.
4.2.1 Concept-Based Explanations
CAV [213] is a numerical representation that generalizes a concept in the activation
space of a neural network layer and measures the extent of that concept’s influence on the
prediction for a particular classification class. a concept’s examples and random opposing
examples in any layer [213].
To determine the CAV of a concept, the following steps must be completed:
1. A concept set and a random opposing set of examples are prepared;
2. A hidden layer of the neural network is targeted;
3. A binary classifier that separates the activations generated by the concept set and
the random set is trained;
4. The sensitivity of the prediction is measured by calculating the directional derivative
of the prediction in the CAV direction;
5. The resulting coefficient vector of the trained binary classifier is the CAV of the
concept.
The accuracy of a classification class, also known as testing with concept activation
vectors (TCAV), is determined by the ratio between the inputs with positive conceptual
sensitivities and the number of inputs for that class. TCAV serves as a metric that
quantifies the relation between the model’s predictions and specific concepts [ 218]. Since
66

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
TCAV evaluates how strongly a concept is associated with a particular class, the resulting
accuracy score provides a global interpretation of the model’s decision-making process,
offering insights into its overall behavior and conceptual understanding.
CAR [214] is an extension of the CAV formalism that does not assume linear separability.
Figure 4.4 exemplifies the linearity and non-linearity of the latent space of the CAV and
CAR classifiers.
TCAV
(a) Latent Space of CAV.
TCAR (b) Latent Space of CAR.
Figure 4.4: Examples of Latent Spaces for CAV and CAR Classifiers. The separation of the
latent space is linear for the CAV classifiers and non-linear for the CAR classifier.
To determine the CAR of a concept, similar steps to those previously described for
the determination of CAV are performed. However, since CAV is computed with linear
classifiers, an alternative approach is required to define concept importance for the non-
linear classifiers of CAR [ 214]. Testing with CAR score is built based on a smoothness
assumption: a concept is crucial for an example if the model’s representation for this
example lies in a cluster of concept representations. With this characterization, these
scores are the CAR equivalent of TCAV scores [214].
Overall, the CAR methodology presents more accurate descriptions of how concepts
are scattered in the neural network latent space, global explanations that are closer to
human concept annotations, and concept-based feature importance that meaningfully
relate concepts with each other in comparison to CAV [214].
4.2.2 Proposed Approach
This research is divided into three main parts: the training of the baseline classifier,
the implementation of the CAR and CAV methodologies, and lastly, the implementation
of a human-in-the-loop approach to improve classifier performance. The experimental
setup is illustrated in Figure 4.5.

4.2.2.1 Dataset Description
The four datasets included in this study are BreakHis [ 219], KID2 [220, 221], CBIS-
DDSM [53, 54], and PAD-UFES-20 [ 222]. The BreakHis dataset [ 219] is composed of
2,480 benign and 5,429 malignant microscopic histological images of breast tumor tissue
collected using different magnifying factors. The KID2 [ 220, 221] dataset consists of 1,778
normal and 593 pathology wireless capsule endoscopy images. The CBIS-DDSM [ 53, 54] is
composed of 1,872 calcification and 1,696 mass region of interest of mammography images.
The PAD-UFES-20 [222] dataset contains 1,089 skin cancer and 1,209 disease smartphone
images depicting various skin lesions. These datasets are publicly available and offer a
extensive collection of medical images with complementary clinical/patient information.
The classification problem is based on binary classification within each dataset, targeting
the main classes previously identified. Table 4.3 presents further description on the
classification problem and the representation of each class within each dataset.
68

Table 4.3: Image Dataset Description.
4.2.2.2 Baseline Classifier Description
The residual network (ResNet) architecture [ 223] is used as the baseline classifier to
implement the classification problems. ResNet has been implemented as the backbone of
several neural networks for classification applications in medical imaging [ 224, 225]. The
ResNet50 architecture is chosen for this work due to its simplicity and lower computational
requirements and because it presents the best overall performance for the image datasets
previously described. This neural network is pre-trained with ImageNet weights and fine-
tuned for the classes of each of the classification problems. For the study of interpretability
across the neural network, hooks modules are introduced on the first pool layer and layers
1 - 4 of the ResNet50 architecture.
The ResNet50 network is trained for a maximum of 200 epochs, using the Adam
Optimizer and learning rate 1e-04. Transformations by horizontal/vertical flips and
rotation are implemented during the training to improve the performance of the model.
The dataset is divided into 60% training, 30% testing, and 10% validation sets for the
ResNet, CAR, and CAV classifiers. To account for stochastic behavior, the simulations of
the classifiers are repeated for 30 runs.
4.2.2.3 Concept-Based Interpretability
The interpretability of the baseline classifiers previously described is obtained using
CAV and CAR classifiers. These methodologies are applied post-hoc, with the initial
training of baseline classifiers preceding the training of CAV and CAR. The implementation
process of the CAV and CAR classifiers comprises five key steps:
69

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
1. Definition of the concepts - User-defined concepts are manually selected for each
image dataset (the concept selection process is detailed in the following section);
2. Creation of Training and Testing Sets - To train classifiers, distinct training and
testing sets are created for both concept and random opposing datasets;
3. Training the Classifiers - The hidden layers of the baseline classifier are targeted
and the CAV/CAR classifier that separates the activations generated by the concept
set and the random set is trained. The sensitivity of the prediction is measured by
calculating the directional derivative of the prediction in the classifier direction. The
resulting coefficient vector of the trained binary classifier is the CAV/CAR of the
concept;
4. Evaluation of Concept Accuracy - After the training, the accuracy of the classifiers
is assessed using the testing dataset;
5. Application of CAV and CAR to the baseline classifier - The classifiers are applied
to the baseline classifier. To produce a global explanation of a class, the testing
score is calculated by the ratio of inputs with positive conceptual sensitivities to the
number of inputs for a class.
This process is repeated for each concept and for each baseline classifier. Given that
the baseline classifiers are repeated for 30 runs, the CAV and CAR classifiers are similarly
trained and tested for each of the 30 classifiers for every dataset.
The analysis of concept-based interpretability is focused on two main components:
the analysis of concept accuracy and the interpretation of the global explanations. The
concept accuracy analysis assesses the generalization of CAV/CAR classifiers to unseen
datasets. The accuracy of the testing dataset is pivotal in determining concept accuracy,
with both concept and random datasets used for training and testing the classifiers. The
global explanation interpretation compares testing scores for each concept and class to
ascertain if CAR concept regions correlate with classification classes.
4.2.2.4 Concept Selection
The selected concepts for this work are based on the information available from each
dataset. Since the selection of user-defined concepts should be meaningful and coherent
to increase human understanding, the datasets included in this work were selected due to
the diversity, representation, and clinical relevance of the information available in each
70

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
dataset. To ensure each defined concept is represented in the creation of the concept and
random datasets, 200 image samples are required for the training set and 30 samples
for the testing set are required for each concept [ 214]. Table 4.4 presents the concepts
considered for each dataset.

The concepts available for:
• BreaKHis dataset are the categories of histological types of tumors. The dataset
contains four types of benign breast tumors - adenosis, fibroadenoma, tubular
adenoma, and phyllodes tumor; and four malignant tumors - ductal carcinoma,
lobular carcinoma, mucinous carcinoma, and papillary carcinoma;
• KID2 dataset are the different types of normal and pathology images: normal small
bowel, normal stomach, normal esophagus, vascular and inflammatory;
• CBIS-DDSM dataset are divided into 4 categories: BI-RADS score (0,2,3,4,5),
breast density score (1,2,3,4), mass and calcification characteristics;
• PAD-UFES-20 dataset are divided into 4 categories: diagnostic, region of skin lesion,
and different indicators for itching, growing and bleeding. The diagnostic category is
associated to the types of skin cancers (Basal Cell Carcinoma) and diseases (Actinic
Keratosis).
71

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
4.2.2.5 Evaluating Explanations and Human-in-the-Loop Insights
The information provided by the concept-based interpretability is further explored
to improve the performance of the classification problems using a human-in-the-loop
approach. This approach with the direct intervention of a team of 2 clinicians is composed
of five main steps:
1. Analysis of the interpretability-based results to identify weaknesses in the training
of the classifiers - After the analysis of the interpretability results, the misclassified
classes/concepts that the classifier is not capable of correctly classifying are identified.
The misclassification is a consequence of a weakness of the neural networks’ learning
process which is not just related to low values of accuracy, precision, and recall but
also related to the way it learns (the features considered during training - relevant
information for a clinician). The identification of the weaknesses in the training
allows the understanding of what happened incorrectly during the training process
and the best alteration to perform to improve the learning process;
2. Manipulation of the training dataset to mitigate these weaknesses- After identifying
the classes/concepts that require improvements, an approach to manipulate the
training dataset based on elimination, augmentation, and/or balancing techniques is
defined;
3. Re-training of the classifiers - the ResNet classifier is retrained with the improved
training dataset;
4. Comparison between the best classifiers - The newly trained classifier is compared
with the baseline and the other retrained ResNet classifiers in terms of performance
and stability to understand if it corrected the weaknesses identified previously. The
retrained classifier is only validated if it is considered trustworthy for real-world
applicability by the clinicians;
5. Repetition of the previous steps - The previous steps are repeated until the clinicians
achieve consensus on the performance and trustworthiness of the new classifier or
100 runs of the loop are achieved.
The inclusion of 2 experienced clinicians within the loop allows the validation of the
improved classifiers in terms of improvement of performance, stability, and trustworthiness
for real-world applicability.
72

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
4.2.3 Evaluating Explanations and Human-in-the-Loop Insights
The baseline classifiers were obtained by running the ResNet50 classifiers for each
dataset. Table 4.5 presents the results of all classifiers, where the best classification is
achieved for the BreaKHis dataset. These results are considered the baseline for this work
and it is expected to improve the performance of these classifiers in the next sections.

74

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
In terms of average concept accuracy, the CAR classifier is overall better than the
CAV classifier. The vascular and inflammatory concepts in the KID2 dataset and the
density 1 concept in the CBIS-DDSM are the only exceptions to these results. The
vascular and inflammatory concepts represent concepts within the pathology images that
are underrepresented in the datasets (only 25.01%). Due to the unbalancing of the dataset,
the linear classifier CAV may be more suitable to classify these two concepts. Similarly,
Density 1 is the concept least represented in the density concept category and consequently,
the CAV may be better than CAR to classify the concept.
In terms of global explanation analysis, the results for the KID2, BreaKHis, and PAD-
UFES-20 show that each concept is strongly correlated with a single classification class.
However, the CBIS-DDSM dataset does not present similar behavior. In CBIS-DDSM,
certain concepts show a slight increase in global explanation values for the incorrect
classification class, suggesting weaker separability. Although the BI-RADS category
achieves higher concept accuracy than the density category, the BIRADS 4 concept fails to
correlate exclusively with a single classification class. As a result, the BI-RADS category is
excluded from the human-in-the-loop approach. Conversely, in the PAD-UFES-20 dataset,
both the diagnostic and bleeding categories demonstrate a strong correlation between
concepts and classification classes. Despite the diagnostic category showing high concept
accuracy, both categories are included in the human-in-the-loop approach to leverage their
interpretability and potential clinical relevance.
After analyzing the global explanations, it is identified that there is a high correlation
between the concepts and the classification classes. However, in certain concepts, there
remains a significant testing CAR score in the incorrect classification class. To improve
the performance of the classifiers, it is decided to balance the representation of all the
concepts to reduce the testing CAR score in the incorrect classification classes. For this
purpose, various manipulation techniques (elimination, augmentation, and balancing) are
explored to gradually improve the representation of the concepts within the training set
while maintaining a continuous validation of the approach with the team of clinicians.
After each application of a manipulation technique, the performance and trustworthiness
of the retrained classifier are re-evaluated and the process is repeated until consensus or
100 runs of the loop is achieved. The final retrained model for all datasets is based on
stratified 10 fold cross-validation where all the concept categories are balanced between
the training and testing sets. The results for these classifiers and respective improvement
are presented in Table 4.8.

A Wilcoxon rank-sum statistical test at an alpha value of 0.05 is used to test for
a statistically significant difference in means of each metric between the stratified 10
fold cross-validation based classifiers and the baseline classifiers. All the improvements
identified in Table 4.8 are considered statistically significant, except for the precision value
of the CBIS-DDSM dataset.
Even though most datasets achieve an improvement in classifier performance (3%),
the improvements of the CBIS-DDSM dataset are not as significant in comparison to
other datasets. This result is a consequence of the weak correlation between concepts
and classification classes for the density concepts that limits the classifier performance
improvement. To improve the performance of this dataset, it is considered that there is
a need to introduce more images to the original dataset to improve the diversity of the
concepts/classes within the training dataset. The augmentation of this dataset should
be focused on the cases that present a significant score in the incorrect class identified in
Table 4.7 (BI-RADS 0 and 5, and Density 1, 2, 3 and 4).
The unbalanced representation of concepts within the dataset, previously mentioned
in the concept-based interpretability results, lead to a reduction of performance of the
CAR classifier. However, after applying the 10 fold cross validation based on the concept,
it is possible to verify that the unbalanced datasets (BreaKHis and KID2) present similar
improvements to the balanced dataset (PAD-UFES-20).
76

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
4.3 Conclusion
In this chapter, we moved beyond pixel-based imputation to explore advanced tech-
niques for mammography through image-to-image translation and concept-based clinical
interpretability. These approaches contribute to both enhancing medical image quality
and improving the reliability of deep learning models in clinical applications.
The first approach focused on assessing the feasibility of reconstructing a mammography
projection from its complementary projection using image-to-image translation architec-
tures. Despite its potential, such an approach has not yet been applied to mammography
for projection reconstruction. Our results demonstrate that ResViT outperforms other
models in translating between both mammographic projections, with a general trend of
better performance when reconstructing the MLO from the CC projection. This finding
suggests that certain mammographic structures and features are better preserved in this
translation direction.
The second approach explored how CAV can be used to identify weaknesses in neural
network training and how these can be mitigated through a human-in-the-loop framework.
By incorporating two experienced clinicians within the loop, we validated the improved
classifiers, demonstrating enhancements in performance, stability, and trustworthiness
for real-world applicability. The results revealed that key concepts within each dataset
highlighted flaws in classifier training. By integrating human expertise into the model
refinement process, the human-in-the-loop approach led to a statistically significant 3%
improvement in accuracy, precision, and recall. These findings highlight how user-defined
concepts can enhance classifier performance while providing insights into how specific
concepts influence classification outcomes.
The approaches presented in this chapter advance the state-of-the-art by introducing
novel applications of image-to-image translation and concept-based interpretability. The
combination of these techniques not only improves mammographic imaging but also
establishes a foundation for future advancements in explainable artificial intelligence for
medical imaging.
For image-to-image translation, future research should focus on improving the recon-
struction of the MLO from the CC projection, incorporating BC characteristics to enhance
clinical applicability. Particular emphasis should be placed on preserving breast shape
and texture, ensuring the technique’s reliability for real-world diagnostic use.
For concept-based interpretability, future work should prioritize expanding high-
quality labeled datasets to increase the number and diversity of user-defined concepts,
77

Chapter 4. Complementary Applications in Mammography
further advancing this technique. The inclusion of more clinically relevant concepts could
significantly enhance the impact of this methodology, providing greater transparency and
improved model performance in medical imaging applications.
78
