79

Chapter 5
The Role of Deep Learning in Medical
Image Inpainting: A Systematic Review
In this chapter, the focus shifts from enhancement and imputation techniques in
mammography imaging to a broader exploration of inpainting methodologies across various
medical imaging modalities. While previous research centered on refining mammographic
images, a diverse range of techniques has been developed to enhance the quality of
other imaging types through advanced imputation and inpainting methods. This chapter
presents a comprehensive review of inpainting methodologies designed for medical imaging,
highlighting state-of-the-art deep learning approaches, their applications, and their impact
on improving image quality and diagnostic accuracy across different imaging modalities.
5.1 Introduction
Image imputation, often referred to as inpainting in the context of images, is a crucial
technique in computer vision, particularly for reconstructing corrupted images. This
technique, known to reconstruct or complete corrupted portions of an image, has garnered
significant attention in both scientific and practical domains. Initially conceptualized in
2000 [226], it aimed to mimic the fundamental image restoration processes employed by
professional art restorers, leveraging digital reconstruction algorithms with minimal human
intervention. Over time, image inpainting has evolved into a cornerstone pre-processing
technique within computer vision, continually advancing with increasingly sophisticated
algorithms and a broadening scope of applications.
80

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
Medical Image Inpainting stands out as a prominent application of this technique,
particularly in addressing issues caused during the image acquisition and transmission
processes due to instrumental error, metallic radiation, or human factors [ 227]. Image
inpainting offers a solution by filling the corrupted regions and improving image quality. In
clinical practice, image inpainting plays a vital role in various applications. For example,
in neuroimaging, precise structural details are essential for diagnosing brain disorders
such as stroke, multiple sclerosis, and neurodegenerative diseases. In oncology, clear
and artifact-free images are crucial for identifying tumor boundaries, assessing disease
progression, and planning radiation therapy. Beyond these examples, image inpainting has
broad applications in cardiology, orthopedics, and pulmonology, where accurate imaging is
key for detecting anomalies, guiding interventions, and monitoring treatment outcomes. By
enhancing image quality and restoring missing or degraded regions, inpainting techniques
contribute to improved diagnostic accuracy and clinical decision-making across multiple
medical fields, ultimately leading to better patient outcomes.
Early approaches to image inpainting primarily relied on traditional methods, which
sought information from surrounding or background areas to complete missing portions
based on similar characteristics [ 228]. These techniques can be categorized into diffusion-
based and patch-based techniques. Diffusion-based methods are defined by a smooth
propagation of the image context from the boundary to the interior of the missing region
[229, 230, 231], while patch-based methods rely on searching for well-matching regions
in undamaged areas or external data of the image to fill the image holes [ 232, 233, 234].
Although these methodologies have been the subject of numerous studies, the generality
of these approaches falls in recreating the high-level semantic content of images and
addressing larger missing portions because their perception is limited to the boundaries of
the missing portions without considering the total available image information [235].
Recognizing the need for more robust and efficient techniques, the development of deep
learning techniques has emerged as a transformative avenue for advancing image inpainting
[15, 236, 237, 238]. Deep learning-based inpainting methods excels in capturing high-level
patterns and semantics, ensuring structural and textural consistency that traditional
methods often fail to achieve[ 15, 235, 238, 239]. These methods have the capability to
handle larger missing regions and reconstruct intricate anatomical details, making them
particularly well-suited for medical imaging applications. Their ability to learn from vast
datasets enables them to adapt to diverse imaging modalities, effectively restoring missing
or corrupted regions while maintaining diagnostic fidelity.
81

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
Furthermore, deep learning techniques have consistently outperformed classical in-
painting methods, demonstrating superior image quality, restoration accuracy, and clinical
applicability. Studies have shown that GAN-based inpainting, in particular, yields more
realistic reconstructions by capturing complex spatial correlations and generating context-
aware restorations. These advancements have proven especially valuable in medical
imaging, where precise reconstructions are crucial for clinical decision-making, disease
diagnosis, and treatment planning.
In this chapter, our main goal is to assess the impact of the use of Deep Learning
Techniques applied to Medical Imaging Inpainting.
To address this question, a comprehensive review was undertaken focusing on deep
image inpainting in medical imaging to identify specific deep learning architectures tailored
to medical imaging applications, and thoroughly discuss the associated limitations. The
identification of relevant research was conducted across several databases, including Web
of Science, Scopus, PubMed, and IEEE Xplore. Articles published between 2015 and 2024
were selected using a combination of keywords such as “deep learning”, “inpainting”, and
“medical imaging”, along with variations. The initial search yielded a total of 217 unique
results. Subsequently, the first filtering phase excluded reviews and works unrelated to
medical imaging, resulting in 166 works that progressed to the next stage. Finally, the
screening process applied a predefined set of exclusion criteria to ensure alignment with our
research goals. Specifically, works implementing deep learning techniques were scrutinized,
and those employing traditional inpainting approaches were excluded. Each publication
underwent meticulous evaluation, resulting in the identification of 53 publications that
satisfactorily met the search criteria and research objectives.
The analysis identified three categories of studies: general-purpose (methods initially
developed for non-medical images but later adapted for medical imaging), artifact-specific
(specialized techniques designed to remove specific artifacts), and multi-task applications
(approaches that combine inpainting with complementary tasks such as segmentation,
classification, and data augmentation). These findings underscore the significance of
deep image inpainting in artifact removal and enhancing the performance of multi-task
approaches, attributable to its pivotal role in location and inpainting regions of interest.
Additionally, it underscores its predominant utilization in magnetic resonance imaging
(MRI) and computer tomography (CT), while identifying GANs and U-Net as the preferred
architectures. The review also highlighted that future research direction should include
the development of blind inpainting techniques, techniques suitable for 3D/4D medical
imaging and addressing multiple artifacts and multi-task applications, and improvement
82

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
of deep learning architectures through alternative convolution techniques, loss functions,
and incorporation of texture information.
The structure of the chapter is as follows: Section 5.2 reviews the related literature
on overall deep learning inpainting techniques; Section 5.3 discusses the results of the
literature review on the application of deep learning inpainting techniques to medical
imaging; Section 5.4 presents the discussion and identify the main tendencies of the
reviewed works; Section 5.5 identifies open challenges, addressing issues such as mask
generation, diversity of medical imaging modalities, artifacts in medical imaging, and
deep learning architectures; lastly, Section 5.6 presents the conclusions, summarizing the
findings and suggesting future research directions.
5.2 Traditional Deep Learning Inpainting Techniques
Before delving into the specific applications of deep learning inpainting techniques
in medical imaging, it is essential to understand the broader landscape of deep learning
inpainting methods. This foundational understanding will set the stage for examining
how these techniques are tailored and applied to address the unique challenges of medical
imaging.
5.2.1 Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs)
Convolutional neural network (CNN) is a category of feed-forward neural networks
designed to learn and optimize kernel weights for feature extraction directly from input
data. Unlike traditional models that rely on manually defined filters, CNNs optimize
their kernel weights, granting flexibility and precision in capturing spatial hierarchies and
critical features essential for image recognition, segmentation, and classification tasks
[240].
A typical CNN architecture consists of three main layers, as shown in Figure 5.1:
convolutional, pooling, and fully connected layers [ 240]. The convolutional layer applies
kernels across the input data to produce feature maps that capture distinctive image char-
acteristics. Pooling layers follow, performing downsampling to reduce the dimensionality
of feature maps, which improves computational efficiency and introduces spatial invariance.
Fully connected layers then aggregate the features to generate an output, often a class
prediction in classification tasks.
83

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
Input Image
ConvolutionalLayer
PoolingLayer
FullyConnectedLayer
FeatureLearning Classification
Figure 5.1: Architecture of a convolutional neural network (CNN).
CNNs have evolved to address increasingly complex tasks like segmentation and in-
painting by incorporating transposed convolutions, which perform the reverse of typical
convolutional layers to facilitate upsampling in encoder-decoder networks (Figure 5.2).
Fully convolutional network (FCN) advance this approach by omitting the fully connected
layers, retaining spatial dimensions across the network, which is essential for dense predic-
tion tasks like segmentation and inpainting. FCNs serve as foundational architectures for
numerous inpainting models, allowing integration of multi-scale and contextual information
for coherent image reconstruction [238, 241].
Input Image
EncoderLayers
DecoderLayers
Output Image
Figure 5.2: Architecture of an Encoder-Decoder Network.
84

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
One notable model, the U-Net has been widely adopted for tasks requiring pixel-level
accuracy, such as medical imaging. Its encoder-decoder structure features symmetric
downsampling and upsampling paths with skip connections that bridge corresponding
layers, preserving high-resolution details and enhancing reconstruction accuracy in fine-
grained tasks [242].
The application of CNNs for image reconstruction emerged early with denoising
algorithms from 2012 to 2014, leading to an increase in CNN-based inpainting research [243,
244, 245]. Traditionally, inpainting requires knowledge of the locations and characteristics
of the missing pixels, often needing predefined masks. However, blind inpainting methods
represent a significant advancement by enabling the autonomous reconstruction of images
without explicit mask specifications for the occluded areas. Among the pioneering methods,
Cai et al. developed a blind inpainting technique that enabled FCNs to autonomously
learn mappings from corrupted regions to ground-truth images [246]. Yang et al. proposed
a multi-scale neural patch synthesis approach, optimizing content and texture constraints
to improve image realism [247].
Recent innovations in inpainting include Partial and Gated Convolutions, which
enhance model capacity for handling irregular masks by dynamically determining feature
importance [248]. This has proven effective in generating high-quality textures in missing
regions, yielding realistic reconstructions. Liu et al. proposed partial convolutions that
normalize only non-masked regions [ 249], while Ma et al. recently introduced a dense
gated convolutional network that modifies the gated convolutional structure to optimize
inpainting with complex inputs [250].
5.2.2 Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs)
Generative adversarial network (GAN) represent a transformative approach to genera-
tive modeling by establishing a competition between two networks: a generator, which
learns to create realistic data samples, and a discriminator, which learns to differentiate
between real and synthetic data, as shown in Figure 5.3 [ 251]. This adversarial process
drives the generator to produce increasingly realistic images, while the discriminator
continuously refines its evaluative capabilities, advancing the field of image generation
and reconstruction [251, 252].
85

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
Generator
Discriminator
Real 
or
Fake
Input Images
FakeImages
Figure 5.3: Architecture of a generative adversarial network (GAN).
Beyond basic image synthesis, GANs have been widely adopted for applications like
data augmentation, image-to-image translation, and inpainting. Pathak et al. [ 253]
pioneered GAN-based inpainting with the context encoder (CE), an encoder-decoder
model that introduced the use of adversarial training for filling in missing image regions.
Expanding on this, Yeh et al. [ 254] proposed semantic inpainting (SI), which eliminated
the need for explicit hole information, enhancing model flexibility. Building on these
advancements, Iizuka et al. [255] then developed the global and local consistency (GLCIC)
model, utilizing dual discriminators to assess global and local coherence.
Building on these foundations, Yu et al. [ 256] introduced contextual attention (CA),
an approach that enhances inpainting by leveraging the features of surrounding pixels in
the image to improve the quality of reconstructed regions. This approach allows the model
to identify and utilize relevant context from the non-missing parts of the image, enabling it
to generate more coherent and realistic inpainted areas by matching similar textures and
patterns found in the neighboring regions. Nazeri et al. [ 257] proposed EdgeConnect (EC),
a two-stage model separating structure prediction and image completion. In more recent
work, Liu et al. [ 258] presented a spatially probabilistic diversity normalization-based
GAN, generating diverse inpainting outputs with realistic variations, and Zhao et al. [ 259]
introduced a novel co-modulation approach that integrates conditional and stochastic
style representations for enhanced synthesis capabilities. Zheng et al. [ 260] proposed the
Cascaded Modulation GAN, which utilizes Fourier convolution blocks in an encoder and a
dual-stream decoder with cascaded global-spatial modulation at each scale level to better
capture multi-scale feature representations, further advancing GAN-based inpainting
capabilities.
86

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
5.2.3 Vision Transformers (ViTs)
Vision transformer (ViT) is a type of neural network that adapt transformer architec-
tures, originally developed for natural language processing, to image recognition tasks.
Introduced in 2020, ViTs marked a shift away from CNNs by relying on self-attention
mechanisms to capture dependencies across different regions of an image without relying
on convolutions [261]. In ViTs, an image is divided into smaller patches, which are linearly
embedded and processed as a sequence of tokens, as shown in Figure 5.4. This setup
allows the model to capture both local and global features simultaneously, resulting in
superior scalability and adaptability across varying image sizes and resolutions.

Figure 5.4: Architecture of a Vision Transformer and Transformer Encoder Block.
ViTs consists of a series of transformer encoder blocks (Figure 5.4), each with multi-
head self-attention and feed-forward layers. Since ViTs lack the inductive biases of CNNs,
such as locality and translational equivariance, they incorporate positional encoding for
each image patch to retain spatial information. This architecture enables ViTs to learn
long-range relationships within image regions, which can be challenging for CNNs to
capture in deeper layers [261].
In recent years, ViTs have also seen integration in image inpainting, where capturing
long-range dependencies is crucial. Deng et al. [ 262] introduced a ViT-based inpainting
model that replaces some convolutional layers with transformer blocks in an FCN. This
model uses a multi-scale, multi-head attention mechanism, enhancing the capture of
complex patterns and long-range dependencies [262].
87

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
Additional advancements include the Incremental Transformer for image inpainting by
Dong et al. [ 263], which integrates masking positional encoding for enhanced inpainting
capabilities, and the Mask-Aware Transformer proposed by Li et al. [ 264], which employs
mask-aware attention mechanisms to fill in large missing regions with high accuracy.
Recently, Campana et al. [ 265] developed a variable-hyperparameter transformer model
tailored for efficient inpainting, allowing the model to adapt hyperparameters dynamically
to improve performance across different inpainting tasks.
5.2.4 Diffusion Models
Diffusion Models are a class of generative models that have shown great promise for
high-quality image generation and restoration tasks, including inpainting. The diffusion
process consists of two primary phases, as shown in Figure 5.5. The first is the forward
(noising) phase, where noise is incrementally added to the image over multiple steps,
effectively transforming it into pure noise [ 266]. The second is the reverse (denoising)
phase, in which a neural network learns to progressively remove the noise, reconstructing
the image step by step until it regains a structured and visually coherent form [ 266].
By leveraging this approach, diffusion models provide greater stability and consistency
compared to GAN, which often struggle with mode collapse and unstable training [266].
Key innovations in diffusion models include the denoising diffusion probabilistic model
(DDPM), which optimizes training and sampling processes, making diffusion models
competitive with GANs for image quality [ 266]. Latent Diffusion Models, developed by
Rombach et al. [267], optimize diffusion processes in a compressed latent space, significantly
improving computational efficiency for inpainting applications by reconstructing images
with high fidelity from low-dimensional representations.
Diffusion models have demonstrated superior performance in image inpainting due to
their step-wise, noise-based approach, which excels in capturing fine details and texture
consistency. Lugmayr et al.’s [ 268] RePaint model leverages DDPMs for inpainting,
allowing for detailed and realistic reconstructions by gradually incorporating contextual
information from the surrounding regions. Fei et al. [ 269] introduced a generative diffusion
prior for unified image restoration and enhancement, integrating denoising and restoration
functions to enhance texture and structural consistency across a range of image types.
88

Figure 5.5: Architecture of a Diffusion Model.
Further exploration of diffusion models in image inpainting includes structured denois-
ing approaches in discrete state spaces by Austin et al. [ 270], which introduce flexibility
in generating both discrete and continuous visual data. These innovations collectively
position diffusion models as a robust framework for inpainting and high-fidelity image
reconstruction tasks, with applications extending from medical imaging to high-resolution
visual content generation.
To summarize, advances in neural network architectures have led to significant improve-
ments in inpainting. CNNs laid the groundwork with their capability for feature extraction
and dense prediction, achieving impressive results in tasks requiring spatial coherence.
GANs further advanced the field by enabling context-aware inpainting, leveraging an
adversarial setup to produce highly realistic textures. More recently, vision transformer
(ViT) and Diffusion Models have brought new techniques to image restoration, with ViTs
excelling at capturing long-range dependencies through self-attention mechanisms and
Diffusion Models generating high-fidelity images via iterative denoising. Table 5.1 presents
a comparative overview of prominent CNN, GAN, ViT, and Diffusion-based methods,
detailing their architectural approaches and primary limitations.
89

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
5.3 Deep Learning Inpainting Applications to Medical Imag-
ing
This section presents a comprehensive review that encompasses 53 articles dedicated
to deep medical image inpainting, exploring how these advanced techniques are leveraged
to address the unique challenges of medical imaging. The reviewed works integrate deep
image inpainting into various tasks within medical imaging. These techniques are classified
into three main categories:
• General-Purpose Applications: These are inpainting methods initially developed for
non-medical images but adapted for medical imaging to reconstruct missing areas.
Such adaptations aim to improve the overall quality and diagnostic completeness of
medical images by seamlessly filling in missing or occluded regions;
• Artifact-Specific Inpainting: This category includes specialized techniques designed
to remove specific artifacts, such as metal artifacts or instrument-related distortions,
which can obscure critical diagnostic information. By focusing on artifact removal,
these methods aim to enhance image clarity and diagnostic accuracy by isolating
and addressing imaging artifacts in medical datasets;
• Inpainting in Multi-Task Frameworks: In this approach, inpainting is combined
with complementary tasks such as segmentation, classification, and data augmen-
tation. These multi-task strategies use inpainting as a foundational technique to
support broader goals, such as improving model robustness and training accuracy
by generating complete datasets or enhancing specific imaging characteristics for
better downstream analysis.
In the following sections, the results and findings of each category are presented.
5.3.1 General-Purpose Inpainting Techniques
General-purpose applications of deep image inpainting in medical imaging center
around the broad objective of filling in missing areas. Ten studies have explored these
techniques in detail, and a summary is provided in Table 5.2.
91

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
Generative adversarial network (GAN) emerge as the predominant deep learning
approach used. These studies primarily aimed at implementing or enhancing GAN-based
approaches, with context encoder (CE) [253], semantic inpainting (SI) [254], and contextual
attention (CA) [256] serving as key baselines. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) and
computer tomography (CT) stand out as the most popular imaging modalities due to
their clinical relevance and complexity.
Two primary limitations in these studies warrant further examination: reliance on
2D images and constraints imposed by the mask use. Sole reliance on 2D images poses a
notable drawback, especially considering that many medical images are obtained in 3D
volumes. Of the reviewed works, 8 out of 10 specifically address CT and MRI images
captured in 3D volumes; however, 6 out of 10 process these images exclusively in a
2D format. The constraint of fixed central masks further limits the versatility of these
techniques. However, recent advancements have begun to mitigate these limitations by
enabling the use of irregular masks. Remarkably, six works from the reviewed studies
have already implemented such irregular masks.
Direct comparison of evaluation metrics across these studies remains challenging due
to differences in imaging modalities, inpainting regions, and evaluation criteria. Table
5.3 provides a structured comparison of these studies, presenting the performance of the
proposed methods alongside relevant comparison deep learning techniques.

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
Each study’s performance is assessed by focusing on key metrics like SSIM and
PSNR to highlight the strengths and limitations of general-purpose inpainting techniques.
Techniques often used for benchmarking include GLCIC [ 255], EdgeConnect [257], Partial
Convolution [249], and Gated Convolution [ 279]. Recently, specialized methods, such as
MedGAN [196] and ResViT [200], have been developed specifically for medical imaging.
In brain MRI studies, varied experimental setups complicate the identification of
a universally superior approach. However, Armanious et al. [ 181] demonstrated that
their enhanced MedGAN model outperformed traditional techniques, while Zhu et al.
[277] reported that pGAN achieved better results than ResViT. For lung CT studies,
more standardized experimental conditions allow for clearer conclusions. In these studies,
ipA-MedGAN from Armanious et al. [ 182] achieves the highest SSIM, while Tran et al.
[275] reports superior PSNR with their method.
5.3.2 Artifact-Specific Applications of Inpainting Techniques
Artifact-Specific applications of deep image inpainting in medical imaging are dedicated
to inpaint areas corrupted by artifacts. In total, 21 studies have investigated inpainting
techniques targeting image artifacts, spanning diverse imaging modalities and anatomical
regions. The summary of these techniques is presented in Table 5.4.
The main artifacts addressed in the reviewed works encompass metal artifacts, mark-
ers/annotations, and cropped artifacts. Metal artifacts commonly occur in X-rays, CT
scans, and MRI images due to the presence of metal implants. These artifacts introduce
noise and hinder the clarity of medical images. Markers and annotations are typically
added to images to delineate regions of interest and convey pertinent information, yet
they pose challenges for segmentation and classification algorithms. Cropped artifacts
arise when sections of the body are inadvertently excluded during image acquisition of
CT and MRI.
The primary architectures employed for artifact inpainting are U-Net and GAN. GANs
exhibit superior performance in producing high-quality inpainted medical images. On
the other hand, U-Net architectures excel in image segmentation tasks, making them
invaluable for identifying artifact locations and subsequently removing and inpainting
them.
94

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
CT, MRI, and ultrasound (US) are the most prevalent imaging modalities, predom-
inantly due to the prevalence of metal and marker/annotation artifacts. Endoscopic
images also hold significance due to the occurrence of a distinct artifact type known
as specular reflection. A significant limitation of these works pertains to defining the
areas requiring inpainting. This challenge can be addressed through three approaches:
1) leveraging existing information about the affected regions, namely by incorporating
human-in-the-loop techniques and expert feedback [ 20, 300]; 2) employing an additional
network to detect or segment artifacts [ 301, 302]; or 3) implementing blind inpainting
techniques capable of autonomously identifying and inpainting structures without prior
information.
Table 5.5 provides a comparative analysis of similar artifact-specific inpainting studies,
organized by artifact and imaging type, with a focus on commonly used evaluation metrics
such as MSE, PSNR, and SSIM.
A notable observation is the significant variation in PSNR values across studies,
which can largely be attributed to differences in datasets. PSNR is highly sensitive to
image quality, noise levels, and resolution, so it is most effective when comparing images
that share similar properties. As such, differences in datasets, even for similar imaging
types, can lead to PSNR variability across studies. Similar to general-purpose inpainting
techniques, Partial Convolution [249] and Gated Convolution [ 279] are widely adopted
across studies. In contrast, methods like GLCIC [ 255] and CA [ 256] are primarily used in
studies focused on marker removal.
For knee CT images, the method proposed by Gottschalk et al. [ 280] outperformed
other techniques in terms of MSE, while the method proposed by Agrawal et al. [ 283]
using Gated Convolution achieved higher PSNR values. In head CT images, both proposed
methods demonstrated good results, indicating effective artifact handling. In head MRI
studies focused on dental implant removal, Xie et al. [ 285] achieved the best SSIM values,
outperforming other approaches. Similarly, in marker removal for thyroid US, Li et al.
[291] reported the highest PSNR values. However, in ovarian US experiments, performance
metrics were lower overall due to the larger artifact regions, which require more extensive
reconstruction.
97

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
5.3.3 Inpainting in Multi-Task Frameworks
Inpainting has emerged as a promising technique for recovering missing information in
corrupted images. However, researchers soon recognized that this technique is suitable for
multiple tasks. In total, 21 reviewed works focused on inpainting in various tasks and a
summary is presented in Table 5.6.
Image inpainting has become increasingly relevant across various tasks, including
segmentation, augmentation, registration, and classification. Its versatility extends to
artifact removal, dataset augmentation, and the targeted elimination of specific regions of
interest, making it a valuable tool for enhancing the performance of multi-task approaches.
As a result, this technique is employed as a pre-processing step, integrated within multi-step
pipelines, or incorporated directly into task architectures [303, 304, 305].
In segmentation tasks, the most common application with 9 works, the importance
of image inpainting lies in its ability to pinpoint abnormal structures within images.
When the inpainting algorithm struggles to reconstruct a particular area, the higher
reconstruction loss signals the potential presence of a new area of interest, aiding in the
precise localization of lesions and tumors.
For augmentation purposes, inpainting offers the flexibility to subtly alter existing
areas or introduce new elements, thereby enriching and diversifying datasets. Additionally,
in registration tasks, inpainting facilitates the precise alignment and placement of different
images, ensuring they share consistent orientation and structure for accurate analysis and
comparison.
Among the various used architectures for multi-task applications, U-Net emerges as
the most prominent choice. Its proficiency in identifying and localizing regions of interest
in medical imaging makes it particularly well-suited for segmentation and augmentation.
MRI, X-ray, and US stand out as the most prevalent imaging modalities, primarily
due to their relevance in segmentation, augmentation, and registration tasks, underscoring
their widespread adoption in medical imaging research and applications.
99

The main metrics used to evaluate segmentation performance include dice similarity
coefficient (DICE) and intersection over union (IoU), while registration tasks typically
rely on structural similarity index measure (SSIM) and peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR)
to assess quality, similar to inpainting evaluations.
For brain MRI lesion segmentation, Manj´ on et al. [307] achieved the highest DICE
scores, indicating superior performance in accurately delineating lesion areas. In con-
trast, for COVID-19 lesion segmentation, Fung et al. [ 305] reported notably low results,
highlighting the challenges faced by these approaches in this specific task. On the other
hand, Lu et al. [ 310] achieved favorable results, suggesting more effective segmentation in
COVID-19 cases.
In registration tasks, the analyzed studies represent a continuation of prior work,
utilizing the same dataset and comparison models across studies. This continuity allows
for a direct performance comparison, showing that Xing et al. [ 322] outperformed previous
models, demonstrating advancements in registration accuracy.
102

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
5.4 Discussion
Deep learning has significantly impacted medical imaging inpainting, addressing various
trends and critical issues as outlined in the reviewed works. The main tendencies identified
in the reviewed works are illustrated in Figure 5.6.
0
5

15
20
MRICTX-rayUSMRI +
CT

(a)

10

30
GAN U-Net CNN
General Artifact-Specific Multi-Task (b)
Figure 5.6: Comparative Images of the Tendencies of the Reviewed Works according to a)
Imaging Modality and b) Architecture.
Our comprehensive literature review reveals that deep image inpainting is predomi-
nantly utilized in magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) and computer tomography (CT).
MRI and CT are extensively used in diagnosing numerous diseases and are particularly
amenable to improvements through image inpainting to mitigate artifacts. Addition-
ally, other modalities such as X-ray, ultrasound (US), and combinations of MRI with
CT are extensively researched. Endoscopy, positron emission tomography (PET), and
optical coherence tomography also benefit from inpainting techniques, particularly in
artifact-specific and multi-task scenarios.
Generative adversarial network (GAN) have become the leading choice for general-
purpose inpainting due to their ability to generate realistic images through the interplay
of a generator and a discriminator. This capability is particularly effective in scenarios
where inpainting masks are predefined and well-defined. However, GANs face challenges
in reliably identifying areas of interest, as they lack strong mechanisms for detecting and
segmenting specific artifacts. In contrast, U-Net architectures, with their characteristic
U-shaped design and skip connections, are specifically structured to capture fine spatial
features and contextual information. This configuration allows U-Nets to identify and
localize regions requiring inpainting, making them particularly valuable for artifact-specific
tasks where precise detection is essential and manually defining a mask is time-intensive.
U-Nets are therefore highly effective for blind inpainting applications, where they can
103

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
dynamically reconstruct image regions without relying on predefined masks. Their
strength in segmentation also makes U-Nets a preferred choice for multi-task applications
that combine inpainting with other tasks like segmentation or classification. Together,
GANs and U-Nets offer complementary approaches: GANs bring powerful image synthesis
capabilities, while U-Nets provide targeted artifact detection and context-aware inpainting.
Metal artifacts are the most commonly encountered issue across various medical
imaging modalities, primarily caused by metal implants, leading to star-streaking artifacts
that affect multiple areas of the image. Other common artifacts include cropped images
and the presence of markers and annotations that limit full image assessment. Additionally,
specific artifacts related to image acquisition and imaging modalities are also identified.
Segmentation is the most prevalent application in multi-tasking, followed by image
augmentation, classification, and registration.
Regarding datasets, the majority of reviewed articles utilize public datasets, with 62%
relying on open-source data. Despite this, there is minimal overlap in datasets used, and
few works provide public access to their code. These factors pose challenges for comparison
and full replication of studies. The need for larger, labeled datasets in medical imaging
is critical for developing more complex algorithms, particularly in 3D and 4D imaging,
where dataset availability is limited.
Most studies focus on 2D medical imaging, with only 6 works addressing 3D and 4D
imaging. Inpainting 3D and 4D images presents significant challenges due to the higher
computational power required and the complexity of maintaining spatial consistency across
all dimensions. The higher dimensionality exponentially increases the information to be
analyzed, necessitating more sophisticated and resource-intensive models. The limited
availability of high-quality, annotated 3D datasets further complicates the training and
validation of these models. These challenges underscore the need for advanced techniques
and infrastructure to effectively address 3D and 4D inpainting.
Evaluation of results in the reviewed works primarily employs three types of metrics:
error metrics, structural metrics, and noise-based metrics. mean squared error (MSE) and
mean absolute error (MAE) are the most frequently used error metrics, assessing pixel value
differences between original and reconstructed images. The structural similarity index
measure (SSIM) is the most commonly used structural metric, evaluating the structural
similarity between original and reconstructed images. Lastly, the peak signal-to-noise ratio
(PSNR) is widely used to analyze noise presence. These metrics provide a comprehensive
and robust basis for performance evaluation and comparison between different algorithms.
104

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
5.5 Open Challenges, Recommendations, and Future Direc-
tions
The open challenges identified throughout this chapter are mainly focused on the mask
defined for inpainting, the variety of medical imaging modalities, the artifacts present in
medical imaging, and the various types of deep learning architecture. In the following
subsections, these open challenges are discussed in further detail to explain the current
open issues, describe possible methodological improvements, and discuss specific medical
applications or use cases.
5.5.1 Mask for Inpainting
The accurate definition of the inpainting area is crucial for the success of this technique.
Randomly inpainting an image without properly identifying the targeted regions often
leads to inadequate results. Since not all methods can automatically detect artifacts,
manual segmentation or a pre-segmentation step is often necessary to identify the regions
requiring inpainting.
Future research should focus on developing techniques capable of autonomously detect-
ing and inpainting these regions in medical images without requiring additional information.
Techniques such as those proposed by Li et al. [ 289] and Manj´ on et al. [307], which
demonstrate the potential to autonomously inpaint regions, reducing human intervention
and algorithmic complexity, are promising.
Enhanced blind inpainting methods would significantly benefit imaging modalities like
MRI and CT scans where motion artifacts or metal-induced distortions are prevalent.
These artifacts dominate the image and may cause errors in certain anatomical structures
that are essential for diagnosis accuracy. The manual delimitation of these artifacts is
a time-consuming process and the inclusion of a previous segmentation algorithm leads
to the increase of computational power. Automated systems could improve workflow
efficiency and diagnostic accuracy by minimizing the need for manual intervention.
5.5.2 Medical Imaging Modalities
Medical imaging encompasses various modalities, each with unique characteristics and
limitations. Artifacts are closely linked to the inherent characteristics and structures of
the images. Although medical images can exist in 2D, 3D, or even 4D formats, most
105

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
techniques have primarily focused on 2D inpainting, which limits analysis, particularly in
CT, PET, and MRI images.
Efforts should be directed toward developing techniques that can accommodate the
original and complete format of 3D and 4D medical images without sacrificing compu-
tational efficiency. This might involve leveraging advanced neural network architectures
designed for volumetric data processing.
Techniques that handle 3D and 4D imaging formats would be beneficial in areas like
neuroimaging and oncology, where accurate and precise reconstruction from MRI or CT
scans is critical for diagnosis and treatment planning.
5.5.3 Artifacts in Medical Imaging
Artifacts in medical imaging are intricately linked to the specific modality being used.
Common artifacts such as metal, cropping, and markers have been extensively studied.
However, research efforts have primarily focused on addressing one artifact at a time,
leading to a gap in addressing scenarios where multiple artifacts coexist within the same
image. Moreover, less common artifacts such as reconstruction artifacts and guidewire
interference have received limited attention, highlighting the need for further research and
development in this area.
Future research should explore multi-task learning frameworks and advanced neural
network architectures that can handle complex scenarios where multiple artifacts coexist
within the same image.
Techniques addressing multiple artifacts would be valuable in clinical scenarios involving
implants and devices, such as orthopedic imaging or interventional radiology, where various
artifacts can compromise image quality.
5.5.4 Deep Learning Architectures
Numerous deep learning architectures and approaches have been analyzed, with GANs
and U-Nets emerging as the most prominent frameworks. While GANs excel in creating
authentic images, U-Net’s strength lies in localizing and inpainting regions of interest.
However, these techniques require enhancements to make them more suitable for medical
imaging applications.
Key areas for improvement include refining the definition of inpainting areas, exploring
alternative convolution techniques such as partial and gated convolutions, integrating
various loss functions beyond reconstruction loss, and incorporating texture information
106

Chapter 5. The Role of Deep Learning in Medical Image Inpainting: A Systematic
Review
to enhance performance. Additionally, the combination of GAN and U-Net architectures
could lead to an overall improvement in the implementation of blind inpainting.
Improved deep learning architectures could revolutionize areas such as neuroimaging,
where high-fidelity reconstruction of brain structures is essential for diagnosing neurological
disorders, and cardiology, where precise imaging is critical for assessing cardiac function
and planning interventions.
5.6 Conclusion
This chapter offers a comprehensive review aimed at assessing the evolution, impact,
and limitations of deep learning in medical image inpainting, while also providing rec-
ommendations and addressing open challenges. A total of 53 studies were meticulously
selected from extensive searches across four databases, using a combination of keywords
including “deep learning”, “inpainting” and “medical imaging”.
In conclusion, image inpainting emerges as a versatile and potent technique with
diverse applications in medical imaging. The findings underscore the significance of image
inpainting in artifact removal and improving the performance of multi-task approaches,
owing to its crucial role in localizing and inpainting regions of interest. Moreover, the
preference for architectures like GANs and U-Net highlights the importance of selecting
suitable tools for multi-task applications. This preference is supported by GANs’ ability
to generate realistic images and U-Net’s proficiency in localizing and inpainting regions
of interest. Additionally, the prevalence of imaging modalities such as MRI and CT
emphasizes their indispensable role in medical imaging research. Overall, image inpainting
stands as a pivotal technique in the domain of medical imaging, offering promising avenues
for further advancements and applications in the field.
Future work stretches along four main avenues: (i) Development of blind inpainting
techniques, (ii) Investigation of methods suitable for processing 3D and 4D medical
images, (iii) Exploration of approaches capable of addressing multiple artifacts and multi-
task applications, and (iv) Enhancement of deep learning architectures through the
exploration of alternative convolution techniques, integration of diverse loss functions, and
incorporation of texture information to improve performance.
107

Chapter 6
Innovative Approaches in PET/CT
Analysis
This chapter builds upon the previous discussion on image inpainting, extending their
application to PET/CT analysis and CT image restoration in cancer diagnostics. By
addressing challenges in both functional and structural imaging, the approaches presented
here explore how innovative techniques can enhance diagnostic accuracy and clinical
decision-making in breast cancer (BC) and lung cancer (LC). Section 6.1 examines the role
of PET/CT in detecting bone metastases in BC patients, evaluating its diagnostic efficacy
compared to bone scintigraphy (BS). This approach aims to determine whether PET/CT
can replace BS in detecting bone metastases, while also helping us develop a deeper
understanding of image sensitivity and workflow that is valuable for future imputation
applications. Section 6.2 focuses on lung CT imaging, investigating the performance of
imputation techniques reviewed in the previous chapter. This approach seeks to improve
the accuracy and reliability of diagnostics of LC, particularly in clinical scenarios where
artifacts may compromise the quality of lung tissue imaging or its surrounding structures.
Together, these studies provide valuable insights into the complementary roles of PET/CT
and the potential of imputation techniques to address limitations in imaging quality.
108

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis
6.1 Comparing PET/CT and Bone Scintigraphy for Bone
Metastases Detection in Breast Cancer Patients
BC is the most commonly diagnosed cancer and the leading cause of cancer death
among women [ 7, 16]. Distant metastases represent the main cause of death and are
common in advanced stages of the disease [ 325]. Bone is the main site of metastasis in
patients with BC, accounting for 20% of the distant metastasis [ 326]. Bone metastasis
are classified as osteolytic, osteoblastic or mixed, and are associated with considerable
morbidity including pain, impaired mobility, hypercalcemia, pathological fracture, and
bone marrow infiltration [ 327, 328]. Therefore, early detection of skeletal metastasis is
essential for the management of the disease and to define staging and optimal treatment
[329].
Imaging plays a key role in the diagnosis of bone metastasis in BC, in which bone
scintigraphy (BS) remains the most used modality [ 330]. This conventional imaging
method provides information on osteoblastic activity and skeletal vascularity, and presents
preferential uptake of tracer at sites of active bone formation [ 331]. Even though this
technique remains popular among clinicians, it still has some limitations, namely low
specificity [332].
Positron emission tomography/computer tomography (PET/CT) has been shown
to obtain improved sensitivity and specificity when compared to conventional imaging
modalities [326]. It detects the presence of cancer cells directly by quantifying metabolic
activity, which allows the analysis of active tumour tissue in the whole body [331].
BS in combination with CT is considered the gold standard for BC staging. However,
the use and relevance of PET/CT in this context have increased in recent years [ 333].
Even though BS and PET/CT have been applied to the detection of bone metastasis, no
consensus has been established on the most suitable imaging modality for this purpose.
International Guidelines recommend PET/CT for staging in patients with locally advanced
disease and inflammatory carcinomas [ 334, 335] and the National Comprehensive Cancer
Network guidelines suggest that BS might be omitted in certain cases when PET/CT
documents bone metastases [334].
In this section, a prospective analysis of 410 female patients treated in Portuguese
Oncology Institute of Porto (IPO-Porto) between 2014 and 2020 is performed to compare
the effectiveness of bone metastasis detection of PET/CT and BS in patients with BC,
aiming to understand the cases where the latter can be avoided. The image analysis was
performed by 2 senior nuclear medicine physicians. The comparison was performed based
109

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis
on accuracy, sensitivity, and specificity on a patient and anatomical region level and was
assessed using McNemar’s Test. An average ROC was calculated for the anatomical region
analysis.
6.1.1 Research Methodology
This study prospectively included patients diagnosed with BC who underwent PET/CT
and BS for staging purposes at the IPO-Porto between 2014 and 2020. Only patients who
underwent both imaging exams within an interval of less than two months (average: 16 ±
14 days, range: 0–60 days) were considered [ 336]. The study was approved by the local
Ethics and Data Protection Committees, and clinical and pathological data were retrieved
from electronic patient records by a medical oncologist.
The BS and PET/CT images were routinely evaluated by 2 senior nuclear medicine
physicians. Consistent readings between BS and PET/CT were considered true positives.
In cases where there was a discrepancy between the 2 modalities, a re-analysis of the
images by other 2 independent nuclear medicine physicians was performed to validate
the findings. Clinical and pathological information of the patients and the disease was
collected from electronic files by a medical oncologist.
A manual comparison of BS and PET/CT results was conducted by reviewing electronic
patient records and identifying which imaging modality successfully diagnosed each
metastasis. This evaluation allowed for a detailed assessment of the lesions detected
by each technique, ensuring a direct comparison of their diagnostic capabilities. For
evaluation purposes, the comparison of the ability of BS and PET/CT to identify bone
disease was performed based on accuracy, sensitivity, and specificity, at a patient and
anatomical region level. In the anatomical region-based analysis, the skeleton was divided
into 5 regions composed of 10 bone segments: cranium, upper limb (scapulae, clavicle and
humerus), spine, thorax (rib cage and sternum) and lower limb (pelvis and sacrum, femur,
tibia and fibula).
The bone metastases distribution was categorized depending on the number of bone
lesions. Accordingly, 3 categories were considered: “isolated metastasis” (only one lesion),
“less than 5 bone lesions” (between 2 and 5 lesions), and “more than 5 lesions”.
To compare the effectiveness (accuracy, sensitivity and specificity) of bone metastasis
detection of PET/CT and BS, the McNemar’s Test was used [ 337, 338]. A p-value < 0.05
was considered statistically significant.
110

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis
An average Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (ROC) curve and the corresponding
Area Under the Curve (AUC) were calculated for the anatomical region analysis. The
ROC curve of each bone segment was combined to obtain a single ROC curve to study
the bone disease detection.
6.1.2 Analysis of Bone Metastases Detection
Distant metastases were identified in 112/410 (27.3%) patients. Bone metastasis was
observed in 81/112 patients (72.3%) and was the only distant metastasis site for 40/81
patients, representing 49.8% of all the patients with bone disease.
6.1.2.1 Patient-based Analysis
The results of the patient-based analysis are described in Table 6.1. In total, PET/CT
misdiagnosed 5/81 patients (6.2%) and BS misdiagnosed 15/81 patients (18.5%). In
the case of PET/CT, 1 patient was misdiagnosed due to the non-detection of a cranium
metastasis, whereas the other 4 patients presented bone lesions that did not show metabolic
activity (whose presence was confirmed in BS). In the case of BS, the exam was not
capable of diagnosing an isolated metastasis in 7 patients, was not capable of identifying
various metastases in 4 patients, and was not capable of identifying diffuse bone disease
in 4 patients. Consequently, PET/CT obtained an accuracy of 98.05%, surpassing the
performance of BS (95.61%).

6.1.2.2 Anatomical Region-based Analysis
The bone metastasis distribution according to the pre-defined categories was as follows:
16 patients with isolated metastasis (19.8%), 26 patients with less than five lesions (32.1%),
and 39 patients with more than five lesions (48.1%).

113

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis
6.1.3 Clinical Impact and Considerations
Staging of early patients with BC with increased risk for metastases is essential to
define prognosis and establish the best therapeutical approach. International guidelines
advocate performing thoracoabdominal CT as well as BS as part of initial BC staging
[334, 335, 339]. Since bone is the main local of metastases, present in almost 50%–70% of
the patients with advanced disease [ 340, 341], procedures must have a high sensitivity to
identify this pathology since its early detection could also help to minimize skeletal-related
effects that decrease patients’ quality of life [342].
For many years, it has been established that osteolytic lesions are not identified in BS
unless the bone marrow already has a huge destruction [ 343]. As a result, hybrid imaging
techniques that associate anatomical and metabolic imaging (e.g., PET/CT) could be
beneficial [344, 345, 346].
Our study raises the question of whether BS is still necessary for BC bone staging
when PET/CT is available. To our knowledge, this is the biggest prospective study that
addresses this question, stratifying the analysis by patient and anatomical region.
Even though PET/CT presents the best overall effect in the bone metastases detection,
the low performance in the detection of cranium lesions requires further analysis. This
issue may be caused by the imaging protocol of PET/CT, which does not require the
imaging of these segments. Accordingly, this modification of the image acquisition protocol
could be enough to solve this handicap.
In a meta-analysis with 13 articles [ 342], PET/CT seems equal to BS for diagnosis
of bone metastases in patients with BC, considering a per-patient basis. However, on a
per-lesion basis, PET/CT had lower sensitivity and higher specificity than BS. In this
analysis, the authors stated that due to limitations such as the small number of datasets
available for PET/CT, and the small sample size of included studies, it was not possible
to devise standard recommendations for clinical practice or future research. Particularly
in patients with BC with bone disease, PET/CT performed at baseline (staging), could
also be important to predict overall survival, being able to define a subgroup of patients
that will live longer [345].
In a small survey of 7 patients with BC, where 41 bones were analysed [ 347], BS
has shown limited sensitivity in the detection of metastases (between 28.6% and 36.6%).
Although PET/CT was not used, positron emission tomography/magnetic resonance
imaging (PET/MRI) and magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) have obtained high sensitivity
results (100%).
114

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis
The comparison of PET/CT and MRI has conflicting results [ 348, 349, 350], with both
techniques generally showing a good performance. PET/MRI is not transversely available,
but the idea to add functional information is very attractive, and is being tested in 2
clinical trials to support BC staging [351].
6.2 Comparative Analysis of Data Imputation Methods for
CT Lung Imaging
Chest CT plays a central role in LC detection, particularly for non-small cell lung
cancer (NSCLC) - the most prevalent form of the disease. Accurate tumor localization,
lymph node assessment, and metastasis detection all depend on high-resolution CT scans.
However, the complexity of CT image acquisition introduces artifacts and missing data,
which can impact diagnostic accuracy, segmentation, and classification tasks [ 14, 20, 352].
To mitigate these issues, image imputation and inpainting techniques have emerged as
promising solutions, aiming to reconstruct missing or degraded regions, thereby reducing
the need for repeat scans [ 15]. As discussed in Chapter 5, several deep learning-based
imputation methods have been explored as benchmark techniques to perform image
inpainting. These include context encoder (CE) [ 253] , global and local consistency
(GLCIC) [255], contextual attention (CA) [ 256], and EdgeConnect (EC) [ 257] . These
models have been extensively tested across various imaging applications, yet they were
not specifically designed for medical imaging.
In this section, we extend the analysis by evaluating these approaches alongside edge
and structure information for medical image inpainting (ESMII) [ 227], a method explicitly
developed for medical imaging, to assess whether its specialized architecture provides
superior reconstruction performance in lung CT imaging. These imputation models
are compared on 5,350 chest CT slices from NSCLC patients, focusing on lung tissue
reconstruction. Performance was assessed at three levels: overall, tissue-specific and
tumour-specific reconstruction. To ensure a robust evaluation, we employed multiple
quantitative metrics, including mean absolute error (MAE), structural similarity index
measure (SSIM), peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR), fr´ echet inception distance (FID) and
dice similarity coefficient (DICE) for tumour segmentation. Through this analysis, we aim
to determine whether ESMII’s specialized approach offers a significant advantage over
conventional imputation methods, ultimately contributing to more accurate and reliable
CT image reconstructions for clinical applications.
115

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis
6.2.1 Experimental Design for Lung CT Imputation
The benchmark models selected include two adapted CE variants, designed for local
and global discrimination [ 253], alongside GLCIC [ 255], CA [256] and EC [ 257]. These
models serve as reference points for evaluating imputation effectiveness in lung CT images.
Additionally, the ESMII [ 227] model is introduced as a deep learning-based inpainting
method specifically designed for medical imaging, leveraging multi-scale feature extraction
and edge-aware reconstruction to enhance structural consistency in medical images. All
selected methods were introduced in Chapter 5.
The dataset used is sourced from the Medical Segmentation Decathlon (MSD) challenge
[353] and consists of 31 NSCLC patient 3D CT scans. Since the primary objective of
this experiment is to assess inpainting performance on lung tissue, a lung identification
method is employed to extract only the CT slices containing pulmonary structures. A
total of 5,350 slices meeting this criterion are selected for training and evaluation.
To standardize pixel intensity values, the raw Hounsfield Units are normalized to a
[0, 1] scale using a window range of [-600, 1000], capturing both healthy thoracic tissue
and cancerous regions. Additionally, all images are resized to 128 ×128 pixels to ensure
compatibility across models and maintain computational efficiency.
To generate missing data, square masks are used to introduce 10%, 20%, 30% and 40%
missing data within the pulmonary region of interest (ROI). To ensure that the missing
areas primarily affect lung tissue, at least 60% of the masked region was constrained
within the pulmonary ROI. The pulmonary ROI was obtained using a threshold-based
segmentation algorithm, ensuring that masked areas were distributed across diverse lung
regions while avoiding overfitting to larger medial lung cavities. The mask generation
process is illustrated in Figure 6.2.
To ensure a robust and fair comparison between models, all models underwent 10-fold
cross-validation, providing a rigorous evaluation framework and training was conducted
for 100 epochs with a batch size of 8, optimizing for stability and generalizability across
different lung regions.
The effectiveness of the imputation models is assessed at three levels: overall level –
evaluates the reconstruction quality across all missing pixels in the image; tissue level –
separates healthy and cancerous tissues to assess how well each region is reconstructed;
tumor level – specifically analyzes the quality of tumor reconstructions, ensuring accurate
inpainting of oncological structures.
116

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis

The performance is evaluated using the metrics MAE, SSIM, PSNR, FID, and DICE.
While MAE, SSIM, PSNR assess the direct outputs of the method, we aimed to extend our
evaluation for a more comprehensive analysis. Therefore, we incorporated FID to measure
the realism of the reconstructed images and their textures, as well as DICE to quantify the
overlap between the reconstructed tumor regions and the ground truth, ensuring precise
tumor delineation. Statistical significance is obtained using the Independent T-Test and
Mann-Whitney U Test at a p-value = 0.05.
6.2.2 Assessing the Performance of Imputation Techniques
The visual results, illustrated in Figure 6.3, reveal distinct variations in model per-
formance across different anatomical structures. Both CE and GLCIC struggled with
extra-pulmonary structures, resulting in noticeable pixelation and artifacts that affected
the clarity of reconstructed regions. CA demonstrated a strong capability in capturing
vascular textures with high fidelity; however, minor inconsistencies in intensity matching
were observed, leading to subtle but visible discontinuities in certain areas. EC exhibited
notable strength in distinguishing hyperdense regions, successfully reconstructing high-
contrast features. However, it encountered some challenges in accurately representing bone
structures, occasionally producing less-defined reconstructions. Among all the models,
ESMII achieved the most visually realistic and structurally coherent reconstructions,
117

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis
excelling in both fine-detail preservation and overall image integrity. These findings em-
phasize the varying strengths and limitations of different approaches in handling complex
medical imaging tasks.

GLCIC and ESMII exhibited the strongest overall performance, with ESMII emerging as
the most effective model across all measured criteria. ESMII consistently demonstrated
robustness in handling diverse reconstruction challenges, maintaining high accuracy and
structural fidelity. Notably, it produced the most visually realistic outputs, as reflected in its
superior FID scores, indicating enhanced perceptual quality. Meanwhile, EC demonstrated
particular strengths in reconstructing intricate features, especially carcinogenic lesions,
underscoring the potential of specialized models for targeted medical imaging applications.

the need for advanced modelling techniques tailored specifically to the complexities of
