Interestingly, the observed difficulties in tumour reconstruction at extreme missing
data rates highlight the crucial role of surrounding tissue context in effective lesion
imputation. When critical anatomical cues were absent, models struggled to generate
plausible reconstructions, leading to increased inconsistencies. Additionally, some models
exhibited suboptimal performance even at lower missing data rates. This was likely due to
their tendency to prioritize global image coherence over the accurate reconstruction of finer
details within smaller missing regions. Such behavior suggests that while these models
excel in preserving overall structural integrity, they may overlook crucial lesion-specific
characteristics necessary for precise medical imaging applications.
121

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis
6.3 Conclusion
In this chapter, we addressed two innovative approaches in analysis of PET/CT for
cancer diagnostics.
The first approach demonstrated that PET/CT achieved superior accuracy and sensi-
tivity (98.0% and 93.83%, respectively) compared to BS, which recorded 95.61% accuracy
and 81.48% sensitivity. A significant advantage was observed in favor of PET/CT in terms
of sensitivity (93.83% vs. 81.48%), while specificity remained similar (99.09%). Across
most bone segments, PET/CT exhibited the highest accuracy and sensitivity, outperform-
ing BS except in the cranium. Notably, significant differences favoring PET/CT were
observed in the upper limb, spine, thorax (sternum), and lower limb (pelvis and sacrum),
whereas BS excelled only in the cranium. The ROC analysis further confirmed that
PET/CT provides higher sensitivity and consistency across bone segments, reinforcing its
suitability as the primary imaging technique for bone metastases detection in BC patients.
Given these findings, PET/CT eliminates the necessity for BS in BC staging when the
correct imaging protocol is followed.
The second approach evaluated five learning-based imputation models for reconstructing
missing or corrupted regions in lung CT images. Among these, ESMII demonstrated
superior overall performance in structural and textural reconstruction, achieving PSNR
values of 38.092 and 33.525 dB and FID scores of 7.777 and 13.049 for 20% and 40%
missing data, respectively. These results highlight ESMII’s capacity to preserve structural
and textural details. Additionally, EC excelled in tumor-specific reconstruction, as
evidenced by its higher DICE scores, reflecting improved overlap between original and
reconstructed tumor regions. The results emphasize that different models exhibit unique
strengths, suggesting that tailored approaches could enhance clinical utility based on
specific anatomical or pathological requirements. Despite these advancements, tumor
reconstruction remains a challenging task, necessitating further research to enhance models’
capability in handling complex tissue structures and missing patterns.
Future research should focus on improving the adaptability of these models for other
anatomical structures, integrating domain knowledge from medical experts, and exploring
hybrid approaches that combine multiple imputation strategies. These enhancements
could refine reconstruction accuracy and ensure broader applicability across diverse clinical
settings, ultimately reducing the need for repeat scans and improving diagnostic reliability
in CT imaging.
122

Chapter 6. Innovative Approaches in PET/CT Analysis
A critical insight gained from this chapter is that 2D imputation approaches are
no longer sufficient for PET/CT imaging. Given that CT imaging inherently captures
3D structural information, future work should focus on 3D imputation techniques that
leverage volumetric data. Exploring 3D-based models could significantly enhance the
quality of PET/CT reconstructions, leading to more accurate clinical interpretations and
better patient outcomes.
Furthermore, given the limitations imposed by publicly available datasets — partic-
ularly the underrepresentation of tumor lesions — future studies should aim to create
more comprehensive datasets that facilitate robust evaluations of imputation models.
Investigating how artificial intelligence can aid in synthesizing missing tumor data and
assessing model performance in specific lesion types and anatomical regions will be crucial
for advancing the field.
By addressing these challenges, future research can contribute to the development
of more reliable imaging methodologies, enhancing diagnostic precision and ultimately
improving patient care.
123

Chapter 7
Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes:
Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
After examining the application of imputation techniques in 2D computer tomography
(CT) imaging, this chapter expands the focus to 3D image reconstruction, exploring how
deep learning can enhance volumetric medical imaging. This chapter explores advanced
deep learning-based imputation techniques for 3D medical imaging, moving beyond
methods for filling missing image regions. The focus changes to reconstructing full 3D
CT volumes from X-rays, leveraging state-of-the-art deep learning models for volumetric
reconstruction. To achieve this, two cutting-edge methodologies are explored, each selected
for its unique strengths in volumetric reconstruction. Section 7.1 investigates neural
radiance fields (NeRF) to reconstruct comprehensive 3D models of the human body from
X-rays, capitalizing on their ability to generate detailed volumetric representations from
sparse data. Section 7.2 examines convolutional neural network (CNN) for reconstructing
high-resolution 3D CT volumes from a limited number of X-rays, emphasizing precision
and clinical applicability. By integrating these approaches, this chapter demonstrates the
transformative potential of deep learning in 3D medical imaging, paving the way for safer,
faster, and more accurate diagnostic techniques.
124

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
7.1 Reimagining X-Ray Imaging: Neural Radiance Fields
for Enhanced Reconstruction
Medical imaging has been a cornerstone of modern healthcare, enabling accurate
diagnosis and effective treatment across a wide range of medical fields. Techniques such as
CT and magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) are indispensable in clinical practice, providing
clinicians with detailed, high-resolution 3D representations of anatomical structures.
However, acquiring these high-quality 3D images often requires multiple scan acquisitions,
leading to prolonged imaging sessions. These extended sessions not only increase the
likelihood of patient discomfort and motion artifacts but also pose potential risks due to
repeated radiation exposure.
To overcome these challenges, there is a critical need for innovative methods capable of
generating high-fidelity 3D reconstructions from a reduced number of input images. Such
advancements would minimize radiation exposure, reduce patient burden, and address
logistical constraints in clinical workflows. Recent progress in deep learning has introduced
powerful algorithms that can extract meaningful information from limited data, achieving
impressive results in 3D scene reconstruction. Among these, NeRF [ 354] have emerged as
a groundbreaking technique in computer vision, capable of reconstructing intricate 3D
scenes from 2D images with remarkable precision.
Several recent studies have started exploring the use of NeRF in medical imaging,
adapting its methodology to accommodate these domain-specific requirements. These
advancements hint at the potential for NeRF to transform how 3D reconstructions are
approached in the medical field, but further research is required to address its limitations
and maximize its utility in clinical settings. The next subsection reviews some of these
pioneering studies, showcasing the progress and identifying areas for further investigation.
Building on the foundational NeRF framework, MedNeRF has been developed specifi-
cally to address the unique demands of medical imaging [ 355]. MedNeRF adapts NeRF’s
capabilities to handle medical images’ inherent complexities, including the need for high
anatomical accuracy, variable tissue densities, and clinical interpretability. Initial studies
have shown promising results, with reconstructing specific organs in modalities like CT
and MRI [356]. This potential positions MedNeRF as a transformative tool in the medical
imaging domain. Successful implementation of this methodology could lead to reduced
radiation exposure, shorter imaging sessions, and improved accessibility to high-quality 3D
imaging in clinical settings. Additionally, it may alleviate patient discomfort and minimize
the risk of motion artifacts, thereby enhancing image quality and diagnostic accuracy.
125

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
In this section, the capability of MedNeRF to reconstruct full-body scans from X-ray
images is explored. Using a dataset of five lung cancer (LC) patients collected from
Portuguese Oncology Institute of Porto (IPO-Porto), MedNeRF is trained on 72 digitally
reconstructed radiograph (DRR) per patient. These DRRs simulate X-ray images taken
from various angles around the body. The performance of MedNeRF is assessed using a
range of quantitative metrics, including mean absolute error (MAE), structural similarity
index measure (SSIM), peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) and fr´ echet inception distance
(FID).
7.1.1 Understanding Neural Radiance Fields
NeRF [354] is a deep learning-based model designed to reconstruct a 3D representation
of a scene from 2D images. NeRF represents a shift in how 3D reconstructions are
approached, moving away from traditional voxel-based or point-cloud methods. While
these earlier methods relied on explicit representations of geometry, NeRF leverages neural
networks to implicitly model scenes, providing smoother and more realistic reconstructions.
NeRF addresses the view synthesis problem in a novel way by optimizing the parameters
of a continuous 5D scene representation to minimize the error in rendering a set of captured
images. This algorithm employs a fully-connected deep network to represent the scene and
considers an input of a continuous 5D coordinate, and the output of the volume density
and the view-dependent emitted radiance at that location, as illustrated in Figure 7.1.
5D Input
Position+Direction
Output
Color +Density
NeRF
Figure 7.1: Illustration of the NeRF Inputs and Outputs for the X-rays Example.
New views are synthesized by querying 5D coordinates along camera rays and using
classical volume rendering techniques to project the output colors and densities into a 2D
image. Because volume rendering is inherently differentiable, NeRF requires only a set of
2D images with known camera poses as input to optimize its scene representation.
126

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
In the context of medical imaging, the concept of a camera cannot be directly mapped
to conventional definitions. While traditional photography involves capturing visual data
from external scenes, medical imaging techniques such as CT or X-ray imaging aim to
capture internal structures of the human body, characterizing them through pixel intensities
that represent tissue density, composition, or function. As a result, defining camera poses
for medical imaging requires rethinking how positional and angular information is captured.
Parameters such as scanner resolution, source-to-detector distance, rotation angles, and
slice thickness must be considered to adapt NeRF for medical imaging applications.
Although NeRF has shown remarkable success in computer vision tasks involving
typical photographs, its application to medical imaging presents unique challenges [ 357].
These challenges include adapting to the volumetric nature of medical data, handling
noise and artifacts common in medical images, and ensuring that the reconstructed
representations are clinically accurate and meaningful [ 356]. Moreover, medical imaging
data often comes with irregular acquisition patterns and higher noise levels, which differ
significantly from the structured input data typically used for NeRF.
7.1.2 Applications in Medical Imaging
Even though NeRF is a very recent development, this technique has already demon-
strated significant potential in creating 5D models of anatomical structures from various
imaging modalities. Its adaptability and efficacy have been particularly noted in ultrasound
(US), MRI and CT imaging, as well as in surgical applications.
For US imaging, several studies have explored variations of NeRF to improve traditional
image reconstruction algorithms. For instance, NeRF-based approaches have been applied
to spine imaging [358], enabling enhanced 3D reconstructions. Physics-enhanced NeRF
models that learn from overlapping US sweeps have also been proposed to improve
reconstruction quality and consistency [ 359]. Additionally, integrations of NeRFs with
generative adversarial network (GAN) have enabled image-to-image refinements, providing
improved fidelity in reconstructed 3D images [360].
NeRF has also been extended to applications in surgical imaging, where real-time and
accurate reconstructions are crucial. For example, neural radiance fields have been used to
reconstruct deformable tissues from binocular captures in robotic surgery, demonstrating
potential in dynamic, high-precision settings [ 361]. Similarly, NeRF has been employed
to render 3D abdominal scenes from laparoscopic surgical videos, offering enhanced
visualization for intraoperative guidance [362].
127

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
For MRI imaging, NeRF-based techniques have shown promise in addressing challenges
associated with undersampled and variable-quality data. Iddrisu et al. [ 363] developed
a method for 3D reconstruction of the brain from MRI scans using neural radiance
fields. Their approach integrates 3D convolutional neural networks to overcome challenges
such as variable slice thickness and incorporates multiple MRI modalities for robust
reconstruction. Jang et al. [ 364] proposed a novel solution for undersampled MRI
reconstruction using NeRF. By reformulating the sparse-view imaging problem into an
implicit neural representation task, their method enables high-dimensional reconstructions
from undersampled k-space data. The approach leverages single undersampled datasets
and applies multi-layer perceptrons to model MR physics-driven rendering relations,
offering advantages such as scan-specific adaptiveness and the ability to work with rare or
incomplete datasets.
Recent advancements in NeRFs have led to significant progress in reconstructing
CT projections and enhancing imaging quality, even from limited or single-view inputs.
Various adaptations of NeRF have been proposed to address the unique challenges of CT
imaging. MedNeRF [ 355] reconstructs high-fidelity 3D-aware CT projections from single
X-rays, effectively disentangling surface and internal anatomical structures. Experimental
results demonstrated high quantitative performance, with PSNR scores of 30.17 and
28.54 and SSIM values of 0.670 and 0.46, for knee and chest datasets, respectively. It
offers diagnostic quality while potentially reducing radiation exposure. However, manual
hyperparameter tuning for clarity and accuracy remains a challenge. UMedNeRF [ 365]
addresses this limitation by introducing uncertainty-aware multi-task learning, automating
the balance between resolution and precision. This approach outperformed prior methods,
achieving PSNR scores of 31.62 and 28.41 and SSIM values of 0.723 and 0.484, for knee
and chest datasets, respectively. These improvements underscore its capability to produce
high-quality CT projections without manual hyperparameter tuning, making it a robust
choice for clinical applications. ACNeRF [ 366] improves pose alignment and rendering
accuracy using latent codes and frequency-domain masks. It enhances the robustness
of NeRF for narrow-range projections, further refining CT reconstruction. CuNeRF
[367] introduces arbitrary-scale super-resolution, generating high-quality images from
low-resolution volumes. This zero-shot framework reduces artifacts and provides versatile
imaging across scales and viewpoints.
128

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
Despite the numerous advancements in NeRF-based CT imaging, MedNeRF remains a
foundational approach due to its versatility, robust results, and accessible implementation.
However, limitations such as its limited tested performance and lack of evaluations on
full-body imaging require further exploration. We aim to investigate MedNeRF in greater
depth, addressing these gaps while evaluating its broader applicability and limitations.
7.1.3 Experimental Design
This approach investigates the potential of MedNeRF [ 355] to reconstruct full-body
scans from single-view X-rays, with a focus on evaluating its performance across different
patients and identifying potential areas for improvement.
MedNeRF is a deep learning framework that adapts Generative Radiance Fields
(GRAF) to the medical domain, rendering CT projections from limited single-view X-ray
inputs. By synthesizing realistic images and capturing the data manifold, MedNeRF
provides a continuous representation of how attenuation and volumetric depth of anatomical
structures vary with the viewpoint, without requiring 3D supervision.
MedNeRF employs a generator and a discriminator, similar to typical GAN-based
frameworks. MedNeRF refines the discriminator to provide stronger, more comprehensive
signals and balances adversarial training with robust feedback. This enables the model to
produce high-quality volumetric representations, even with limited training data.
The dataset of CT scans is composed by five LC patients at the IPO-Porto. These
patients are carefully selected to ensure both consistency in pathology and variation in
positioning. Among the selected patients, two are positioned with their arms down, while
three have their arms up during imaging.
To avoid subjecting patients to additional imaging or radiation exposure, DRR are
generated from the CT volumes. The DRRs, which simulate X-ray images, are created
using the Plastimatch toolbox [368] and underwent preprocessing to ensure optimal quality
for the training process. For each patient, 72 X-ray projections are generated, uniformly
distributed around 360 ° of the body at 5 ° intervals, to form the training set. Furthermore,
288 additional X-ray images, generated at 1 ° intervals and excluding the training images,
are used as a testing set to evaluate the model’s ability to generalize to unseen viewpoints.
To ensure the reliability of the results, the training process is repeated five times for each
patient. This repeated training allowed for a thorough evaluation of the model’s perfor-
mance across different runs and provided insights into its consistency when reconstructing
volumetric representations from limited input data.
129

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
The performance of MedNeRF is assessed in two distinct phases to evaluate its
reconstruction capabilities. In the first phase, the model’s ability to reconstruct the 72
training images is analyzed to ensure it could accurately render the input DRRs. In the
second phase, the model is tested on the additional 288 DRRs generated at 1 ° intervals,
simulating unseen viewpoints. This allowed for a comprehensive evaluation of the model’s
generalization to new perspectives beyond the training set.
To quantify the quality of the reconstructions, MAE, SSIM, PSNR, and FID are used.
7.1.4 Evaluation of 3D X-Ray Reconstruction Performance
Figure 7.2 presents a visual comparison of the results for one of the best-performing
patients (Patient 1). The first row displays the original X-ray images from the training
set captured at angles of 0 °, 45°, and 90 °. In the second row, the corresponding X-ray
views generated by MedNeRF at the same angles (0 °, 45°, and 90 °) are shown.

130

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
The initial visual comparison demonstrates that MedNeRF effectively captures the
overall structure of the body, including the patient’s positioning and the alignment of
anatomical features such as the lungs. However, the generated views exhibit noticeable
blurriness and struggle to accurately depict essential structures like the bones. This
limitation may stem from the method used to generate the images or from inherent
constraints in the MedNeRF framework. Interestingly, the DRRs used for training do
not exhibit the same level of blurriness, suggesting that the issue arises during the
reconstruction process rather than due to the quality of the training data.
To gain deeper insights into the performance of MedNeRF, quantitative evaluations
are conducted for the training and testing DRRs. The average values of the evaluation
metrics are presented in Table 7.1.
Table 7.1: Average Evaluation Metrics for the Training and Testing DRRs.
Training DRRs Testing DRRs
Patient MAE ↓ PSNR ↑ SSIM ↑ FID ↑ MAE ↓ PSNR ↑ SSIM ↑ FID ↑
1 0.018 34.146 0.858 142.666 0.018 34.192 0.861 134.650

The results in Table 7.1 demonstrate that MedNeRF effectively reconstructs training
DRR views, achieving strong performance across various evaluation metrics. However,
variability in performance across patients suggests potential limitations in generalization.
Notably, patients 1 and 4 exhibit similar testing metrics to their training counterparts,
with patient 3 achieving the highest performance in terms of SSIM and PSNR. In contrast,
for patients 2 and 5, MedNeRF struggles to maintain the same level of performance,
indicating a decline in generalization.
A closer inspection of the reconstructed images reveals a specific issue in patient 4,
where a poorly defined volume creates an effect resembling image “rotation back” at certain
points. Interestingly, this issue is absent in patient 5, despite their highly similar data, and
is confined to the final 25 º of the rotation, suggesting that certain angles or anatomical
features may contribute to the reconstruction failure.
These findings underscore the need for further studies using larger datasets to better
understand the factors affecting model performance and to improve the robustness of
MedNeRF in diverse clinical scenarios.
131

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
7.1.5 Challenges and Limitations
Although the results of MedNeRF for full-body reconstruction from X-rays are promis-
ing, several limitations inherent to the algorithm that impact its utility and scalability
have been uncovered. Addressing these limitations will be critical for future improvements
and broader clinical applications. The main challenges identified are as follows:
• Computational Demands - MedNeRF is highly computationally intensive and time-
consuming, which limited the current experiment to only five patients. Training
the model requires significant computational resources, presenting a barrier to
scalability, particularly in large-scale datasets or clinical workflows. Optimizing
the computational efficiency of MedNeRF is a necessary step toward practical
deployment;
• Patient-Specific Nature - A key limitation of MedNeRF is its inability to generalize
across diverse patient anatomies. The model requires training on a per-patient
basis, restricting its use in population-wide studies or clinical settings where batch
processing is essential. This patient-specific nature limits its adaptability and
feasibility for broader clinical use. Enhancing the model’s generalization capabilities
is vital to address the variability in patient anatomy and ensure its applicability to
diverse populations;
• Data Requirements - While NeRF models are typically less data-intensive than
traditional deep learning methods, MedNeRF still requires a substantial number of
X-rays (72 views) to achieve effective 3D reconstruction. Moreover, some of this
data appears redundant, raising concerns about the model’s efficiency and potential
for data reduction. Future research should explore strategies to reduce the required
number of X-rays while preserving reconstruction quality;
• Sensitivity to Noise - The performance of MedNeRF is highly sensitive to noise in
the input data. Even minimal levels of noise in the X-rays significantly degrade the
reconstruction quality. This underscores the importance of robust preprocessing
pipelines and the need for model designs that are inherently more noise-tolerant;
• Challenges in 3D Reconstruction - Full 3D volume reconstruction remains a tech-
nically challenging task. During the exploration of this approach, several issues
have been encountered, including the inability to propagate the background during
reconstruction and the failure of COLMAP-based [ 369, 370] reconstruction attempts
132

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
to converge. These limitations highlight the inherent difficulties in using MedNeRF
to generate complete CT scans from X-rays. While MedNeRF excels at synthesizing
new views, its ability to reconstruct entire volumes is limited, requiring exploration
of alternative architectures or hybrid approaches.
Addressing these limitations is essential to advancing MedNeRF’s applicability in
medical imaging. Potential directions include optimizing computational efficiency to
reduce training times and resource demands, enhancing the model’s generalization to
accommodate diverse patient anatomies, and investigating strategies to reduce data
redundancy while maintaining reconstruction quality. Additionally, developing robust
methods for handling noise and exploring alternative architectures for full 3D volume
reconstruction could significantly enhance MedNeRF applications in medical imaging
applications.
7.2 From 2D Projections to 3D: Deep Learning for CT
Volume Reconstruction
The reconstruction of high-quality 3D CT volumes is a critical yet complex process
that underpins modern medical diagnosis and treatment planning. This process typically
involves combining multiple 2D X-ray scans to create detailed 3D representations of
anatomical structures. While essential for accurate diagnostics, traditional approaches
pose significant challenges, including prolonged acquisition times, high costs, and increased
patient exposure to radiation. While widely adopted in commercial systems, these
techniques are limited by their susceptibility to noise, motion artifacts, and distortions
caused by metal implants. These challenges underscore the urgent need for innovative
methods that can efficiently reconstruct 3D CT volumes while minimizing the number of
input X-rays.
Recent advancements in machine learning have shown great promise in addressing
these challenges. Powerful models, particularly those utilizing deep learning techniques,
now offer the potential to generate high-resolution 3D CT volumes from a limited set of
2D X-rays. Such methods not only reduce radiation exposure for patients but also improve
the cost-efficiency and accessibility of imaging in clinical settings. However, achieving
clinically viable reconstructions requires a thorough evaluation of these methods to identify
their strengths, limitations, and optimal configurations.
133

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
Convolutional neural network (CNN) have been extensively explored for their ability to
enhance feature representation in CT reconstruction tasks. Ratul et al. [ 371] introduced a
class-conditioned convolutional network that leverages semantic segmentation and depth-
aware connections to improve reconstruction accuracy. Their approach incorporated
an Adaptive Feature Fusion module to address registration challenges between input
views, achieving significant performance improvements. For biplanar X-rays, their method
achieved PSNR = 28.18 and SSIM = 0.752, outperforming single-array setups (PSNR =
24.73, SSIM = 0.597). Similarly, Kyung et al. [ 372] proposed a framework incorporating
a perspective projection scheme that combines local and global features to infer 3D
coordinates. This approach demonstrated competitive results for biplanar X-rays, achieving
PSNR = 27.45 and SSIM = 0.732, while also offering computational efficiency and fast
inference times, making it suitable for clinical applications.
Generative adversarial network (GAN) have also shown significant potential for CT
reconstruction. Ying et al. [ 373] developed X2CT-GAN, which employs a feature fusion
mechanism to integrate orthogonal X-rays for dimensional transitions from 2D to 3D.
Using two views, their method achieved PSNR = 27.29 and SSIM = 0.721, compared to
single-view inputs (PSNR = 23.12, SSIM = 0.587). Building on this foundation, Cafaro
et al. [ 374] introduced a GAN-based framework leveraging deep structure priors for
biplanar X-rays. Their results demonstrated a PSNR of 26.19 and SSIM of 0.656 for
biplanar inputs, but single-view performance remained limited (PSNR = 22.30, SSIM =
0.525), emphasizing the importance of multi-view setups. Wang et al. [ 375] incorporated
Transformers into a GAN architecture, introducing dynamic attention modules to improve
contextual associations. This approach achieved PSNR = 27.46 and SSIM = 0.726
for biplanar setups, outperforming single-view results (PSNR = 23.21, SSIM = 0.591).
Similarly, Cheng et al. [ 376] developed SdCT-GAN, a self-driven GAN incorporating a
Sobel Gradient Guider to enhance fine details. With two-view inputs, SdCT-GAN achieved
PSNR = 31.937 and SSIM = 0.708, significantly outperforming single-view reconstructions
(PSNR = 27.379, SSIM = 0.566). Additionally, Wang et al. [ 377] proposed a coarse-to-fine
cross-view fusion method that integrates view attention alignment and fine-distillation sub-
modules to extract complementary information from orthogonal X-rays. Their approach
achieved superior results, with a PSNR of 29.7508 and SSIM of 0.8113, setting a benchmark
for accuracy in multi-view setups.
Recently, diffusion models have been explored for CT reconstruction. Qiao et al. [ 378]
introduced a probabilistic diffusion model incorporating a projection loss function to
improve structural integrity. Their approach achieved PSNR = 27.8391 and SSIM = 0.83,
134

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
demonstrating the potential for clinical applications. Similarly, Liu et al. [ 379] developed
DiffuX2CT, which utilizes a tri-plane decoupling generator for structure-controllable 3D
reconstructions. This method achieved PSNR = 26.35 and SSIM = 0.6872, emphasizing
texture fidelity and structural coherence.
Although these studies have made significant strides, several challenges remain. Most
existing methods focus on reconstructing specific anatomical regions, such as the chest or
spine, rather than full-body CT volumes. Furthermore, the majority of studies rely on single
or biplanar X-rays, with limited exploration of incorporating additional views to enhance
reconstruction quality. Therefore, the optimal number of X-rays required to achieve
high-quality reconstructions while minimizing patient burden should be investigated.
This section focuses on exploring the capabilities of convolutional neural network
(CNN), specifically Convolutional Autoencoders, for reconstructing high-quality 3D CT
volumes from digitally reconstructed radiograph (DRR). More specifically, it investigates
the use of Convolutional Autoencoders and their variations for efficiently reconstructing
3D CT volumes while minimizing the number of input X-rays. A private dataset of 363
LC patients was used with 72 DRR. Three architectures were explored: 2D-to-3D, 3D
CNN, and 3D CNN with Attention. The models were evaluated on metrics including
peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR), structural similarity index measure (SSIM), and mean
absolute error (MAE). Beyond evaluating architectural components, we aim to identify
the minimum number of X-rays required to maximize reconstruction fidelity, addressing a
critical question in efficient medical imaging.
7.2.1 Proposed Methodology
This study explores the use of Convolutional Autoencoders and their variations for
reconstructing high-quality 3D CT volumes from X-rays. The methodology was designed
to systematically evaluate the performance of these models under different experimental
configurations, focusing on the impact of input configurations, channel setups, and
architectural variations on reconstruction quality.
The dataset used in this study comprises CT images from 363 LC patients collected
from IPO-Porto. To ensure consistency and reduce complexity, the dataset was limited to
patients with tumors isolated in the lungs, excluding those with metastases. This selection
allowed the reconstruction task to focus on a well-defined anatomical region.
135

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
DRR were generated from the CT volumes using the Plastimatch toolbox [ 368]. DRRs
simulate X-ray images and were preprocessed to ensure optimal quality for training.
The preprocessing steps included normalization, resizing to fixed dimensions, and noise
reduction to mimic real-world clinical conditions. For each patient, 72 X-ray projections
were created, evenly distributed around 360 ° at 5° intervals, forming the experimental
dataset.
The experimental setup was designed to evaluate the effects of varying the number
and configurations of input X-rays on reconstruction performance, as illustrated in Figure
7.4. Single-channel and multi-channel configurations were explored: Single-channel setup -
Only the X-ray at 0 ° was used; and Multi-channel setups - Configurations included 2, 3, 9,
18, 36, and 72 X-rays, spaced at intervals of 90 °, 45°, 40°, 20°, 10°, and 5 °, respectively.
For example, the three-channel setup used X-rays at 0°, 45°, and 90°. These configurations
were tested to understand how the number of input views and their spatial distribution
affected reconstruction accuracy and fidelity.

2D 
Encoder

Figure 7.3: Illustration of the Inputs and Outputs of the Network.
Three primary Convolutional Autoencoder architectures were developed and evaluated:
• 2D-to-3D Model: This architecture uses a 2D encoder to extract features from the
input X-rays, followed by a linear bottleneck to compress the feature representation.
The decoder is a 3D structure designed to reconstruct the full CT volume from the
latent space;
136

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
• 3D CNN Model: This architecture employs 3D convolutions throughout the en-
coder, bottleneck, and decoder. By directly processing spatial relationships across
all dimensions, this model aims to capture detailed 3D structural features more
effectively;
• 3D CNN with Attention: This enhanced architecture integrates 3D convolutions
with batch normalization for stability and attention layers to focus on critical regions
in the data. The attention mechanism emphasizes relevant features, improving the
model’s ability to reconstruct fine details.
All models were trained using the mean squared error loss function, which mini-
mizes pixel-wise reconstruction errors. Training was conducted for 200 epochs using the
Adam optimizer with a learning rate of 10−4. To ensure robustness, each experimental
configuration was repeated 30 times, allowing for statistical validation of the results.
To evaluate the quality of the reconstructed CT volumes, we used several quantitative
metrics. peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) and structural similarity index measure
(SSIM) were employed to assess reconstruction fidelity and structural similarity to the
ground truth, while mean absolute error (MAE) measured the overall pixel-wise differences
between the reconstructed and reference volumes. These metrics provided a comprehensive
evaluation of the autoencoder’s performance, facilitating detailed comparisons across
different architectures and with existing state-of-the-art methods. This framework enabled
a thorough analysis of the models’ ability to achieve an optimal balance between efficiency,
accuracy, and structural fidelity in reconstructing high-quality 3D CT volumes from a
limited number of input X-rays.
7.2.2 Performance Assessment of 3D CT Reconstruction
The results obtained for the three architectures explored in this study are presented
in Table 7.2. These metrics offer a comprehensive assessment of reconstruction quality
across various input configurations.
The 3D CNN Attention model consistently outperformed the other architectures across
all input configurations. For single-view X-rays, it achieved a PSNR of 30.94 and an
SSIM of 0.5653, while for biplanar views, these metrics improved to 32.07 and 0.6892,
respectively. These results align with state-of-the-art performance in the field and validate
the effectiveness of attention mechanisms in enhancing reconstruction quality.
137

Interestingly, the configuration using 18 X-rays produced the best overall performance,
with a PSNR of 31.95 and an SSIM of 0.6925. This finding is noteworthy, as it suggests
diminishing returns in reconstruction quality with an increasing number of input X-rays
beyond 18. Although higher numbers of X-rays theoretically provide more information, the
results indicate that redundancy and noise in the input data limit further improvements
in performance.
Figures 7.3 and 7.4 illustrate the reconstructed CT images generated by the 3D CNN
Attention model for various input configurations. Figure 7.3 presents coronal views, while
Figure 7.4 shows axial views of the same patient.
The visual results indicate that while fewer input X-rays (e.g., 1 or 3) allow the model
to capture the overall body shape, they fail to preserve finer anatomical details such as
lung textures, bone structures, and organ delineations. As the number of input X-rays
increases, the reconstructions improve, particularly in structural clarity. However, even
with 72 X-rays, the reconstructed images fall short of clinical quality, suggesting that
limitations in the input data, including noise and insufficient textural detail, continue to
restrict reconstruction fidelity.
These results highlight the promise of the 3D CNN Attention model for CT recon-
struction tasks but also underscore the importance of addressing key limitations in input
quality and data redundancy to achieve clinically viable outcomes.

7.2.3 Challenges and Future Perspectives
The results of this study demonstrate the potential of the 3D CNN Attention model in
reconstructing 3D CT volumes from X-rays. While the model consistently outperformed
other architectures, the findings also reveal several limitations that must be addressed
to achieve clinically viable outcomes. A deeper analysis of these results provides insights
into the challenges and opportunities for further improving CT reconstruction techniques.
A key factor influencing reconstruction performance is the resolution and quality of
the input X-rays. Initial experiments using 64x64 pixel projections yielded suboptimal
results, primarily due to insufficient detail. Increasing the resolution to 128x128 pixels
significantly enhanced the model’s ability to capture structural features, highlighting the
critical role of high-resolution data in achieving accurate reconstructions. Despite these
improvements, the X-rays remained limited in their ability to convey essential anatomical
details, such as lung textures, bone structures, and organ delineations. High levels of
noise in the input data further compounded these issues, reducing the model’s capacity to
reconstruct fine-grained details even with preprocessing aimed at enhancing image quality.
139

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction

Another significant challenge lies from the inherent limitations of the X-ray data
used. The reconstruction process is highly dependent on the richness and quality of the
input images; however, the X-rays struggled to capture intricate anatomical features
with sufficient detail. Consequently, the reconstructed volumes were largely confined
to broader structural elements, such as body and lung contours, while lacking the finer
details essential for accurate clinical interpretation. This constraint highlights the need for
higher-fidelity input data, particularly in anatomically complex regions crucial for medical
diagnosis. Enhancing the quality of input imaging — whether through higher-resolution
scans, multi-modal integration, or advanced preprocessing techniques — could significantly
improve reconstruction accuracy and facilitate more precise medical assessments.
The results also indicate diminishing returns in reconstruction quality when increasing
the number of input X-rays beyond 18. Although higher numbers of X-rays theoretically
provide more information, the added data often included redundancy and noise, which
limited their effectiveness in enhancing the final output. This finding suggests that the
reconstruction process could benefit from optimized input selection, focusing on fewer,
higher-quality X-rays to reduce redundancy while maintaining performance.
140

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
Architectural design played a pivotal role in determining reconstruction accuracy.
Extensive experimentation with alternative configurations, including skip connections
and Vision Transformers, did not yield significant improvements over the 3D CNN
Attention model. Attention mechanisms proved particularly effective, allowing the model
to emphasize relevant features and enhance overall reconstruction fidelity. However, these
results suggest that further exploration of advanced architectures, such as diffusion models
or hybrid approaches that integrate attention mechanisms with other frameworks, could
unlock new possibilities for improving reconstruction quality.
Despite the progress demonstrated in this study, significant challenges remain. The
inability of the model to reconstruct fine anatomical details limits its clinical applicability.
Additionally, the computational demands associated with high-resolution inputs and
complex architectures present scalability concerns. Addressing these limitations will
require advancements in input data acquisition, preprocessing techniques, and model
optimization to balance performance and computational efficiency.
In summary, the findings highlight both the potential and the challenges of leveraging
Convolutional Autoencoders for CT reconstruction from X-rays. While the 3D CNN Atten-
tion model represents a promising step forward, achieving clinically viable reconstructions
will require a concerted effort to improve input data quality, optimize architectures, and
reduce redundancy in the reconstruction process. These insights provide a foundation for
future research aimed at advancing machine learning-driven solutions in medical imaging.
7.3 Conclusion
After analyzing inpainting techniques across a broad range of positron emission
tomography/computer tomography (PET/CT) applications, this chapter explored the
potential of deep learning-based imputation techniques for 3D medical imaging, extending
beyond traditional methods for filling missing image regions. The focus shifted toward
reconstructing complete 3D CT volumes from X-ray images, leveraging cutting-edge deep
learning models for volumetric reconstruction.
The first approach evaluated the capability of MedNeRF to reconstruct full-body
3D scans from X-ray images. The results demonstrated that MedNeRF can generate
high-fidelity full-body 3D X-ray models, achieving impressive average performance metrics:
PSNR = 33.2738, SSIM = 0.7789, MAE = 0.0353, and FID = 145.0755. These values
underscore MedNeRF’s potential for practical applications in clinical settings, where
both accuracy and efficiency are paramount. However, despite its promising performance,
141

Chapter 7. Reconstructing 3D CT Volumes: Exploring Deep Learning-Based
Reconstruction
several challenges remain. The model’s substantial computational demands, its reliance on
patient-specific data, high data requirements, sensitivity to noise, and inherent limitations
in achieving robust 3D reconstructions present obstacles to its widespread adoption.
Addressing these challenges through future research will be critical to unlocking the full
potential of NeRF in advancing medical imaging technology.
The second approach explored the use of Convolutional Autoencoders, particularly
the 3D CNN Attention model, for reconstructing high-quality 3D CT volumes from
X-ray inputs. The results demonstrated the effectiveness of attention mechanisms in
enhancing reconstruction quality, with the 3D CNN Attention model outperforming
alternative architectures. It achieved PSNR = 32.07 and SSIM = 0.6892 for biplanar inputs,
aligning with state-of-the-art performance. These findings emphasize the importance of
attention-based architectures in improving reconstruction fidelity. However, the study also
highlighted key limitations, including constraints in input resolution, sensitivity to noise,
and scalability issues in the network architecture, which impact the clinical applicability
of the reconstructed images. Future research should focus on enhancing input quality,
mitigating redundancy, and exploring advanced architectures to further improve the fidelity
and efficiency of 3D reconstructions.
Overall, this study represents a significant step toward reducing dependence on con-
ventional CT imaging processes and pushing the boundaries of what is achievable in
3D medical imaging reconstruction. By addressing existing challenges and further refin-
ing these models, this research lays the groundwork for more efficient, accessible, and
patient-friendly diagnostic solutions. The integration of deep learning-based volumetric
reconstruction into clinical workflows has the potential to revolutionize diagnostic imaging,
reducing radiation exposure, lowering costs, and expanding access to high-quality imaging
technologies. Future advancements in this field could pave the way for real-time, AI-driven
reconstruction techniques, ultimately transforming the landscape of medical imaging and
personalized healthcare.
142

Part IV
Final Remarks
143

Chapter 8
Conclusions
This chapter provides a comprehensive synthesis of the key findings from the research
conducted in this thesis, aligning them with the overarching research task and discussing
their contributions to advancing imputation methodologies. In addition to summarizing
the conclusions drawn from the individual chapters, this section extends the discussion
to broader implications for the field, addressing the limitations encountered, the gaps
identified, and potential future directions for continued exploration and innovation in
medical imaging imputation.
8.1 Panorama in Medical Imaging Imputation
One of the primary tasks of this thesis was to review and evaluate the state-of-the-art
imputation methodologies relevant to mammography — the gold standard for breast cancer
(BC) detection — as well as other medical imaging modalities, assessing their strengths,
limitations, and practical applicability. The literature review, detailed in Chapters 2 and
5, systematically examines existing imputation techniques and their suitability across
different imaging modalities. This comprehensive analysis not only highlights the current
state of the field but also identifies critical gaps and opportunities for improvement,
shaping the foundation for the novel approaches proposed in this thesis.
8.1.1 Image Quality Enhancement in Mammography Imaging
The initial phase of this research concentrated on mammography imaging, specifically
analyzing existing methodologies for image enhancement and imputation. The literature
suggests that mammography image enhancement is predominantly achieved through
144

Chapter 8. Conclusions
contrast enhancement or denoising techniques, with methods specifically tailored to the
unique characteristics of mammographic images.
Among these approaches, contrast enhancement techniques were found to be effec-
tive due to their simplicity, low computational complexity, and ease of implementation.
Similarly, denoising techniques, particularly filtering-based methods, provided benefits as
they did not require large training datasets. However, the use of enhancement techniques
introduces new challenges:
• Certain methods, such as unsharp masking and filtering, may inadvertently generate
artifacts that obscure critical diagnostic information;
• Calcifications, which are crucial indicators of malignancies, may be mistakenly
removed during denoising processes because they often exhibit similar characteristics
to noise, such as small size, high-frequency components, and low contrast against the
surrounding tissue. As a result, standard denoising algorithms may fail to distinguish
them from unwanted artifacts, potentially compromising diagnostic accuracy;
• The margins of a tumor, which are crucial for determining the malignancy type of
BC, can be blurred or lost when aggressive denoising techniques are applied. This
occurs because these techniques may inadvertently remove fine structural details,
leading to the loss of important textural and morphological features essential for
accurate diagnosis.
These findings highlight the need for a careful evaluation of enhancement techniques
to ensure that they do not compromise clinically significant features. While deep learning-
based approaches, such as denoising autoencoders and convolutional neural network (CNN),
have demonstrated superior performance in mammography image processing, they also
present some issues. One of the primary bottlenecks is their reliance on large, high-quality
datasets, which remain scarce in medical imaging research. The limited availability of
robust open-source datasets continues to hinder progress, making standardization and
dataset accessibility critical areas for future work.
Another major gap identified in the literature is the lack of focus on inpainting tech-
niques for mammography imaging [26]. While many studies explore contrast enhancement
and denoising, very few have investigated methodologies dedicated to reconstructing
missing regions within mammographic images. This presents an important avenue for
future research, particularly in the development of imputation models that prioritize both
image realism and diagnostic accuracy. Addressing this gap is essential for improving data
completeness, lesion detectability, and overall image integrity in mammography.
145

Chapter 8. Conclusions
8.1.2 Deep Learning and Image Inpainting in Medical Imaging
Beyond mammography, this thesis explored the broader application of image imputation
across different medical imaging modalities, particularly focusing on deep learning-based
image inpainting. This phase of the literature review emphasized artifact removal and
multi-task applications, evaluating the strengths and limitations of various architectures.
Image inpainting was found to be a versatile and impactful technique in medical
imaging, with architectures such as generative adversarial network (GAN) and U-Nets
demonstrating strong potential. GANs excelled at generating realistic reconstructions,
while U-Nets proved highly effective in localizing and inpainting missing regions, preserving
structural coherence. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) and computer tomography (CT)
emerged as the most widely studied modalities, highlighting their critical role in advancing
medical imaging research [181, 182, 275, 277].
While several artifacts have been successfully addressed using inpainting techniques,
including metal artifacts, cropped images, markers, and annotations, some significant
and frequent artifacts remain underexplored. These include motion artifacts, which can
severely degrade image quality in dynamic scans; beam hardening artifacts, which distort
CT images due to variations in X-ray absorption; and scatter and streak artifacts, which
commonly occur in high-density tissue regions. Image inpainting holds significant potential
for improving these types of artifacts, yet further research is required to fully harness its
capabilities.
Additionally, while multi-task applications involving image reconstruction and synthesis
have shown promise, they remain a small subset of the existing literature. Given the
increasing clinical demand for AI-driven reconstruction methods, further exploration is
needed to develop more sophisticated and adaptable techniques that can handle a variety
of medical imaging challenges.
Another major gap identified in the field is the lack of techniques tailored to 3D and
4D medical imaging inpainting. Most research has focused on 2D inpainting, leaving a
considerable void to reconstruct volumetric and temporal medical imaging data.
Lastly, one of the most critical challenges identified is the lack of validation for the
clinical relevance of imputed images. While many studies focus on improving the visual
realism and structural integrity of reconstructions, very few evaluate whether the imputed
images retain clinically important diagnostic features. There is a notable absence of
validation using interpretability and explainability techniques. Without these methods, it
remains unclear whether imputation techniques preserve the key features that radiologists
rely on for diagnosis.
146

Chapter 8. Conclusions
8.2 Findings in Mammography Imputation
The next key task of this research was to investigate and evaluate the potential
applications of imputation in mammography to enhance image quality and improve BC
diagnosis. Beyond the literature review, this thesis conducted extensive experimental
studies to assess state-of-the-art image imputation algorithms and their applicability to
medical imaging. These experiments, detailed in Chapters 3 and 4, focused on evaluating
different imputation methodologies, including pixel-based imputation and image-to-image
translation techniques. The findings provide critical insights into the strengths and
limitations of current imputation approaches, highlighting areas for future research and
potential clinical integration.
8.2.1 Pixel-Based Imputation in Mammography
The first set of experiments explored pixel-based imputation techniques for mammog-
raphy, where a variational autoencoder (VAE) extension was developed and evaluated.
The results demonstrated that this model outperformed standard VAEs and generative
adversarial imputation network (GAIN), showcasing its strong potential for pixel-based
imputation in medical imaging.
However, despite significant improvements in reconstructed image quality, a major
limitation of this approach is the lack of emphasis on preserving anatomical information and
clinically relevant textures within the image. Although the model effectively reconstructed
missing regions, ensuring that the inpainted areas maintain diagnostically significant
features, such as lesion margins, microcalcifications, and tissue structures, remains a
challenge.
A subsequent approach assessed the impact of various imputation algorithms on lesion
preservation and classification performance. One key limitation identified in the literature
is the lack of comprehensive performance assessments of imputation algorithms when
reconstructing missing pixels in different morphological structures in medical imaging. In
this approach, several imputation techniques were evaluated, with k-nearest neighbour
(KNN),multivariate imputation by chained equation (MICE), deep image prior (DIP)
emerging as the most effective methods [ 20]. Among them, DIP demonstrated the
highest performance in preserving lesion morphology, particularly at high missing rates,
highlighting its potential for morphology-aware imputation.
147

Chapter 8. Conclusions
Interestingly, the results indicated no significant differences in the imputation outcomes
across the eight different lesion morphologies present in the dataset. However, this study
focused exclusively on mass lesions, excluding calcifications due to the lack of region of
interest (ROI) annotations and the absence of specific labeling for these images.
This omission is notable because calcifications play a critical role in BC diagnosis. A
key concern is whether imputation methods may unintentionally eliminate calcifications,
thereby affecting diagnostic accuracy. However, further analysis revealed that even
after reconstructing 80% of missing pixels, the classification model remained capable of
distinguishing between calcifications and masses, while maintaining robust performance in
differentiating malignant and benign lesions. This suggests that calcifications were not
systematically eliminated during the inpainting process.
8.2.2 Image-to-Image Translation for Mammography Reconstruction
Another major focus was evaluating image-to-image translation techniques for recon-
structing mammography projections. A comprehensive study was conducted to compare
several state-of-the-art image-to-image translation models, assessing their ability to recon-
struct one mammographic view from another.
Among the tested models, ResViT demonstrated the highest performance, particularly
in reconstructing mediolateral oblique (MLO) from craniocaudal (CC) views. This finding
constitutes a crucial point because reconstructing mammographic projections from a
single view could potentially reduce patient discomfort and radiation exposure, offering a
compelling clinical advantage. However, despite the promising results, the generated images
still exhibited noticeable pixelation and texture inconsistencies, limiting their immediate
applicability in real-world clinical settings. These artifacts could introduce ambiguity in
lesion interpretation, necessitating further refinement before clinical deployment.
8.3 Findings in CT Imputation
Following the investigation of imputation techniques in mammography, this research
expanded to CT imaging, where imputation methods were applied to CT slices to evaluate
their effectiveness in reconstructing missing regions and preserving clinically relevant
structures. This phase aimed to assess the ability of imputation models to enhance image
completeness, structural fidelity, and diagnostic accuracy, particularly in the context of
tumor lesion reconstruction.
148

Chapter 8. Conclusions
Additionally, the study extended the scope of imputation techniques to positron
emission tomography/computer tomography (PET/CT) imaging, exploring their role in
improving data completeness and diagnostic accuracy in 3D medical imaging. These
investigations are detailed in Chapters 6 and 7, where both 2D and 3D reconstruction
techniques were explored to determine the most effective approaches for medical imaging
applications.
8.3.1 Enhanced Structural and Morphological Imputation with ESMII
in 2D CT Images
Initially, benchmark imputation techniques, as defined in Chapter 2, were evaluated to
establish a baseline performance within a controlled experimental environment. This step
provided valuable insights into the impact of different imputation methods when applied
to the same dataset, allowing for direct comparisons of their effectiveness in reconstructing
structural and morphological details.
Among the models tested, edge and structure information for medical image inpainting
(ESMII) emerged as the most effective technique, particularly for structural and textural
reconstruction. It demonstrated superior performance in preserving anatomical details and
restoring missing regions with high fidelity. Additionally, EC exhibited strong capabilities
in reconstructing tumor masses, maintaining lesion morphology and contrast, which are
crucial for diagnostic accuracy.
However, other imputation methods tested did not perform as effectively, as they
exhibited blurring effects and failed to retain fine-grained textural details essential for
clinical evaluation. These models struggled to reconstruct complex anatomical features,
leading to loss of important diagnostic information.
Despite the promising results obtained with ESMII and EC, tumor lesion reconstruction
in 2D CT images remained a major challenge. The primary limitation was that 2D slices
do not provide sufficient contextual information to accurately reconstruct missing regions,
particularly for volumetric structures and complex tissue formations. This limitation
highlighted the need to expand the research to 3D reconstruction techniques, where spatial
information from adjacent slices could be leveraged to improve imputation accuracy.
Given these findings, the next phase of this research focused on 3D reconstruction
techniques, aiming to enhance both structural integrity and diagnostic quality in medical
imaging.
149

Chapter 8. Conclusions
8.3.2 3D Reconstruction in CT Imaging
Recognizing the limitations of 2D imputation, this thesis explored advanced 3D
reconstruction methods to enhance CT imaging quality and improve volumetric medical
imaging.
In the first approach, this study investigated neural radiance fields (NeRF), a novel deep
learning technique that models continuous 3D scenes from 2D input images. Specifically,
medical neural radiance fields (MedNeRF), a NeRF variant adapted for medical imaging,
was evaluated for its ability to reconstruct 3D volumes from X-rays.
MedNeRF demonstrated strong 3D reconstruction capabilities, achieving a PSNR
of 33.27 and a SSIM of 0.78. These results indicate that NeRF-based models have the
potential to generate highly detailed 3D representations, preserving anatomical structures
with minimal information loss. However, despite these promising results, the application
of NeRF in medical imaging remains highly challenging, primarily due to:
• The need to adapt medical images to the neural radiance format, requiring pre-
processing techniques that translate traditional CT scans into a format suitable for
NeRF-based reconstruction;
• Difficulties in calibrating clinical features, as current NeRF implementations lack
direct integration with radiological metrics used in clinical diagnostics;
• Difficulties in calibrating clinical features, as current NeRF implementations lack
direct integration with radiological metrics used in clinical diagnostics.
• High computational costs, making real-time or large-scale clinical deployment chal-
lenging.
The second approach explored 3D CNN-based models for full CT volume reconstruction
from X-ray projections. This method aimed to generate high-resolution 3D representations
by leveraging deep learning architectures trained on volumetric datasets.
3D CNN Attention models demonstrated competitive performance, effectively re-
constructing missing slices while preserving anatomical structures. However, challenges
persisted in handling resolution variations and noise levels in the input data, leading
to occasional artifacts and inconsistencies in the reconstructed volumes. The methods
developed in this study were unable to achieve the required textural quality and or-
gan delineation necessary for further medical diagnosis, indicating the need for further
refinement.
150

Chapter 8. Conclusions
Although both NeRF-based and CNN-based approaches showed promising results,
several limitations remain:
• Texture and Detail Preservation - The reconstructed images lacked fine-grained
textures, which are crucial for differentiating healthy and pathological tissues;
• Clinical Feature Calibration - Current 3D reconstruction models are not optimized
for clinical interpretation, requiring further validation from radiologists to ensure
the imputed data aligns with diagnostic standards;
• Computational Complexity - 3D reconstruction methods require high computational
power, limiting their applicability in real-time clinical settings.
8.4 Future Work
This thesis has laid the foundation for understanding state-of-the-art techniques in
medical imaging imputation. Future work should prioritize addressing the limitations
identified in this research while exploring novel directions to enhance the quality, inter-
pretability, and clinical applicability of these algorithms. Four key research areas are
outlined below:
• Exploration of 3D models for Medical Imaging - While this thesis highlighted the
potential of MedNeRF and 3D CNN Attention models, future efforts should focus on
advancing 3D reconstruction techniques capable of generating anatomically accurate
and high-resolution outputs. Refining these models to better handle complex
structures and integrating multi-modal data—such as combining X-ray, CT, and
MRI data—could significantly improve reconstruction fidelity;
• Testing and Evaluating Algorithms on Real-World Artifacts - The research developed
until this moment has been primarily based on idealized or controlled datasets, which
do not fully represent the complexities of clinical environments. Future studies should
emphasize the evaluation of algorithms on datasets with authentic artifacts, including
noise, motion blur, metal implants, and imaging inconsistencies. Investigating how
these algorithms perform under such conditions will provide crucial insights into
their robustness and adaptability. This will also help identify potential limitations
and lead to more effective solutions tailored to real-world clinical challenges;
151

Chapter 8. Conclusions
• Development of Interpretable Models for Image Imputation - Current methodologies
often act as black boxes, making it difficult for clinicians to trust or understand why
specific reconstructions or imputations are performed. Future work should focus
on developing interpretable models that provide transparency in the reconstruction
process. These models should include mechanisms for explaining decisions, such
as visualizing how missing pixels are reconstructed or identifying the algorithm’s
reasoning for specific outputs. By enhancing the interpretability of these models,
stronger collaboration between clinicians and machine learning researchers can be
achieved, ultimately improving patient care and accelerating the integration of AI
solutions into clinical practice;
• Expanding Research into Transformer-Based and Diffusion-Based Models - Recent
advancements in transformer-based and diffusion-based models have demonstrated
exceptional potential in other fields of machine learning, such as image generation.
These architectures could be adapted to medical imaging to improve reconstruction
quality. Transformer models, with their ability to capture long-range dependencies
and contextual relationships, can aid in reconstructing intricate anatomical details.
Diffusion models, known for their ability to denoise and produce high-fidelity images,
may address current challenges related to noise and resolution. Future research
should explore the adaptation and integration of these advanced techniques into
existing frameworks, potentially leading to hybrid architectures that combine the
strengths of multiple approaches.
By focusing on these four research areas, future work can address the limitations
identified in this thesis and push the boundaries of what is currently achievable in medical
imaging. These directions have the potential to establish more robust, interpretable,
and clinically relevant solutions, ultimately contributing to improved diagnostic accuracy,
reduced patient burden, and enhanced medical outcomes.
